Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Title and Author••
	• The book is titled "The Marrow of Sacred Divinity" or "Marrow of Theology"
	• It was written by William Ames, a doctor and professor of divinity
• ••Publication Details••
	• Published in 1639 in London
	• Printed by Edward Griffin for Henry Overton in Popet-Head alley
	• Translated from Latin for those who don't speak other languages
• ••Content and Structure••
	• The book is a collection of tables and chapters on theology
	• It includes tables representing the substance and heads of the book
	• There is also a table opening up hard words contained in the book
• ••Purpose and Usefulness••
	• The book is useful for this season (1639)
	• It is meant to be a helpful resource for understanding theology
	• It is based on the Holy Scriptures and the interpreters of it
• ••Translation and Editing••
	• The book was translated from Latin into English
	• The translator made changes to modernize the language and simplify syntax
	• The original text had many typographical errors and inconsistencies
• ••Additional Features••
	• The book includes tables and definitions
	• The translator added footnotes and annotations to help clarify the text
	• The book includes references to Scripture and other sources
• ••Influence and Legacy••
	• The book was influential in the American colonies
	• Religious leaders in the colonies referred to Dr. Ames more than any other writer
	• The book's ideas were later employed by John Owen and Jonathan Edwards
• ••Changes and Corrections••
	• The translator made changes to modernize the language and simplify syntax
	• The original text had many typographical errors and inconsistencies
	• The translator corrected and standardized Scripture citations
• ••Format and Style••
	• The book is written in a formal and technical style
	• The translator avoided paraphrasing and allowed the original text to speak for itself
	• The book includes underlined portions and annotations to help clarify the text
• ••Availability and Accessibility••
	• The book is available online through the archive.org website
	• The translator made changes to make the book more accessible to modern readers
	• The book is not a light read, but it is meant to be edifying and helpful.

• ••Book Structure••
	• The book is divided into two parts: Sacred Theology and Observance
	• Sacred Theology is further divided into 41 chapters
• ••Sacred Theology Chapters••
	• Chapters 1-21 cover the death, exaltation, and application of Christ
	• Chapters 22-30 cover predestination, calling, justification, adoption, sanctification, and glorification
	• Chapters 31-33 cover the church, its ministers, and the holy scripture
	• Chapters 34-41 cover the sacraments, ecclesiastical discipline, and the administration of the covenant
• ••Specific Topics••
	• Chapter 22: Death of Christ
	• Chapter 23: Exaltation of Christ
	• Chapter 24: Application of Christ
	• Chapter 25: Predestination
	• Chapter 26: Calling
	• Chapter 27: Justification
	• Chapter 28: Adoption
	• Chapter 29: Sanctification
	• Chapter 30: Glorification
• ••Observance Chapters••
	• Chapters 1-8 cover observance in general, virtue, good works, religion, faith, hope, charity, and hearing the word
	• These chapters are part of the second book of Sacred Theology

• ••Glossary of Terms••
	• Genuine distribution: proper division
	• Metonymy: a figure of speech where the cause is put for the effect
• ••Philosophical Concepts••
	• Inaccessible: something that cannot be gone into
	• Essence: the beginning or fundamental nature of something
	• Subsistence: the manner of being or existence
• ••Abstract and Concrete••
	• Abstract: a substantive concept, such as whiteness
	• Concrete: an adjective, such as white
• ••Relationships and Analogies••
	• Analogically: by way of resemblance
	• Relatives: respective or related
• ••God and Creation••
	• Efficiency of God: His working power
	• Procession: issuing or emanating from God
• ••Knowledge and Understanding••
	• Syllogism: an argument or logical reasoning
	• Identity: sameness of a thing
	• Analysis: resolution or breaking down into parts
• ••Existence and Being••
	• Existence: the actual being of a thing
	• Quiddity: the being or essence of a thing
	• Contingent: accidental or dependent on circumstances
• ••Causality and Agency••
	• Passive attingency: the efficacy of the will of God upon one thing causing another
	• Virtually: in power or potential
• ••Miscellaneous Terms••
	• Entity: the being or existence of a thing
	• Aggregation: heaping up or joining together
	• Incomplete: imperfect or lacking something
• ••Theological and Philosophical Concepts••
	• Consubstantiation: the being of two substances together
	• Tropes: a translation of the signification of words
	• Delegated: appointed or entrusted with a task

• ••Causes of change••
	• The four causes of change in the world are:
		- Material cause (what something is made of)
		- Formal cause (what makes something one thing rather than another)
		- Efficient cause (what actually produces the change or effect)
		- Final cause (why efficient causes do what they do)

â€¢ ••Definition of Divinity••
	• Divinity is the doctrine of living to God
	• It is a doctrine that comes from divine revelation and institution, not human invention

â€¢ ••Nature of Divinity••
	• Divinity is concerned with life, the most noble of all acts
	• It is about living according to the will of God, to the glory of God

â€¢ ••Faith as a Cause••
	• Faith is the means by which we receive new life
	• Without faith, it is impossible to please God (Heb 11:6)

â€¢ ••Faith as a Material Cause••
	• Faith is a quality of the heart and mind
	• It is the cause of the application of new life

â€¢ ••Affection••
	• Affection is a felt response to an object called forth by an understanding of its nature
	• It is an exercise of the will, not a passion that enslaves the will

â€¢ ••Divinity as a Doctrine••
	• Divinity is a doctrine that is not from nature or human invention
	• It is based on divine revelation and institution

â€¢ ••Principles of Divinity••
	• The principles of divinity are not inbred in us from nature
	• They must be studied and learned through revelation

â€¢ ••Life as the Focus of Divinity••
	• Life is the most noble of all acts
	• Divinity is concerned with living to God, with God inwardly working in us

â€¢ ••Eternal Life••
	• Eternal life remains one and the same from its beginning to eternity
	• It is a gift to those who believe in the Son (Joh 3:36, 5:24)

â€¢ ••Goodness vs. Blessedness••
	• Living well is more excellent than living happily
	• Goodness, which is referred to God's glory, is what should be respected chiefly and finally

	• All aspects of Divinity tend towards practice and living well

	• No precept in household government, morality, or politics is not related to Divinity
	• Divinity is the supreme art, treating of God and divine matters

	• Faith is believing in God and His word
	• Observance is following God's commands and living according to His will

	• Abraham's faith and observance are examples of this (Genesis 15:6, 17:1)
	• Christ requires faith and observance from His disciples (Matthew 28:20)

	• It is not just a theoretical or speculative discipline, but a living and working relationship with God

	• It is the masterpiece of all arts, proceeding from God and treating of divine matters
	• It is the key to living a life of faith, hope, and love, pleasing to God.

â€¢ ••Definition of Faith••
	• Faith is a resting of the heart on God as the author of life and eternal salvation
	• It involves trusting in God to be freed from evil and obtain good

â€¢ ••Nature of Faith••
	• Faith is not just an act of the understanding, but also an act of the will
	• It involves receiving and embracing the good that is made ours through faith

â€¢ ••Relationship between Faith and Obedience••
	• Faith and obedience are always joined together, but are distinct in nature and precepts
	• Faith holds the first place, and spiritual obedience holds the latter place

â€¢ ••Order of Faith and Obedience••
	• Faith must come first, as there can be no vital actions without a principle of life
	• Obedience is the natural result of faith

â€¢ ••Definition of Spiritual Obedience••
	• Spiritual obedience is the act of observing God's commands and living a life of good deeds

â€¢ ••Purpose of Faith••
	• Faith is not just about intellectual assent, but about trusting in God and receiving good
	• It involves a willingness to obey God's commands and live a life of good deeds

â€¢ ••Importance of Faith••
	• Faith is essential for salvation, as it involves trusting in God and receiving eternal life
	• Without faith, there can be no saving knowledge or obedience to God's commands

â€¢ ••Relationship between Faith and the Law••
	• Faith does not make the law void, but rather establishes it
	• Faith is not a license to continue in sin, but rather a motivation to live a life of obedience

â€¢ ••Importance of Practicing Good Deeds••
	• Practicing good deeds is essential for demonstrating faith and living a life of obedience
	• It involves going before others in doing good and living a life of service to God

â€¢ ••Purpose of Teaching Faith and Obedience••
	• The purpose of teaching faith and obedience is to help people live a life of good deeds and service to God
	• It involves affirming the importance of faith and obedience in the life of a Christian.

Here are the 13 points summarized in 10 bullets with sub-bullets:

• ••The Nature of Faith••
	• Faith is a choice grounded in the belief that God is speaking, promising, and declaring.
	• It is a trust in God's power and goodness.
• ••The Object of Faith••
	• The object of faith is God, not as He is considered in Himself, but as He is revealed in Christ.
	• Christ is the mediator of faith, but not the highest object of faith.
• ••The Role of Scripture••
	• Scripture contains and presents the object of faith.
	• The promises and sentences in Scripture are the object of faith by metonymy.
• ••The End and Effect of Faith••
	• The good that is proposed to be obtained is the end and effect of faith.
	• The One upon whose power we rest in obtaining that good is the proper object of faith.
• ••The Authority of God••
	• The authority of God is the proper and immediate ground of all truth to be believed.
	• The Word of the Lord is the source of all truth.
• ••The Resolution of Faith••
	• The last resolution of faith is into the authority of God or Divine revelation.
	• It is also into the operation and inward persuading of the Holy Spirit.
• ••True Confidence••
	• True confidence is not a certain and absolute persuasion of the good to come.
	• It is a choosing and apprehending a sufficient and fit means, and on such a persuasion and expectation is founded.
• ••The Importance of Divine Faith••
	• We must not simply believe any man, but God above.
	• Our faith must be grounded in the will of God and our own salvation.
• ••The Role of the Holy Spirit••
	• The Holy Spirit is the one who operates and persuades us to believe in God.
	• None can call Jesus Lord except by the Holy Spirit.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Faith••
	• Faith is not just a general assent to the truth, but a special assent that involves a personal relationship with God.
	• It involves leaning on God, resting in Him, and trusting in His salvation.

â€¢ ••Papists' Definition of Faith••
	• The Papists define faith as a general assent to the truth, which can be without any life or action.
	• This is not the biblical definition of faith, which involves a personal relationship with God.

â€¢ ••Faith as an Act of the Will••
	• Faith is not just an act of the understanding, but also an act of the will.
	• It involves a personal decision to trust in God and His salvation.

â€¢ ••Faith as Union with God••
	• Faith involves a union with God, which cannot be achieved by just giving assent to the truth.
	• It requires a personal relationship with God, which involves a consent of the will.

â€¢ ••Faith and Confidence••
	• Confidence is a fruit of faith, but it is also a part of faith itself.
	• It involves a firm hope in God for what is to come, and a trust in God's present offer of salvation.

â€¢ ••Titles of Faith••
	• Faith is often referred to as persuasion, boldness, and a full persuasion in Scripture.
	• It is also referred to as the substance of things hoped for, which involves a personal relationship with God.

â€¢ ••Faith and Assurance••
	• Faith involves a personal assurance of salvation, which cannot be achieved by just giving assent to the truth.
	• It requires a personal relationship with God, which involves a consent of the will.

â€¢ ••Faith and Works••
	• Faith without works is dead, according to James 2:17.
	• Faith involves a personal decision to trust in God and His salvation, which requires action and obedience.

â€¢ ••Faith and the Will••
	• Faith involves a personal decision to trust in God and His salvation, which requires a consent of the will.
	• It involves a personal relationship with God, which cannot be achieved by just giving assent to the truth.

â€¢ ••Faith and the Understanding••
	• Faith involves a personal understanding of the truth, but it also requires a personal relationship with God.
	• It involves a consent of the will, which cannot be achieved by just giving assent to the truth.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Faith••
	• Faith is a single virtue that brings together acts of science and affection (1 Cor 13:13)
	• Faith is confidence in the promises of the Gospel
	• Faith is a solid assent that apprehends the actual possession of God's grace

â€¢ ••Knowledge of God••
	• God's essence cannot be fully understood by humans (1 Tim 6:16)
	• God reveals himself to us, but only partially and in a way that is understandable to humans (Exo 33:23)
	• Our understanding of God is limited and must be expressed in human terms (anthropopathic language)

â€¢ ••Understanding God's Nature••
	• We cannot know God's nature fully, but we can know enough to live well (Exo 33:19-20)
	• What we can know about God is sufficient for us to live well (Deu 29:29)
	• God's sufficiency and efficiency are the two pillars of faith

â€¢ ••God's Sufficiency••
	• God's sufficiency refers to his ability to provide for himself and for us (Gen 17:1)
	• God's sufficiency is the first ground for our faith, as it shows that he is able to give us life (Rom 4:20)
	• God's sufficiency is rooted in his essence and substance

â€¢ ••God's Essence••
	• God's essence refers to his being as the absolutely first and only God (Isa 44:6)
	• God's essence is declared in his name, Jehovah (YHWH)
	• God's essence is the foundation of his sufficiency and efficiency

â€¢ ••God's Efficiency••
	• God's efficiency refers to his ability to perform his promises (Rom 4:21)
	• God's efficiency is a key aspect of his sufficiency and is closely tied to his essence

â€¢ ••Faith and Assurance••
	• Faith can be lacking in some people, but true faith is still present in their hearts
	• Assurance of understanding may be lacking, but faith can still be present

â€¢ ••The Importance of Faith••
	• Faith is a key aspect of true religion and is closely tied to God's sufficiency and efficiency
	• Faith is the foundation of comfort and piety

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Faith and God••
	• Faith is based on our understanding of God's sufficiency and efficiency
	• Our understanding of God's nature is limited, but we can know enough to live well

â€¢ ••The Role of Anthropopathic Language••
	• Anthropopathic language is used to describe God's nature in human terms
	• This language is necessary because we cannot fully understand God's essence.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••God's Essence••
	• God is one and only one (Deu 6.4, 1Tim 2.5, Eph 4.6, 1Cor 8.5-6, Mk 12.32, Rom 3.29-30)
	• God exists of himself, not from another, nor of another, nor by another, nor for another
• ••God's Attributes••
	• Attributes are said to be attributed to God, rather than properly being in him
	• Attributes are one most pure and simple act, and can be explained as consequences or conclusions
• ••Nature of Divine Attributes••
	• All attributes of God are truly spoken of God in the abstract, as well as in the concrete
	• Attributes that are common to God and creatures belong first to God, and secondarily to creatures
• ••Characteristics of Divine Attributes••
	• Divine attributes allow for no inward intention, extension, remission, or imperfection
	• Attributes are not contrary to one another, but agree well together
• ••Perfection of Divine Attributes••
	• All Divine attributes are Divine perfections, with imperfections removed in their application to God
	• Attributes are conceived with greatest eminence in God
• ••Existence of Divine Attributes••
	• Attributes exist in God not only virtually, but also formally, although not in the same manner as in creatures
	• Attributes exist in God as in a secondary essence
• ••Distinction of Divine Attributes••
	• Attributes are distinguished from the Essence, and among themselves, not only in reason, but also in reality
	• The foundation of the distinction is in God himself
• ••Incommunicability of Divine Attributes••
	• Attributes that include something proper to the Divine Essence are altogether incommunicable to creatures
	• Examples include Omnipotency, Immensity, Eternity, and similar attributes
• ••Communication of Divine Attributes••
	• Attributes that are said to be communicated to creatures are agreeable only in their likeness, and not altogether in the way they exist in God
	• Attributes are not communicated equivocally, but in a way that is similar, yet distinct
• ••Conclusion••
	• The attributes of God are complex and multifaceted, and can be understood in various ways, but ultimately, they are one most pure and simple act.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••God's Nature••
	• God is a Spirit (Joh 4.24, Joh 5.26)
	• God is a Spirit because he is not a body (negatively) and has many perfections in common with spiritual substances (analogically)
• ••God's Life••
	• God is Living (Joh 5.26)
	• God's life is his very Essence (vital action)
	• God is the Fountain of all being and the vital operation to other living things (Act 17.25, 28)
• ••God's Independence••
	• God receives neither being nor life from any other
	• God lives in himself
• ••God's Distinction from Idols••
	• God is distinguished from idols as the living God (Deu 32.40, Psa 84.2, Jer 5.2)
• ••God's Attributes••
	• God's attributes are indivisible (inequality between persons of the Godhead)
	• God's attributes are identical to those of the other persons of the Godhead
• ••God's Greatness••
	• God is One (not in kind, but in perfect unity)
	• God is Infinite (void of all bounds of his Essence)
	• God is Eternal (without beginning and end)
• ••God's Immeasurability••
	• God is unmeasurable (void of all matter of dimension or measure)
	• God is incomprehensible (void of any bounds to encompass him)
• ••God's Presence••
	• God is present everywhere (no place from which he is excluded)
• ••God's Faithfulness••
	• Faith looks for no certain measure of blessedness from God, but instead unmeasurable glory
	• Faith apprehends eternal life in God
• ••God's Faculties and Virtues••
	• God's faculties are understanding and will
	• God's virtues are the properties of Essence, quantity, simplicity, immutability, eternity, and immensity

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Nature of God••
	• God is simple, without composition, discourse, or representation of shapes (Heb 4:13)
	• God is one and the same thing, not only in nature, but in number
• ••The Understanding of God••
	• The understanding of God is unchangeable (Act 15:18)
	• The understanding of God is eternal (Act 15:18)
	• The understanding of God is infinite, perceiving all truths and reasons for all things (Job 11:8-9, Psa 139:6)
• ••The Will of God••
	• The will of God is single and only one in God
	• The will of God is unchangeable, always willing the same in the same manner (Psa 33:1)
	• The will of God is eternal, not beginning or ceasing to will what it did or did not will (Mal 3:6)
	• The will of God is infinite, with no outward limitation
• ••The Attributes of God••
	• Virtues such as wisdom and holiness are the perfection of the understanding and will of God
	• Virtues that arise from sin and imperfection in man do not agree to God
• ••The Perfection of God••
	• The perfection of God is the source of all blessedness and glory
	• Our faith has a firm foundation because it leans on God, the possessor and author of all perfection
• ••The Subsistence of God••
	• The subsistence of God is the one essence with its personal properties
	• The same essence is common to three subsistences (the three Persons of the Godhead)
	• Nothing is attributed to the essence that may not be attributed to every subsistence in regard to the essence of it

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Distinguishing the Subsistences••
	• The subsistences are distinguished from the Essence.
	• They are distinguished among themselves as Relatives.
• ••Relative Properties••
	• Relative properties are individuating in an Essence that lives spiritually and perfectly.
	• These properties are not inherent qualities, but relative affections.
• ••Perfection and Imperfection••
	• Perfection in the Creature agrees with the relative affections.
	• Imperfection in the Creature does not agree with the relative affections.
• ••God's Relative Properties••
	• A relative property in God makes or infers a person.
	• This is different from the Creatures, where the same reason does not apply.
• ••The Father's Relative Property••
	• The Father's relative property is to beget.
	• This is seen in passages such as Psalm 2:7 and John 3:16.
• ••The Son's Relative Property••
	• The Son's relative property is to be begotten.
	• This is seen in passages such as Hebrews 1:3 and John 1:14.
• ••The Holy Spirit's Relative Property••
	• The Holy Spirit's relative property is to be breathed or sent forth.
	• This is seen in passages such as John 15:26 and Romans 8:9.
• ••The Difference between Begotten and Proceeding••
	• The difference between being begotten and proceeding cannot be fully explained.
	• However, the Son proceeds from the Father alone, while the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father and the Son.
• ••Similitudes••
	• The Father can be thought of as Deus intelligens, God understanding.
	• The Son can be thought of as Deus expressus, God expressed.
	• The Holy Spirit can be thought of as Deus dilectus, God beloved.
• ••The Son's Production••
	• The Son is produced by an act of understanding or speaking from the Father's understanding or fruitful memory.
	• The Holy Spirit is produced by an act of loving or breathing from the Father's and Son's fruitful will.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Scripture's description of the Trinity••
	• The procession of the Son is described in Scripture
	• There is no special name or manner of proceeding given to the Third Person (Holy Spirit)

â€¢ ••Proper name and titles of God••
	• The proper name of God (Jehovah) is given to the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit
	• Titles such as "God", "Lord", and "King of Kings" are also given to the Son and Holy Spirit

â€¢ ••Divine attributes••
	• Attributes such as "most mighty God" and "Father of eternity" are given to the Son
	• Attributes such as "Spirit" and "eternal" are given to the Holy Spirit

â€¢ ••Proper operations of God••
	• Election is attributed to the Son (His elect)
	• Eternal counsel is attributed to the Holy Spirit (Who has weighed the Spirit of the Lord)
	• Creation is attributed to the Son (All things were made by him) and the Holy Spirit (By the Word of the Lord)

â€¢ ••Upholding and governing created things••
	• Upholding and governing created things is attributed to the Son (Who upholds all things by his mighty Word)
	• Upholding and governing created things is also attributed to the Holy Spirit (By my Spirit says the Lord of Hosts)

â€¢ ••Power to do miracles••
	• Power to do miracles is given to the Son (Through the name of Jesus Christ)
	• Power to do miracles is also given to the Holy Spirit (They began to speak with tongues, as the Spirit gave them utterance)

â€¢ ••Communicating spiritual life and grace••
	• Communicating spiritual life and all grace is given to the Son and the Holy Spirit
	• This includes justification, adoption, sanctification, and glorification

â€¢ ••Ordaining, sending, and blessing of ecclesiastical ministry••
	• The ordaining, sending, and blessing of ecclesiastical ministry is given to the Son (He gave gifts, .he gave some Apostles)
	• The ordaining, sending, and blessing of ecclesiastical ministry is also given to the Holy Spirit (The Holy Spirit has made you overseers)

â€¢ ••Resurrection of the flesh••
	• The resurrection of the flesh is ascribed to the Son (I will raise him up)
	• The resurrection of the flesh is also ascribed to the Holy Spirit (He shall raise up your bodies by his Spirit dwelling in you)

â€¢ ••Divine honor and worship••
	• Divine honor and worship is given to the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit
	• The Son and Holy Spirit are called upon in worship, along with the Father (In the name of the Father, and the Son, and Holy Spirit)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••1. The Nature of God's Temples••

• The Bible refers to believers as temples of God, both individually and collectively.
	• Rev 21:22 - The Lord God Almighty is her Temple, and the Lamb.
	• 1Cor 3:16 - You are the Temple of God, and the Spirit of God dwells in you.
	• 1Cor 6:19 - Your body is the Temple of the Holy Spirit.

••2. The Authority and Majesty of God••

• The authority and majesty of God is given to the Son and the Holy Spirit.
	• 1Cor 2:8 - The Lord of glory.
	• 1Pet 4:14 - The Spirit of glory.
	• 1Pet 3:19 - Christ by his Spirit went and preached to the spirits in prison.

••3. The Holy Spirit as a Person••

• The Holy Spirit is a person subsisting, with life, understanding, will, and power.
	• 2Pet 1:21 - Holy men spoke being moved by the Holy Spirit.
	• Act 28:25 - The Holy Spirit spoke by Isaiah the Prophet.

••4. The Distinction of the Holy Spirit••

• The Holy Spirit is distinct from the Father and the Son.
	• Joh 14 - The Holy Spirit is called another, or he is sent, as coming from the Father and the Son.

••5. The Sufficiency of God••

• God is sufficient to impart salvation to us, as all love, grace, and communication of good things flow from the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.
	• 2Cor 13:14 - The grace of the Lord Jesus Christ, and the love of God, and the communion of the Holy Spirit be with you all.

••6. The Efficiency of God••

• The efficiency of God is the power by which he works all things.
	• Eph 1:11 - Who works all things.
	• Rom 11:36 - Of him, by him, and for him are all things.

••7. The Nature of God's Efficiency••

• The effecting, working, or acting of God is not really different from God himself.
	• No composition or mutation of power and act can have a place in the most simple and immutable nature of God.

••8. The Order of God's Efficiency••

• The order of God's efficiency is: to be able, to know, to will, and to effect.
	• This order is distinct from the effectual will of God, but only in reason.

••9. The Power of God••

• The power of God is his sufficiency, and it properly pertains to the nature of God as a being.
	• Rom 11:23 - For God is able to graft them in again.

••10. The Execution of God's Power••

• The power of God, as it is being executed, is in some sort after sufficiency, and it pertains to the efficiency of God.
	• Psa 115:3; 135:6 - Whatever he pleased, he did.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Will of God is a Powerful Principle••
	• The Will of God is an effecting principle that has a kind of power (Rom 9:19)
	• Executive Omnipotence is the effecting will of God (Psa 33:9, Rev 4:11)
• ••God's Omnipotence is Not Limited••
	• God is able to effect all things that he wills or can will (2Chr 20:6, Luk 1:37)
	• God is able to subject all things to himself (Phi 3:21)
• ••Attributes of God's Power••
	• God is called "mighty God" in the Old Testament (Isa 9:5, Jer 32:18)
	• God is called "God all-sufficient" in the Old Testament (Gen 17:1, 35:11)
	• God is called "the Lord Almighty" in the New Testament (2Cor 6:18, Rev 1:8)
• ••Active Power in God••
	• Active power is attributed to God as a causing power (Potentia, vel potestas causae)
	• Active power is not a different thing from God's Essence, but rather an expression of his power
• ••The Nature of God's Omnipotence••
	• God's Omnipotence is conversant about things that are absolutely impossible
	• God's power is not exercised about things that are altogether impossible
• ••Distinction between Absolute and Ordinate Power••
	• Absolute power is the ability to do all things possible, even if they shall never be
	• Ordinate power is the ability to do what God wills, and actually does what he wills
• ••The Manner of God's Subsistence••
	• The co-working of all Persons is a manner of God's subsistence
	• The distinct manner of the Persons in working is also a manner of God's subsistence
• ••The Co-Working of the Persons••
	• The Persons work together inseparably in external actions
	• Each Person works of himself in terms of causal power
• ••No Pre-eminence of Dignity••
	• There is no pre-eminence of dignity among the Persons in terms of causal power
	• Each Person has equal dignity and causal power
• ••The Nature of God's Power••
	• God's power is not a different thing from his Essence, but rather an expression of his power
	• God's power is not exercised about things that are altogether impossible

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Trinity and their roles••
	• The Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are distinct Persons with equal honor
	• Each Person works according to their distinct manner of subsistence

â€¢ ••The manner of working••
	• The Father's manner of working is of himself, by the Son and Holy Spirit
	• The Son's manner of working is from the Father, by the Spirit
	• The Holy Spirit's manner of working is from the Father and the Son by himself

â€¢ ••The order of working••
	• The Father is the first Person in the order of beginning
	• The Father is responsible for creation
	• The Son is responsible for redemption
	• The Holy Spirit is responsible for sanctification

â€¢ ••The termination of action••
	• The termination of action is attributed to the Person who chiefly shines forth in that action
	• Creation is attributed to the Father
	• Redemption is attributed to the Son
	• Sanctification is attributed to the Holy Spirit

â€¢ ••The Decree of God••
	• The Decree of God is his determinate purpose of effecting all things by his almighty Power
	• The Decree of God is eternal and unchanging

â€¢ ••Attributes of the Decree of God••
	• Constancy: the Decree of God always remains immutable
	• Truth: God declares what he alone has decreed
	• Faithfulness: God effects what he has decreed, as he has decreed it

â€¢ ••The Counsel of God••
	• The Counsel of God pertains to his Decree
	• God's Counsel is eternal and unchanging

â€¢ ••The power of God••
	• With God, all things are possible
	• God does whatever he pleases, in heaven and on earth

â€¢ ••The role of the Holy Spirit••
	• The Holy Spirit is responsible for the communication of things, such as regeneration and spiritual gifts
	• The Holy Spirit is also responsible for the perfection of natural things

â€¢ ••The relationship between the Decree and the manner of working••
	• The Decree of God is the most perfect manner of working, and it chiefly agrees to the Divine Nature

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••God's Mercy and Salvation••
	• God saves us through the washing of regeneration and renewing of the Holy Spirit (1 Corinthians 12:4-11)
	• This salvation is a result of God's mercy and not our own efforts
• ••God's Goodness and Creation••
	• God saw everything He created and declared it very good (Genesis 1:31)
	• God's creation is a reflection of His goodness and perfection
• ••God's Sovereignty and Election••
	• God elects people according to His foreknowledge and sanctifies them through the Spirit (1 Peter 1:2)
	• God's election is not based on human merit but on His sovereign will
• ••God's Wisdom and Counsel••
	• God's wisdom is a mystery that He ordained before the ages for our glory (1 Corinthians 2:7)
	• God's counsel is His deliberate and intentional plan for all things
• ••God's Counsel and Perfection••
	• God's counsel is perfect and involves a scope or end, intention, and good pleasure of the will
	• The scope or end of God's counsel is the glory of God Himself
• ••God's Platform and Mind••
	• God has a platform or mind that exists before He creates anything
	• This platform is the Divine Essence, which is the exemplary cause of all things
• ••God's Knowledge and Creation••
	• God understands all things by composition or genesis, not by analysis or resolution
	• God's knowledge is the copy-platform, and the things themselves are the image or express likeness of it
• ••Human Knowledge and Creation••
	• Human knowledge is the image or copy of the things themselves
	• Human knowledge is collected about things themselves through analysis or resolution
• ••God's Sovereignty and Purpose••
	• God works all things according to the counsel of His will (Ephesians 1:11)
	• God's purpose is to bring glory to Himself and to manifest His goodness and perfection
• ••God's Glory and Inheritance••
	• We have obtained an inheritance in Christ, being predestined according to the purpose of God (Ephesians 1:11)
	• This inheritance is a result of God's sovereign will and counsel.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••God's Deliberation••
	• God does not deliberate in the same way humans do (2)
	• God is deliberate, meaning He has a clear plan and purpose (2)
• ••God's Nature••
	• God is the inventor and creator of all things (3)
	• God's nature is reflected in the image of man (5)
• ••Ideas in God••
	• Ideas in God are not impressed from external sources, but are properly expressing (6)
	• Ideas in God are manifold, reflecting the diversity of creation (19)
• ••Perfections and Imperfections••
	• God has platforms of all perfections in creation (20)
	• God does not have platforms of imperfections, as they are formally considered as privations of good (20)
• ••Knowledge of Evil••
	• The knowledge of evil depends on denying good (21)
	• The being of evil consists in the privation of good (21)
• ••Divine Knowledge••
	• Divine knowledge is distinguished into two types: knowledge of simple understanding and knowledge of vision (24)
	• Knowledge of simple understanding is knowledge of all possible things (25)
	• Knowledge of vision is the knowledge of all future things (26)
• ••God's Knowledge••
	• God knows all possible things by simple intelligence (25)
	• God knows all future things by vision, which is tied to His efficiency and decree (27)
• ••Middle Knowledge••
	• Some imagine a middle knowledge by which God knows events that would come to pass if certain causes were put (28)
	• This middle knowledge is not compatible with the great perfection of God (28)
• ••The Divine Idea••
	• The Divine Idea is one, but includes diverse respects to creation (19)
	• The Divine Idea is manifold, reflecting the diversity of creation (19)
• ••God's Sovereignty••
	• God frames the hearts of men and observes all their works (27)
	• God declares and orders the things to come, from the time He disposed the people forever (27)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••God's Knowledge••
	• Has different aspects, including:
		- Intelligence (perceiving every thing in every thing)
		- Truth (belonging to every thing)
		- Science (omniscience, prescience, sapience, prudence, and art)
• ••Arminianism••
	• A concept that makes God dependent on human actions
	• Makes God react to human actions rather than causing them
• ••God's Essence••
	• The being or nature that makes something what it is
	• Different from other things
• ••God's Will••
	• An act of Divine will that determines all things
	• Most freely and effectually determines all things
• ••Good Pleasure••
	• An act of Divine will that determines a saving good
	• Used to explain every Counsel of God
• ••Freedom of God's Will••
	• Truly free, not bound by necessity of nature
	• Chiefly and absolutely free, depending on no other
• ••Freedom of Human Will••
	• Less free than God's will, due to dependence on God
	• Operates within the bounds of God's will
• ••Effectiveness of God's Will••
	• Whatever God wills, he effects it in its time
	• Nothing is done if he wills it not to be done
• ••God's Will as First Cause••
	• The first cause of things
	• By God's will, all things were created
• ••Scriptural References••
	• Acts 17:25-29
	• Hebrews 11:10
	• Psalm 115:3 and 135:6
	• Revelation 4:11

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••God's Will is not dependent on the goodness of the object••
	• God's Will can make an object good, even if it is not inherently good (Jas 1.18, Rom 9.18)
• ••There is no cause for God's Will••
	• God's Will is not caused by anything outside of itself (39)
	• God's Will is not caused by anything inside of itself, either (41)
• ••God's Will is not a cause for other things••
	• The Sun and stars are not a cause for God's Will to create and sustain the world (40)
	• God's Will is not a cause for other things, even though those things may be causes and effects among themselves (40)
• ••The Will of God is not sequential••
	• God's Will is not a series of separate acts, but rather a single, unified act (41)
	• God's Will is not dependent on the act of the creature (42)
• ••God's Will is not mutable••
	• God's Will is not changed by the act of the creature (43)
	• God's Will is not mutable, even if the creature's act is changed (43)
• ••The concept of "velleitas" is not applicable to God••
	• Velleitas is a willingness or inclination that is imperfect and not applicable to God (42)
	• God's Will is not an imperfect will, but rather a perfect and all-encompassing Will (42)
• ••God's Will is not antecedent to the act of the creature••
	• God's Will is not something that exists before the act of the creature (43)
	• God's Will is not something that is changed by the act of the creature (43)
• ••The Schoolmen's doctrine is not applicable to God••
	• The Schoolmen's doctrine of passive attingency is not applicable to God (41)
	• God's Will is not a passive attingency, but rather an active and perfect Will (41)
• ••God's Will is not dependent on the creature's act••
	• God's Will is not dependent on the creature's act, even if the creature's act is necessary for God's Will to be fulfilled (42)
	• God's Will is not a consequential event dependent on the creature's will (42)
• ••The form of speech "I will do this or that, if God wills" is not applicable to God••
	• This form of speech would imply that God's Will is dependent on the creature's act (44)
	• This form of speech would imply that God's Will is mutable and dependent on the creature's act (44)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's Sovereignty••
	• God determines all things, including the greatest and least, contingent and necessary, and free events.
	• Scripture shows this in various examples, such as Christ's glorification and the Church's salvation.

â€¢ ••Examples of God's Sovereignty••
	• The selling of Joseph (Genesis 37-50)
	• Pharaoh's hardening and Israel's persecution (Exodus 13)
	• The casting of lots (Proverbs 16:33)
	• The fall of sparrows and the number of hairs on a person's head (Matthew 10:29-30)

â€¢ ••God's Will••
	• God's will is the first cause of all things.
	• If God did not determine all things, his will would not be the first cause.

â€¢ ••Reasons for God's Sovereignty••
	• God's knowledge knows all things that may be known.
	• God's power can do all possible things.
	• God's will is not limited to what actually happens, but can stretch beyond it.

â€¢ ••Scriptural Support••
	• Psalm 2:12, 110:4, 40:7-9
	• Hebrews 7:15-21
	• Ephesians 5:25
	• 2 Timothy 1:9

â€¢ ••Human Freedom••
	• Pharaoh and Israel worked freely, despite God's sovereignty.
	• Humans have the ability to make choices, but God's sovereignty is not limited by human freedom.

â€¢ ••God's Control over Human Hearts••
	• Psalm 33:15
	• 1 Samuel 10:9, 26
	• Proverbs 21:1

â€¢ ••God's Sovereignty over Chance Events••
	• Exodus 21:13 (man killing another man by chance)
	• Proverbs 16:33 (casting of lots)

â€¢ ••God's Sovereignty over Creation••
	• Job 38, Psalm 104, Isaiah 45:7, Jeremiah 14:22, 46

â€¢ ••Implications of God's Sovereignty••
	• If God did not determine all things, there would be multiple first causes, which is not true.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's Will and Omnipotence••
	• God's will is not limited by His omnipotence (48)
	• God's will is universally effectual, and He cannot be hindered or frustrated (48)
	• God's will implies a certainty of the event, but not a necessity (49)

â€¢ ••God's Will and Human Freedom••
	• God's will does not impose necessity on all future things (49)
	• God's will is the prime root and efficient cause of all contingency and freedom in things (50)

â€¢ ••God's Will and Order••
	• God's will conceives a certain order, with the end preceding the means (51)
	• God works by most perfect reason, and the order of intention is contrary to the order of execution (51)

â€¢ ••God's Will and Revelation••
	• God's will is partly hidden and partly revealed (52)
	• The means by which God's will is revealed are called the Will of the sign (53)

â€¢ ••Signs of God's Will••
	• There are five signs of God's will: Command, Forbid, Permit, Counsel, and Fulfill (54)
	• Counsel is the same as Command, and it would be better to use Promittit (He promises) instead (54)

â€¢ ••Efficiency of God••
	• The efficiency of God is either creation or providence (1)
	• Creation is the efficiency of God whereby He made the world from nothing (2)

â€¢ ••Active and Passive Creation••
	• Active creation is conceived as a transient action with an object presupposed (3)
	• Passive creation is conceived as the manner of making something from nothing (4)

â€¢ ••God's Will and Scripture••
	• God's will is not limited by Scripture, but Scripture is a means of revealing God's will (52)
	• The bones of Christ were not broken, as God willed it, but there was no necessity imposed on the soldiers' spears (49)

â€¢ ••God's Sufficiency and Faith••
	• God's sufficiency and efficiency make a fit and adequate object of faith (55)
	• Faith is necessary to understand God's efficiency and sufficiency (55)

â€¢ ••God's Will and Human Understanding••
	• God's will is not fully comprehensible by human understanding (52)
	• The secret things belong to God, but those things which are revealed belong to us and our children (Deu 29:29)

Here are 10 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Creation and God's Relationship with the World••
	• Creation respects the whole world, including all things that exist besides God.
	• All things that exist besides God are created and are altogether created, including their matter and form.
• ••Nature of Creation••
	• Creation produces originally, meaning it produces a being that exists absolutely in every part.
	• Creation produces out of nothing, meaning it produces from matter that does not pre-exist.
• ••Pre-Existence of Creatures••
	• Before creation, creatures had no real being, either of existence or essence.
	• Creatures had a known being in the knowledge of God from eternity.
• ••Creation and God's Perfection and Freedom••
	• God's perfection is displayed in creation, as he did not need any creature or outward thing.
	• God's freedom is displayed in creation, as he brought forth all things without natural necessity.
• ••Time and Creation••
	• The world was not made from eternity, nor could it be created from eternity in its current disposition and order.
	• The concept of time is tied to the creation of the world, and would not have existed if the world had been created from eternity.
• ••Causes of Creation••
	• No creature was or could be a cause, either instrumentally or principally, in the act of creation.
• ••Goodness of Creation••
	• Everything created was very good, as it was made neither rashly nor in vain, but for a specific end.
	• The goodness of a created thing is its perfection, which makes it fit for its use.
• ••Types of Use••
	• Particular use refers to the proper operation of a thing according to its nature.
	• Universal use refers to the ordaining of one thing with others for the perfection of the universal whole.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's Goodness and Creation••
	• All created things tend towards God from whom they came (Rom 11:36)
	• God's goodness is the reason for creation (Isa 48:13, Psa 19:1)

â€¢ ••Natural Things and God's Glory••
	• Natural things declare God's glory (Psa 19:1)
	• They give occasion for us to know and seek God (Rom 1:20, Act 17:26-27)
	• They sustain our life, so we can live well to God (1Cor 10:31, 1Tim 4:3-4)

â€¢ ••Time and Place••
	• Time exists with all natural things (Gen 1:1)
	• Place exists, bounded by the extension of creatures (Gen 1:22)

â€¢ ••Con-Creation vs. Creation••
	• Time and place are not properly created, but con-created (annexed) to created things
	• They have a relative, not absolute, existence

â€¢ ••Faith and Creation••
	• God created all things out of nothing (Rom 4:17, 18)
	• Our faith rests in God against hope, under hope, for things that are not (Rom 4:17, 18)

â€¢ ••The Creation of the World••
	• The world is one, but consists of parts (distinguished by situation, etc.)
	• The creation of the world is distributed according to its parts

â€¢ ••The Use of Creatures••
	• Every creature of God is good, and nothing is to be refused if received with thanksgiving (1Tim 4:4)
	• Where it does not fit its intended use, it is perverse (Isa 48:13)

â€¢ ••God's Hand in Creation••
	• God's hand laid the foundation of the earth (Isa 48:13)
	• God's right hand stretched out the heavens (Isa 48:13)

â€¢ ••The Glory of God in Creation••
	• The heavens declare God's glory (Psa 19:1)
	• God's invisible attributes are clearly seen in creation (Rom 1:20)

â€¢ ••The Purpose of Creation••
	• Creation is for the glory of God (1Cor 10:31, 1Tim 4:3-4)
	• God's purpose is that we seek Him and find Him (Act 17:26-27)

â€¢ ••Essential Change••

	• Hidden wisdom ordained before the ages (1Cor 2.7-9)
	• Prepared for those who love Him (1Cor 2.9)

â€¢ ••Future Knowledge••
	• Now we see in a mirror, dimly (1Cor 13.12)
	• Then face to face, just as we are known (1Cor 13.12)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Nature of Creation••
	• Things were made from principles that existed before
	• Creatures are subject to change and corruption
• ••The Process of Creation••
	• Things have a double existence: a rude and incomplete existence, and a complete and beautiful existence
	• The rude existence was in the unformed mass of Earth, Waters, and the Deep
• ••The Constitution of Creation••
	• Two things are respected in the constitution of creation: manner and order
	• Manner contains four things:
		- The command of God
		- His approbation (acknowledging that the thing brought forth was good)
		- His ordination (assigning to everything its use)
		- Establishing law and order
• ••The Order of Creation••
	• The order of creation was:
		- Day 1: The heavens, angels, and unshapen mass were brought forth
		- Day 2: Air was made
		- Day 3: The waters were gathered, the sea was separated, and the earth appeared
		- Day 4: The luminaries of heaven were made
		- Day 5: Fishes and birds were brought forth
		- Day 6: Terrestrial living creatures were brought forth, including man
• ••The Wisdom, Power, and Goodness of God••
	• The wisdom, power, and goodness of God shine forth in creation
	• God's wisdom is seen in the order of creation, with simple elements created before complex ones
	• God's power is seen in the creation of the heavens and earth
	• God's goodness is seen in the creation of all things for a good end and use
• ••The Imperfection of Creation••
	• The imperfection of some things depends on the perfection of others
	• The former things (command, approbation, ordination, and law) are not severally expressed in some kinds of things
• ••The Purpose of Creation••
	• The purpose of creation is to show the wisdom, power, and goodness of God
	• The order of creation is to demonstrate God's wisdom, power, and goodness
• ••The Completion of Creation••
	• The heavens and earth were perfected on the sixth day
	• All the hosts of heaven and earth were brought forth
• ••The Significance of Creation••
	• The creation of the world is a demonstration of God's wisdom, power, and goodness
	• The order of creation is a reflection of God's character
• ••The Implications of Creation••
	• The creation of the world implies the existence of a creator God
	• The order of creation implies a rational and intentional creator

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Creation and Progress••
	• God created things in a hierarchical order, from simple to complex
	• The hierarchy is from things that are more perfect to things that are less perfect in simple things, but from less perfect to more perfect in compound things
• ••God's Power and Goodness••
	• God's power is shown in creating plants, herbs, and trees before creating the sun and stars
	• God's goodness is shown in creating dwellings before inhabitants, food before living creatures, and useful things before man
• ••Man as the Compendium of Creatures••
	• Man is the last of the creatures and is the compendium of all creatures
	• Man partakes in the nature of both the mediately perfect creatures and the immediately perfect creatures
• ••Man's Creation••
	• Man was created in a different manner than other creatures, with greater counsel and deliberation
	• The body was prepared first, and then the soul was inspired
• ••The Excellency of Man••
	• Man's excellency is placed in bearing the image of God
	• The image of God is not found in inferior creatures, but only in man
• ••The Image of God in Man••
	• Three things are required to make an image: likeness, expression, and nobility
	• The image of God in man is imperfect, but not with a privative imperfection
• ••The Nature of the Image of God in Man••
	• The image of God in man is natural, but in a different respect
	• It is partly the very nature of man, partly it flows from the principles and perfection of nature, and partly it is due to nature in a certain manner
• ••The Inward and Outward Perfection of the Image of God in Man••
	• The inward perfection is the perfection of body and soul
	• The outward perfection is the attractiveness and use of the body agreeable to God's will
• ••The Perfection of the Soul••
	• The perfection of the soul is its immortal nature
	• It is a free principle of its own actions in understanding and will
• ••The Image of God in Man's Body and Soul••
	• The image of God in man's body is its attractiveness and use agreeable to God's will
	• The image of God in man's soul is its immortal nature and free principle of its own actions

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••1. Creation and Perfection of Man••

• Man was created in God's image and was perfect in his external and internal being.
• He was adorned with gifts of wisdom, holiness, and righteousness (Eph 4:24, Col 3:10).

••2. Dominion over Creatures••

• Man was given dominion over other creatures to use them freely for God's glory and his own necessity (Gen 1:26, 2:19-20).
• He was tasked with tilling the earth and getting food from the plants (Gen 2:5-6).

••3. Image of God••

• Man was created in the image of God, but as a creature, he was less perfect than his Creator (Col 1:15, Heb 1:3).
• His image was negatively marred or broken due to sin (Rom 6:13).

••4. Original State of Man••

• Man and his wife were created naked and not ashamed (Gen 2:25).
• They were to present themselves to God as instruments of righteousness (Rom 6:13).

••5. Creation of Man and Woman••

• Man was created from the earth, and woman was created from man (Gen 2:19-20).
• Woman was created for man's well-being (1 Cor 11:8-9).

••6. Garden of Eden••

• Man was placed in the Garden of Eden as his palace (Gen 2:15).
• The Garden was a place of perfection and completeness.

••7. Image of God in Man••

• The creation of man was an image of God or divine perfection (Eph 4:24, Col 3:10).
• This image arose from the perfection of man.

••8. Creation of Man and Woman••

• Man and woman were created from nothing, with the soul created first (1 Cor 11:8-9).
• The body of man was created from the earth, and the body of woman was created from man.

••9. Faith and the Glory of God••

• Our faith ascends above the order of nature and apprehends the light of God's glory in Jesus Christ (2 Cor 4:6).
• God commanded the light to shine out of darkness.

••10. Providence of God••

• The providence of God is his efficiency in providing for his creatures in all things according to his will (Psa 145:15-16, Pro 16:9, 33).
• God's providence is either immediate or mediate, and he does all things that come to pass immediately.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••God's Power and Work••
	• God's power attains to every effect (Deu 8:3, Isa 28:26)
	• God works immediately and inwardly in all things (Ps 145:15-16)
• ••Immediate and Mediate Work••
	• God works immediately in some cases (e.g. creation, salvation)
	• God works mediate in other cases, using second causes and their virtues (e.g. natural laws)
• ••Scriptural Examples••
	• God's work in creation (Gen 2:15)
	• God's work in salvation (1Cor 11:8-9, 2Cor 4:6)
	• God's work in providence (Eph 1:11, Psa 145:15-16)
• ••God's Use of Means••
	• God uses means to communicate dignity and efficiency to his creatures (1Sam 14:6, 1Cor 1:27-28)
	• God often uses means that are not apt to produce the desired effect (Amos 5:9, 2Chr 24:24)
• ••Faith and Means••
	• Faith does not depend on means, but on God alone (Dan 3:17)
	• God can relieve all necessities with or without means (Dan 3:17)
• ••Types of Providence••
	• Ordinary and usual providence (Hos 2:21-22)
	• Extraordinary and unusual providence (e.g. miracles)
• ••Ordinary Providence••
	• God observes the order in things appointed from the beginning (Hos 2:21-22)
	• This order arises from the Law of Nature, established by God's Word (Jer 31:35-36)
• ••The Law of Nature••
	• The Law of Nature is the order in natural things, common to all things (Jer 31:35-36)
	• It arises from the force and efficacy of God's Word (Jer 31:35-36)
• ••God's Word and Providence••
	• God's Word is the source of the Law of Nature and all things that come to pass (Jer 31:35-36)
	• God's Word is perpetual and constant, expressing the respect of a thing to come (Jer 31:35-36)
• ••God's Sovereignty••
	• God's sovereignty is evident in his use of means and his ability to relieve all necessities (Dan 3:17)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Extraordinary Providence••
	• Defined as God providing for things beyond the usual and appointed order
	• Results in a Miracle, which is an operation above the usual order
• ••Miracles••
	• A true Miracle always gives evidence of God's omnipotence
	• Only God is the Author of true Miracles
• ••Scriptural Examples••
	• 1 Samuel 14:6 - Jonathan trusts in God's ability to work for them
	• 1 Corinthians 1:27-28 - God chooses the weak and foolish to shame the strong
	• Amos 5:9 - God rains ruin on the strong
	• 2 Chronicles 24:24 - God delivers a great army into the hands of a small company
• ••Human Involvement in Miracles••
	• Humans may be moral causes of Miracles, but not efficient or instrumental causes
	• God uses human help as a sign or token of a Miracle
• ••God's Providence••
	• Providence is either conservation or gubernation
	• Conservation is God's sustaining of all things in existence and strength
• ••Conservation••
	• Defined as God making all things to persist and continue
	• Necessary for the Creature, as it depends on the Creator for being, existence, continuance, and operation
• ••Creation and Governance••
	• Conservation comes between Creation and Governance
	• Creatures are created to some end and use, which they cannot attain without conservation
• ••God's Conservation••
	• Necessary for the Creature's existence and operation
	• Without conservation, the Creature would return to nothing
• ••Human Effort••
	• Human effort is in vain without God's help
	• God gives His beloved sleep, and humans should not strive in vain
• ••God's Answer••
	• God answers the heavens and the earth (Jeremiah 33:22)
	• The earth answers with grain, wine, and oil, and responds to God's call

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Creation and Conservation••
	• Creation is the act of bringing something into existence
	• Conservation is the act of preserving something that already exists
	• Conservation is a continued creation, with God preserving all things (Neh 9:6)

â€¢ ••Gubernation (Government)••
	• Gubernation is the act of directing and leading all creatures to their proper ends
	• God is the ultimate governor of all things (Psa 29:10)
	• Creatures would never attain their end without God's governance

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Gubernation••
	• Includes means and efficacy to achieve the end
	• Order of government is certain, immoveable, and indissoluble
	• Creatures cannot withdraw themselves from all order of government (Gen 50:20)

â€¢ ••Types of Gubernation••
	• Common government: God governs all things in a like manner
	• Special government: God governs specific things or individuals in a unique way

â€¢ ••Common Government••
	• Includes the Law of nature, a natural inclination, and a natural instinct
	• Creatures have a certain obediential power to obey God's command
	• Examples: ants, mice, locusts, spiders, storks, turtles, cranes, and swallows (Pro 6:6, Pro 30:24-28, Jer 8:7)

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Common Government••
	• Creatures always look to a certain end and are governed by an intelligence that is everywhere present and omnipotent (Job 38:27)
	• Works of nature are ordained accurately and agreeably to reason (Pro 30:25-28)
	• Creatures are acted and governed by an intelligence that is everywhere present and omnipotent (Job 38:27)

â€¢ ••Government and Reason••
	• The government of all things is based on reason
	• Creatures are governed by an intelligence that is everywhere present and omnipotent
	• Works of nature are ordained accurately and agreeably to reason

â€¢ ••God's Role in Government••
	• God is the ultimate governor of all things
	• God upholds all things by the word of His power (Heb 1:3)
	• God is the brightness of His glory and the express image of His person (Heb 1:3)

â€¢ ••Government and Creatures••
	• Creatures are governed by God's government
	• Creatures have a certain obediential power to obey God's command
	• Creatures are acted and governed by an intelligence that is everywhere present and omnipotent

â€¢ ••Government and Order••
	• The order of government is certain, immoveable, and indissoluble
	• Creatures cannot withdraw themselves from all order of government
	• God's government is the ultimate source of order in the universe

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Common Gubernation••
	• All things seek their own perfection
	• Desire the conservation of the whole over their own interests
	• Heavy things are carried upward to avoid emptiness

â€¢ ••Second Causes••
	• Are determined before they act
	• Are stirred up to work by an influence or previous motion
	• Are applied to a specific object to work on

â€¢ ••God's Role in Second Causes••
	• Communicates strength and sustenance to second causes
	• Brings forth into act what was previously in the power of the creature

â€¢ ••God's Government of Creatures••
	• Is a common government that applies to all creatures
	• Is a special government that applies to intelligent creatures

â€¢ ••Special Gubernation••
	• Is God's government of reasonable creatures in a special way
	• Takes into account their immortal nature, creation in God's image, and inward principle of action

â€¢ ••Moral Government••
	• Consists in teaching and fulfilling God's revealed will
	• Involves making laws and establishing them through commanding and forbidding

â€¢ ••God's Methods of Government••
	• Governs by teaching, making laws, and establishing them
	• Governs by fulfilling, performing what he has taught

â€¢ ••God's Judgment••
	• Gives to each person according to their ways and the fruit of their doings
	• Is based on the revealed will of God

â€¢ ••God's Relationship with Creatures••
	• Creatures depend on God for their strength and sustenance
	• We trust in God alone, not in creatures that derive kindness from him

â€¢ ••Purpose of God's Government••
	• To lead creatures to an eternal state of happiness or unhappiness
	• To fulfill God's revealed will and bring about good from evil

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Covenant between God and Creatures••
	• A covenant is a transaction between God and creatures, where God commands, promises, threatens, and fulfills.
	• Creatures tie themselves in obedience to God, covenanting with Him.
• ••The Nature of the Covenant••
	• The covenant is not between equals, but between God and His servants.
	• It is a government, and therefore properly called the "covenant of God".
• ••The Purpose of the Covenant••
	• The covenant is for the purpose of governing reasonable creatures.
	• It is a means of performing God's covenant with His people.
• ••The Consequences of the Covenant••
	• The moral works of creatures respect either happiness as a reward or unhappiness as a punishment.
	• Meriting occurs in the case of unhappiness, but not in the case of happiness.
• ••The Difference between Good and Evil Works••
	• A good work is an operation expecting happiness from God as a reward.
	• Evil works are made extremely evil in their kind by the opposite privation of happiness.
• ••The Role of Conscience••
	• Conscience is the judgment of an intelligent creature about itself, as it is subjected to God.
	• Conscience is the force and reason of moral decision-making.
• ••Special Government of Creatures••
	• Special government of creatures is exercised by angels and men.
	• Angels have a special government that is either a prescription or an ordering of events.
• ••The Nature of Angelic Government••
	• Angelic government is a special prescription or ordering of events.
	• It is the same law as the moral law contained in the Decalogue.
• ••The Exceptions to the Decalogue••
	• Some precepts in the Decalogue pertain to the nature of man's body or the condition of mortal life.
	• These precepts have no place in the angelic government.
• ••The Consequences of Angelic Government••
	• Some angels are confirmed in good and endowed with full happiness.
	• Others are permitted to fall into apostasy due to their abuse of liberty.
