	• The Fall of Mankind in Adam
	• The beginning of Mankind was Adam, not Eve
• ••The Relationship between Adam and Eve••
	• Eve was made for Adam, and with him, she made one and the same beginning
	• There is no second Eve, but a second Adam (1 Corinthians 15:45, 47)
• ••The Nature of Sin••
	• Sin is a tempting of God and man
	• It is a testing of the truth and Grace of God
• ••The Consequences of Sin in Man••
	• All or most of the temptations are found in every man's sins
	• Sin is consummated as touching the Fall of Mankind in Adam
• ••The Relationship between Sin and God••
	• Sin is a testing of God's faithfulness to His Word and His Justice
	• It is forbidden in Deuteronomy 6:16, but invited in Malachi 3:10

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Guiltiness of Sin••
	• Guiltiness is not the form of sin, but an effect or consequent adjunct
	• It is partly separable, partly inseparable from sin
	• It follows sin by virtue of the Law of God adjudging punishment to sins

â€¢ ••Nature of Guiltiness••
	• It has some good in it, as it is a consequence of God's law
	• It is also a vicious thing, partaking of the nature of sin
	• It cannot be separated from sin in both respects

â€¢ ••Consequences of Guiltiness••
	• It leads to a conscience that is altogether evil, accusing and condemning us for sin
	• This leads to horror and fleeing from the presence of God

â€¢ ••Filthiness of Sin••
	• Filthiness is a spiritual pollution that makes a sinner destitute of all attractiveness and honor
	• It is a result of sin and remains in the sinner after the act of sin is past

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Filthiness••
	• It is a spot of sin, corruption, defilement, deformity, dishonesty, nakedness, uncleanness, and a blot
	• It is also called a fault (culpa)

â€¢ ••Consequences of Filthiness••
	• It leads to a turning away of God (abomination and detestation)
	• It leads to shame and confusion in a man

â€¢ ••Relationship between Guiltiness and Filthiness••
	• Filthiness immediately follows the offense of sin
	• Guiltiness is a consequence of filthiness

â€¢ ••Scriptural References••
	• Lev 5:5-6, Rom 3:9, 19, 1 Cor 15:17, Rom 1:32, Gen 3:8, 10, Heb 2:15, Rom 8:15

â€¢ ••Definition of Filthiness••
	• Filthiness is a spiritual pollution that makes a sinner vile and destitute of all attractiveness and honor

â€¢ ••Scriptural Examples••
	• Adam and Eve hid themselves from God after sinning (Gen 3:8)
	• The Israelites were guilty of sin and had to bring a trespass offering to atone for their sin (Lev 5:5-6)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Punishment is an evil••
	• It is a privation of good, specifically the good of happiness
	• It is inflicted upon the sinner for their sin, not simply contracted

â€¢ ••Punishment is related to justice••
	• It pertains to rewarding and revenging justice
	• It respects and is in order to the desert of the sin

â€¢ ••Punishment is specific to intelligent creatures••
	• It has no place except in beings who can commit sin
	• Sin is reduced to order by punishment

â€¢ ••Sin is more evil than punishment••
	• Sin is against the goodness of God, while punishment is only against the good of the creature
	• The least sin is not to be allowed, even if it means great punishment or good

â€¢ ••God's attributes shine forth in punishment••
	• Holiness: God is free from imperfections and cannot commune with sin
	• Righteousness/Justice: God inflicts evil upon those who sin
	• Mercy: God's mercy is also involved in the ordaining of punishment

â€¢ ••God's holiness is key to punishment••
	• God's holiness is his purity from any spot of sin
	• God cannot dwell with evil, and evil cannot dwell with him

â€¢ ••God's justice is revenging••
	• God inflicts evil upon those who sin
	• God's justice is a key attribute that shines forth in punishment

â€¢ ••Punishment is not simply a matter of reward and punishment••
	• It is also a matter of God's holiness and justice

â€¢ ••Sin is a form of disorderliness••
	• It is an inappropriate and unacceptable response to God's law

â€¢ ••God's mercy is involved in punishment••
	• God's mercy is a key attribute that shines forth in the ordaining of punishment

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••God's Justice••
	• God renders affliction to those who afflict others (2 Thessalonians 1:6)
	• This justice is called wrath, fury, judgment, and revenge (Romans 1:18, Ephesians 5:6, Deuteronomy 29:28, Hebrews 10:30)
• ••Mercy••
	• Mercy is God's punishment of sin that is less than its deserved punishment (Romans 2:5)
	• Mercy is clemency or beneficence (Lamentations 3:22)
• ••Clemency••
	• Clemency is God's moderation of punishments due to sin (Lamentations 3:22)
	• Clemency appears in patience and long-sufferance (2 Peter 3:9, Exodus 34:6)
• ••Patience and Long-Sufferance••
	• Patience is God's forbearance with sin and sparing of sinners (2 Peter 3:9)
	• Long-sufferance is God's suspension of revenge (Exodus 34:6)
• ••Beneficence••
	• Beneficence is God's pouring out of good things on sinners (Matthew 5:45)
• ••Punishment of Sin••
	• The punishment of sin is death (Genesis 2:17, Romans 5:12)
	• Death is a miserable privation of life (Romans 5:12)
• ••Death••
	• Death is not from God as a natural part of human nature, but as a result of sin (Romans 5:12)
	• Death is properly from sin as the meritorious cause of death (Romans 5:12)
• ••Degrees of Death••
	• There are two degrees of death: the beginning and the perfection
	• There are two members of death: the punishment of loss and the punishment of sense
	• There are two kinds of death: spiritual and corporal
• ••Spiritual Death••
	• The beginning of spiritual death is the defacing of the image of God (Romans 3:23)
	• Spiritual death involves the loss of grace and original justice (Romans 3:23)
• ••Corporal Death••
	• Corporal death is the physical consequence of sin (Genesis 2:17, Romans 5:12)
	• Corporal death involves the separation of the soul from the body (Genesis 2:17)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Spiritual Death••
	• Caused by the loss of God's grace
	• Results in the weakening of nature and a wounded state
	• Begins with spiritual bondage

â€¢ ••Spiritual Bondage••
	• Subjection to the power of darkness or spiritually deadly enemies
	• Caused by the Devil and his servants
	• Results in a loss of freedom and power to resist sin

â€¢ ••Bondage to the Devil••
	• Subjection to the Devil's power and command
	• Results in a loss of spiritual life and freedom
	• Can lead to death and misery

â€¢ ••Bondage to the World••
	• Subjection to the enticements and temptations of the world
	• Results in a focus on worldly desires and a lack of spiritual life
	• Can lead to sin and spiritual death

â€¢ ••Bondage to Sin••
	• Subjection to the power of sin and its desires
	• Results in a lack of power to resist sin and a focus on worldly desires
	• Can lead to spiritual death and misery

â€¢ ••Consequences of Spiritual Death••
	• Multiplying of sin in this present life
	• Punishment for the first sin, both in terms of its effects and consequences
	• Inward suffering and a pressing down of the soul

â€¢ ••Punishment for Sin••
	• Can be attributed to the effects or consequences of sin
	• Can be attributed to the inward suffering and pressing down of the soul
	• Can be attributed to the loss of righteousness and divine help

â€¢ ••The Role of the Devil••
	• Works in men to bring about spiritual death and bondage
	• Has command over death and can bring about spiritual death
	• Can blind the minds of men to the gospel and the glory of Christ

â€¢ ••The Role of the World••
	• Can entice and tempt men to sin and spiritual death
	• Can lead to a focus on worldly desires and a lack of spiritual life
	• Can result in a loss of freedom and power to resist sin

â€¢ ••The Nature of Spiritual Death••
	• Results in a loss of freedom and power to resist sin
	• Results in a focus on worldly desires and a lack of spiritual life
	• Can lead to spiritual death and misery.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••Chapter 13: Of Original Sin••

• ••1. Definition of Original Sin••
	• It is either Original or Actual sin (Thesis 1)
	• Original sin is a habitual exorbitance of the whole nature of man (Thesis 2)
• ••2. Characteristics of Original Sin••
	• It is a deviation from the Law of God (Thesis 2)
	• It is a corruption of the whole man (Thesis 3)
• ••3. Scriptural References to Original Sin••
	• Called the "old man" (Rom 6:6, Eph 4:22, Col 3:9)
	• Called the "body of Sin" (Rom 6:6, 7)
	• Called the "Law of the members" (Rom 7:23)
	• Called the "Flesh" (Joh 3:6, Rom 7:5, 18, 25)
• ••4. Extent of Original Sin••
	• Affects the understanding (Gen 6:5)
	• Affects the imagination and thoughts (Rom 8:5-7)
	• Affects the conscience (Tit 1:15)
	• Affects the will (Gen 8:21)
	• Affects the affections (Rom 1:24)
	• Affects the body and all its members (Rom 6:19)
• ••5. Nature of Original Sin••
	• It is a habitual privation of conformity to the Law of God (Thesis 5)
	• It is an exorbitance or deviation from the Law of God (Thesis 5)
• ••6. Scriptural References to Original Depravity••
	• Called Sin or that Sin (Rom 6:12, 7:7)
	• Called Hamartia (Rom 7:8, 20)
	• Called the Law of Sin (Rom 7:23)
	• Called Sin dwelling in us (Rom 7:17, 20, 21)
• ••7. Consequences of Original Sin••
	• Leads to condemnation (Rom 5:16)
	• Results in justification (Rom 5:16)
• ••8. Relationship between Original Sin and Actual Sin••
	• Actual sin is a consequence of Original Sin (Thesis 1)
• ••9. Scriptural References to the Corruption of Man••
	• The wickedness of man is great in the earth (Gen 6:5)
	• The thoughts of the heart are only evil continually (Gen 6:5)
• ••10. Conclusion••
	• Original Sin is a fundamental aspect of human nature (Thesis 1)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Carnal Mind••
	• Is opposed to God (Rom 8:7)
	• Is not subject to God's law (Rom 8:7)
	• Leads to death (Rom 8:6)

â€¢ ••The Spiritual Mind••
	• Is opposed to the carnal mind (Rom 8:6)
	• Leads to life and peace (Rom 8:6)

â€¢ ••Purity and Defilement••
	• The pure see all things as pure (Tit 1:15)
	• The defiled and unbelieving see nothing as pure (Tit 1:15)

â€¢ ••God's View of Humanity••
	• God smells a soothing aroma when humans obey (Gen 8:21)
	• God is displeased with humanity's evil imagination (Gen 8:21)

â€¢ ••Human Depravity••
	• Humans are morally perverse and impaired (Depravity)
	• Humans are slaves to uncleanness and lawlessness (Rom 6:19)

â€¢ ••Sin and the Law••
	• Sin is not the law, but rather the law reveals sin (Rom 7:7)
	• Sin takes opportunity through the law to produce evil desires (Rom 7:8)

â€¢ ••The Nature of Sin••
	• Sin is a disorder in man, with a formal and material part (Jer 2:13)
	• The formal part is an aversion from good (Rom 3:12)
	• The material part is turning to evil (Rom 7:23)

â€¢ ••Original Depravity••
	• Humans are depraved from birth (Rom 7:14)
	• Humans are captive to sin, even when they want to do good (Rom 7:14)

â€¢ ••Actual Sin••
	• Actual sin is an exorbitance of human action, deviating from God's law (1Joh 3:4)
	• Actual sin flows from original sin, like an act flows from a habit (Rom 7:14)

â€¢ ••The Relationship Between Original and Actual Sin••
	• Original sin is the foundation of actual sin (Rom 7:14)
	• Actual sins are tied together by their common origin in original sin (Jas 2:10)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Nature of Sin••
	• Sin dwells within individuals (Rom 7:20-21)
	• Sin is a law that is present with those who will to do good (Heb 12:1)
	• Sin is a slave that holds individuals captive (Joh 8:34, 2Pet 2:19)

â€¢ ••Original Sin••
	• Defined as an excess of man's whole nature, not just his actions (Chapter XIII)
	• The fomes peccati (sinful inclination) is a habitual, inordinate concupiscence of the sensitive appetite (Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologiae 3, 27, 3)

â€¢ ••Actual Sins••
	• Diversely distinguished among themselves
	• Distinguished by degree (Eze 5:6, 8, Luk 12:47-48)
	• Distinguished by respect to the person committing the sin (Num 12:14)
	• Distinguished by the kind and nature of the thing (Mat 5:21-22)
	• Distinguished by intent and remitting of the act (Phi 3:6, 1Tim 1:13)
	• Distinguished by the way and manner of committing it (Num 15:27, 30)
	• Distinguished by circumstances of place, time, and the like (Isa 26:10)

â€¢ ••Classification of Actual Sins••
	• Properly privative, depending on the difference in rectitude from which these acts decline
	• Distributed according to the Commandment of God

â€¢ ••Distinguishing Actual Sins••
	• In respect to their part (e.g., murder, adultery, etc.)
	• In respect to the circumstances of place, time, and the like (e.g., murder in a church, adultery in a public place)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Sin••
	• Sin is divided into two parts: Sin of Omission and Sin of Commission
	• Sin of Omission is turning from good, while Sin of Commission is turning to evil

â€¢ ••Sin of Omission••
	• Refers to not doing what ought to be done (e.g. Jas 4:17, Mat 25:42)
	• Is a sin of omission because it involves a failure to act

â€¢ ••Sin of Commission••
	• Refers to doing what ought not to be done (e.g. Eze 5:6, Mat 11:22-24)
	• Involves a positive action that goes against God's law

â€¢ ••Relationship between Sin of Omission and Sin of Commission••
	• Sin of Omission is most directly contrary to the command of God
	• Sin of Commission is most directly contrary to the forbidding of God

â€¢ ••Distribution of Sin••
	• Sin is not divided into kinds (e.g. covetousness)
	• Sin of Omission and Sin of Commission are not different kinds of sin

â€¢ ••Distribution of Sin by Subject••
	• Sin is divided into Sin of the heart, of the mouth, and of the work
	• Examples: Isa 29:13, Mat 5:28, 15:19

â€¢ ••Distribution of Sin by Object••
	• Sin is divided into Sin against God and Sin against men
	• Examples: Luk 15:18, 1Sam 2:25

â€¢ ••Distribution of Sin by Effect••
	• Sin is divided into Sin that destroys the conscience and Sin that does not
	• Sin is also divided into Sin that reigns and Sin that is mortified

â€¢ ••Consequences of Sin••
	• Sin leads to spiritual death, both in matter of loss and in matter of sense
	• Loss includes security of conscience and stupidity (deprivation of the sense of Sin and misery)

â€¢ ••Types of Spiritual Death••
	• Matter of loss: security of conscience and stupidity
	• Matter of sense: deprivation of the sense of Sin and misery

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••1. Habit of Sinning••
• Leads to a hardened will and mind (Jer 13:23)
• Brings about a "hard skin" over the conscience (Jer 13:23)

••2. Additions and Subtractions to God's Word••
• Forbidden in Deuteronomy 4:2 and 12:32
• Condemned in Revelation 22:18-19, with severe consequences

••3. Hypocrisy and False Worship••
• People may appear to worship God, but their hearts are far from Him (Isa 29:13-15)
• Even looking at someone with lust is considered adultery in the heart (Mat 5:28)

••4. Sin and Consequences••
• Sin against God is more serious than sin against man (1Sam 2:25)
• The conscience can become hardened through repeated sin (Heb 3:13)

••5. False Sense of Security••
• The conscience can become numb to danger or injury through repeated sin (Heb 3:13)
• This can lead to a false sense of being free from guilt or punishment

••6. Desperation and Terror of Conscience••
• The terror of conscience can be overwhelming, especially when faced with great guilt and punishment (Heb 10:26-27)
• This terror can arise from a sense of the unavoidableness of imminent punishment

••7. God's Moderation••
• God uses moderation in punishing sin, both internally and externally
• This moderation is a sign of God's mercy and love

••8. Reminders of God's Image••
• Even in spiritual death, there are remainders of God's image in the human soul (1Joh 3:9)
• These remainders appear in both the understanding and the will

••9. Principles of Truth••
• There are principles of truth that direct both theoretical and practical judgment (Rom 1:20; Psa 19:2-3)
• These principles are written in the hearts of all men (Rom 2:15)

••10. Natural Conscience••
• A natural conscience arises from these principles of truth (Rom 2:15)
• However, this conscience is often corrupt and dead, and can be defiled (Tit 1:15)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Nature of Sin••
	• Sin is a manifestation of spiritual death
	• It is a departure from God's will and nature
	• It is characterized by a show of godliness without true devotion

â€¢ ••The Restraining Power of Sin••
	• The will and understanding play a role in restraining excess sin
	• Sinners often abhor committing grosser sins
	• This is seen in the example of fornication not named among the Gentiles

â€¢ ••The Outward Moderation of Sin••
	• External means, such as politics and economics, can hinder the course of sin and misery
	• This can lead to a partial restraint of sin and its effects

â€¢ ••The Concept of Ethiopian••
	• Refers to a person with dark skin, especially from northern Africa
	• Literally means "Cushite" from the land of Cush (modern Sudan and Ethiopia)

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Willful Sin••
	• Sinning willfully after receiving knowledge of the truth leads to a loss of sacrifice for sins
	• This results in a fearful expectation of judgment and fiery indignation

â€¢ ••The Nature of Spiritual Birth••
	• Those born of God do not actively make sin
	• They are unable to continually sin because they have been born of God

â€¢ ••The Revelation of God's Attributes••
	• God's invisible attributes are clearly seen in creation
	• This knowledge is available to all, leaving no excuse for ignorance

â€¢ ••The Beginning of Bodily Death••
	• Can be inward or outward
	• Inward refers to the loss of internal good things, such as health and long life

â€¢ ••The Nature of Mortality••
	• Mortality is a dissolving and loosing of the bond between the soul and body
	• It is a state of being nearest to death

â€¢ ••The Outward Beginning of Bodily Death••
	• Refers to the loss of outward good things, such as dominion over creatures or poverty
	• This can lead to a loss of beauty or sustenance in life

â€¢ ••The Paralytic's Forgiveness••
	• Jesus tells the paralytic "Son, be of good cheer; your sins are forgiven you."

	• The punishment of death is eternal and never-ending (Mar 9:44, 46, 48)

â€¢ ••Consequences of Sin••
	• Sin leads to curses and punishments (Deu 28:20)
	• These can include loss of honor, barrenness, disease, and poverty (Deu 28:17-18, 25-48)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Second Coming••
	• The trumpet will sound, and the dead will be raised incorruptible (1 Corinthians 15:52)
	• We will be changed, and the Lord will descend from heaven with a shout and the voice of an archangel (1 Thessalonians 4:16)

â€¢ ••The Fate of the Wicked••
	• The cowardly, unbelieving, and wicked will have their part in the lake of fire and brimstone (Revelation 21:8)
	• They will experience torment, weeping, and gnashing of teeth (Luke 13:28)

â€¢ ••The Nature of Hell••
	• The Scripture does not describe the place of Hell or the manner of torture (Luke 16:23)
	• The focus is on the spiritual consequences of sin, rather than physical details

â€¢ ••The Propagation of Sin••
	• Sin is passed down from Adam to his descendants through natural descent (Job 14:4, Psalm 51:5, Romans 5:14, Ephesians 2:3)
	• This is a result of God's just ordination, and is seen in the natural world (e.g. inbred qualities, hereditary right)

â€¢ ••The Two Parts of Sin••
	• Imputation: the same singular act of disobedience as Adam's is imputed to us (Romans 5:12-19)
	• Real communication: the same kind of sin as Adam's is communicated to us through our nature (Romans 5:12-19)

â€¢ ••Original Sin as Punishment and Sin••
	• Original sin is a privation of original righteousness, which is a punishment (Romans 5:12-19)
	• It is also a sin, as it is a lack of what we should have (Romans 5:12-19)

â€¢ ••The Derivation of Sin••
	• Sin is derived from Adam by way of desert (as a punishment)
	• Sin is also derived by way of a real efficient (as a sin joined to it)

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Sin••
	• We are born with an unaptness or perverseness of our bodily faculties, opposite to God's rectitude (Romans 5:12-19)
	• This unaptness is a result of the privation of original righteousness

â€¢ ••The Inheritance of Sin••
	• We all inherit the same condition as Adam, and are subject to the same consequences (Romans 5:12-19)
	• This is seen in our natural desires and actions, which are opposed to God's will (Ephesians 2:3)

â€¢ ••The Hope of Redemption••
	• Through faith in Christ, we can be redeemed from the consequences of sin (Romans 5:12-19)
	• We can be restored to the original righteousness that we were created to have (Romans 5:12-19)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Nature of Sin••
	• Sin is a natural inclination to evil
	• It arises from the deprivation of righteousness
	• It leads to moral evil and errors in the mind

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Sin••
	• Sin leads to actual sin
	• The will is turned away from God and burns with love of self and evil desires
	• Sin propagates death, both spiritual and corporal, to all of Adam's posterity

â€¢ ••The Need for Salvation••
	• Faith is not just for life, but for salvation
	• God must give life to man who is dead in sin
	• This is a key difference between the questions of the rich young man and the jailer

â€¢ ••The Restoring of Man••
	• The restoring of man is lifting him up from sin and death to an estate of grace and life
	• The cause of this restoring is the merciful purpose of God

â€¢ ••The Two Parts of Redemption••
	• Redemption is the first act of restoring, the matter of salvation
	• Application is the second act, the form and efficiency of redemption

â€¢ ••The Prime Reason of Redemption••
	• The prime reason, rule, and measure of application is the gracious will of God
	• This will is the cause of redemption itself

â€¢ ••Redemption is Appointed to All••
	• Redemption is appointed to all and every one for whom God intended it to be obtained
	• This is according to the saying of Christ, "Whoever the Father gives me, shall come to me"

â€¢ ••The Price of Redemption••
	• Redemption is bringing man into freedom from the bondage of sin and the devil
	• This freedom was not primarily effected by power or prayers, but by the payment of a just price

â€¢ ••The Need for a Mediator••
	• The price of redemption could not be paid by man
	• A mediator was necessary to make a perfect reconciliation between God and man

â€¢ ••The Mediator••
	• The mediator is Christ, who has purchased the Church by his own blood
	• Christ's death is the payment of the just price for redemption

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Mediator••
	• The Mediator is Jesus Christ, who gave himself as a price for our redemption.
	• He is not given for one age only, but for all ages, past, present, and future.
• ••The Necessity of the Mediator••
	• The Mediation was equally necessary in all ages.
	• It was sufficient and effectual from the beginning, by virtue of God's decree, promise, and acceptance.
• ••The Uniqueness of the Mediator••
	• There is no other name under Heaven by which we must be saved, except Jesus Christ.
	• He is the only Mediator between God and men.
• ••The Fitness of the Mediator••
	• Jesus Christ's fitness to perform the works of redemption consists of two parts: his person and his office.
	• His person is the second person of the Deity, and his office is the office of Mediator.
• ••The Distinction of the Two Natures••
	• The two natures of Christ are the Divine nature and the human nature.
	• The Divine nature is the second person of the Deity, and the human nature is like our nature, except for sin and the manner of subsisting.
• ••The Personal Union••
	• The second person of the Deity took the human nature, that it might inseparably subsist in the same person.
	• This union is a personal union, not a mixture or confusion of the two natures.
• ••The Twofold Way of Subsisting••
	• The second person of the Deity has a twofold way of subsisting: one in the Divine nature for eternity, and another in the human nature after the incarnation.
	• This latter way of subsisting agrees with the Son of God in respect to the union with the human nature.
• ••The Change in the Human Nature••
	• The human nature is elevated to highest perfection by its union with the Divine person and nature.
	• It is made a proper adjunct of the Divine person, and a member of the same whole God-man.
• ••The Description of the Union••
	• The union between the two natures cannot be sufficiently explained by any one term or concept.
	• We use various terms to describe the union, including those that convey consent and unity.
• ••The Importance of the Union••
	• The union between the two natures is essential to the Mediator's role and function.
	• It is a fundamental aspect of Christ's nature and identity as the Mediator between God and men.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Mystical Union of Christ's Natures••
	• The union of Christ's divine and human natures is mystical and secret.
	• It cannot be fully expressed in human words and notions.
• ••Communication of Properties••
	• The divine and human natures communicate properties, but not in a real transfusion or donation.
	• This communication is a communion or concurring unto the same operations.
• ••Predication and Attribution••
	• Properties of one nature are attributed to the whole person or the other nature.
	• Examples: Christ is said to be dead (human nature), and to have been in the beginning (divine nature).
• ••The Person of Christ••
	• The communication of properties respects the person of Christ, not the natures considered in themselves.
	• This communication is not merely verbal, but real in the sense that it affects the operations of the natures.
• ••Examples and Analogies••
	• Examples of communication between matter and form, soul and body, and iron and fire do not apply to the union of Christ's natures.
	• These examples do not prove the position of communication between the divine and human natures.
• ••Christ's Understandings and Wills••
	• Christ had two understandings: one divine and one human.
	• Christ had two wills: one divine and one human, with a natural appetite.
• ••The Double Presence of Christ••
	• Christ has a double presence: divine and human.
	• The human presence is limited and cannot be everywhere or in many places at once.
• ••Faith and Christ's Office••
	• Our faith is carried towards Christ and by Christ towards God.
	• Christ's office is to obtain salvation for men.
• ••The Office of Christ••
	• The office of Christ is that which he undertook to obtain salvation for men.
	• This is a sure saying and worthy of all acceptance.
• ••The Purpose of Christ's Mission••
	• Those who deny that the purpose of Christ's mission was the salvation of men deprive God and Christ of their honor and men of their comfort.

â€¢ ••The Calling of Christ••

â€¢ ••Characteristics of the Calling••

â€¢ ••The Prophecy of Christ••

	• Then he entered into his kingdom

â€¢ ••The Importance of the Calling••

â€¢ ••The Nature of a Prophet••

â€¢ ••Christ as Altar••

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Office of a Prophet••
	• A prophet declares the will of God to men
	• This office can be performed by a mere man
	• However, purging sins by sacrifice and governing souls are unique to the offices of a priest and king

â€¢ ••The Office of a Priest••
	• A priest purges sins by sacrifice before God
	• This duty cannot be performed by a mere man
	• It requires a special office and authority

â€¢ ••The Office of a King••
	• A king governs souls and consciences of men
	• This duty cannot be performed by a mere man
	• It requires a special office and authority

â€¢ ••The Authority of Kings••
	• Kings of nations are not subordinate to Christ, but to God
	• Their authority is derived from God, not Christ

â€¢ ••Redemption••
	• There are two parts of redemption: humiliation and exaltation
	• Humiliation refers to Christ's subjecting himself to God's justice

â€¢ ••Humiliation••
	• Humiliation was not of Christ's divine nature, but of the Mediator God-man
	• It was a condition of being a servant that accompanied the divine nature
	• The divine nature suppressed its dignity during this time

â€¢ ••Satisfaction••
	• Satisfaction refers to the recompense for the injury done to God by our sins
	• It is the end of Christ's humiliation and is what takes away condemnation
	• It brings reconciliation unto salvation

â€¢ ••Satisfaction as Imputation of Sin••
	• Christ was made sin for us, but not by inward pollution or outward reputation
	• He was made sin by imputation, so that he could undergo punishment for our sin

â€¢ ••Satisfaction as Bearing of Iniquities••
	• Christ bore our iniquities, but not by bearing patience
	• He bore our sins in his body on the cross and took them away

â€¢ ••Satisfaction as Payment of Redemption••
	• Christ paid the price of redemption for us, which is a payment of silver or gold
	• This price was applied through the sprinkling of blood and the purging of evil consciences

Here are 12 concise bullets and sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Death of Christ••
	• Was an act of Christ, not a mere suffering
	• Was voluntary, not compelled
	• Was done out of power, not infirmity
	• Was done out of obedience to his Father and love for us
• ••Punishments Contained in the Death of Christ••
	• Equalled all the misery that the sins of men deserved
	• Included the greatest punishments, but without the accompanying circumstances of the damned
	• Was a satisfaction by overcoming, not a yielding to perdition
• ••Characteristics of the Death of Christ••
	• Was a cutting off, casting away, and curse
	• Was a heaping up of sorrows
	• Was a death that was swallowed up in victory
• ••Humiliation and Satisfaction••
	• The death of Christ was the consummation of all humiliation
	• The death of Christ represents all that satisfaction contained in his whole humiliation
• ••Kind and Proportion••
	• The death of Christ was the same in kind and proportion as the death due to the sins of men
	• Represented the same degrees, members, and kinds of punishment
• ••Spiritual Death of Christ••
	• Began with the loss of joy and delight in the enjoyment of God and the fullness of grace
	• Did not lose the principle and habit of joy, but only the act and sense of it
• ••Tasting the Wrath of God••
	• Was the beginning of spiritual death in matter of sense
	• Involved tasting the wrath of God and a subjection to the power of darkness
• ••Object of God's Anger••
	• The object of God's anger was Christ, not absolutely, but only as touching the punishment
	• Christ underwent the punishment as our surety
• ••Subjection to the Power of Darkness••
	• Was not to bondage, but to vexation, which Christ felt in his mind
• ••Effects on the Soul of Christ••
	• The soul of Christ was affected with sorrow, grief, fear, and horror in an agony
	• Was affected not only in the inferior part, but also in the whole soul

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Agony of Christ••
	• Caused by a sense of supernatural and spiritual death
	• Not just out of compassion for others, but out of a proper suffering that Christ underwent in our name
	• Not just out of a horror of temporal death, but out of a sense of spiritual death
• ••Effects of the Agony••
	• A vehement deprecation (prayer to avert evil) showing Christ's mind was astonished and his nature was flying from the bitterness of death
	• A watery sweat with clots of blood dropping down to the ground
• ••Moderation and Mitigation••
	• Inward moderation: Christ had spaces of time when the pressure and vexation in his soul slackened, allowing him to attend to his duties and will
	• Outward mitigation: an angel strengthened Christ in talking with him
• ••Bodily Death••
	• No inward beginning of bodily death besides natural mortality and weakening
	• External beginning was manifold, including loss and sense
• ••Loss••
	• Rejected by his own people
	• Counted worse than a murderer
	• Forsaken by his closest disciples
	• Denied and betrayed by all kinds of men
• ••Sense••
	• Shameful apprehending
	• Violent taking away
	• Unjust judgments
	• Being worked, whipped, and crucified with reproaches and injuries
• ••Mitigation of Death••
	• Manifestation of the Divine Majesty in working certain miracles
	• Operation of Divine providence, whereby Christ was justified by the Judge before he was condemned
• ••Consummation of Death••
	• In the highest degree of the punishment appointed
	• Death itself and its continuance
• ••Consummation of Spiritual Death••
	• Forsaking of the Father, where Christ was deprived of all sense of consolation
	• Curse, whereby Christ endured the full sense of God's judgment upon man's sin
• ••Consummation of Bodily Death••
	• Curse, whereby Christ endured the full sense of God's judgment upon man's sin
	• Death on the cross, where Christ was crucified and suffered the full punishment of sin

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Curse of the Law••
	• The curse of the law was not caused by Christ's crucifixion, but was a sign and symbol of it.
	• Christ redeemed us from the curse of the law by becoming a curse for us.

â€¢ ••Christ's Death••
	• Christ's death was a true and natural death, not feigned or supernatural.
	• It was voluntary, but also violent and caused by natural causes.
	• Christ kept his life and strength for as long as he willed, and then laid it down.

â€¢ ••The Continuance of Death••
	• The continuance of Christ's death was a state of lowest humiliation, not punishment or affliction.
	• Christ remained under the dominion of death for three days, which is often referred to as descending into Hell.

â€¢ ••The Exaltation of Christ••
	• The exaltation of Christ is his glorious triumph over his and our enemies.
	• He overcame death, sin, and the devil through his death and resurrection.

â€¢ ••The Perfection of Victory••
	• The perfection and manifestation of Christ's victory is in his exaltation.
	• There was a virtual triumph in his death and on the cross, but the actual triumph was in his exaltation.

â€¢ ••The Nature of Christ's Death••
	• Christ did not die of natural causes, but was killed or executed.
	• He had the power to lay down his life and take it up again.

â€¢ ••The State of Death••
	• Christ's death was a state of lowest humiliation, not punishment or affliction.
	• He remained under the dominion of death for three days.

â€¢ ••The Burial of Christ••
	• Christ's burial was a witness and representation of his state of death.
	• He was buried for three days, which is often referred to as a representation of his state.

â€¢ ••The Exaltation as Triumph••
	• The exaltation of Christ is a triumph over his and our enemies.
	• He overcame death, sin, and the devil through his death and resurrection.

â€¢ ••The Significance of Exaltation••
	• The exaltation of Christ is a manifestation of his victory and triumph.
	• It is a representation of his power and glory, and a witness to his triumph over death and sin.

Here are the 14 points summarized in 10 bullets with sub-bullets:

• ••Christ's Triumph••
	• Christ triumphed in the Cross as in a Field of Victory (bullet 4)
	• Christ triumphed as in the Kingly Seat and Chariot of Triumph (bullet 4)
• ••Exaltation••
	• Christ's glory was in changing from a humble form to a Heavenly blessedness (bullet 5)
	• Christ received a name above every name (Phil 2:9) (bullet 5)
• ••Human Nature••
	• Christ's human nature received all perfections a created nature could take (bullet 7)
	• Christ's body was adorned with purity, agility, splendor, and strength (Heb 12:2, Phil 3:21) (bullet 7)
• ••Glorified Body••
	• Christ's glorified body retained the essence and essential properties of a body (bullet 8)
	• Christ's body could not be everywhere, in many places together, or in the same place with another body (bullet 8)
• ••Degrees of Exaltation••
	• Christ's Resurrection was opposed to his death (bullet 9)
	• Christ's Ascension was opposed to his descent into the Grave and the lowest place of the Earth (bullet 9)
	• Christ's Sitting at the right Hand of God was opposed to his remaining in the Grave and in the state of death or in Hell (bullet 9)
• ••Resurrection••
	• Christ's Resurrection was of his whole human nature (bullet 10)
	• Christ's Resurrection was from Hell or the state and dominion of death (bullet 10)
• ••Testimony to Resurrection••
	• Angels testified to Christ's Resurrection (bullet 13)
	• Christ himself testified to his Resurrection through various appearances (bullet 13)
	• Men testified to Christ's Resurrection through seeing, hearing, and handling him (bullet 13)
• ••Christ's Power••
	• Christ rose by his own power, not by the power or leave of another (bullet 14)
	• Christ's operation was attributed to God the Father in Act 2:24 (bullet 14)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Resurrection of Jesus••
	• Occurred on the third day after his death and burial (Mark 16:2, Luke 24:7, Acts 10:40, 1 Corinthians 15:4)
	• Had several purposes:
		- To declare Jesus as the Son of God (Romans 1:4)
		- To seal a full victory over death (1 Corinthians 15:57)
		- To fulfill his office as the one who justifies (Romans 4:25)
		- To show himself as both justified and justifying others (1 Corinthians 15:17)
		- To be the substance, example, and entrance of our spiritual and corporal resurrection (1 Corinthians 15:20-23)

â€¢ ••The Cause of Resurrection••
	• Christ as God is the principal cause of our resurrection
	• As satisfying by his humiliation and death, he is the meritorious cause of it
	• As rising from the dead, he is the exemplary cause and demonstration of it

â€¢ ••The Ascension of Christ••
	• Was a middle degree or progress of exaltation, where Christ left the earth and ascended into the highest heaven (Acts 1:11, Ephesians 4:10)
	• Was of the whole person, but only figuratively agrees to the divine nature
	• Properly agrees to the human nature, as it allowed change from a lower place to a higher

â€¢ ••Time of Ascension••
	• Occurred 40 days after his resurrection (Acts 1:3)
	• Was delayed to confirm the disciples' faith and instruct them in the kingdom of God (Acts 1:3)

â€¢ ••Place of Ascension••
	• Was from Mount Olivet (Acts 1:12)
	• Was also the place of his deepest humiliation (Luke 22:39, 44)

â€¢ ••Place of Ascension (continued)••
	• Was the heaven of the blessed, not an ubiquitary heaven
	• Is the seat, house, or mansion of God (John 14:2)
	• Is the highest heaven above all others (Ephesians 4:10)

â€¢ ••Witnesses of Ascension••
	• Were both many men and angels (Acts 1:3, 10, 1 Corinthians 15:5)

â€¢ ••Order of Ascension••
	• Christ was the first of all those who ascended

â€¢ ••Christ's Presence on Earth••
	• His human nature is not with us on earth (Matthew 26:11)
	• However, he is everywhere with his people through his spiritual efficacy (Matthew 28:20)

â€¢ ••Christ's Glory••
	• His glory was manifested in his ascension
	• He had emptied himself of his divine rights when he descended into human nature (Philippians 2:7)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Ascension of Christ••
	• Was a cause for others to ascend (Heb 9:8, Col 1:20)
	• Was a result of the power of God, the same power that raised Christ from the dead
• ••The Cause of Ascension••
	• The power of God, which is the same both of the Father and the Son
	• Called an "Assumption" in respect to the Father and an "Ascension" in respect to the Son (Act 1:11)
• ••The Ends of Christ's Ascension••
	• To place his human nature in the mansion of glory (Joh 14:3)
	• To show himself as the one who could pierce into the Heavenly and deepest counsels of God (Joh 3:13)
	• To prepare mansions for all his people in the house of his Father (Joh 14:3)
	• To take possession of the heavenly Kingdom in the name of his own (Eph 2:6)
	• To take care for the things that were to be performed for the salvation of his people (Joh 16:7)
	• To provide a certain argument for the ascension of believers into Heaven (1Cor 15:20)
	• To enable believers to follow after Heavenly things in thought, affection, and conduct (Col 3:1, Phi 3:20)
• ••Scriptural References••
	• Matthew 26:11
	• 1 Corinthians 15:5-6
	• Hebrews 9:8
	• Colossians 1:20
	• Genesis 5:24
	• Hebrews 11:5
	• 2 Kings 2:11
	• Acts 1:11
• ••The Assumption of Christ••
	• The Greek and Latin Gospels use the term "assumption" for the ascension of Christ
	• The Latin Vulgate uses the term "assumptus" (Acts 1:2)
• ••The Exaltation of Christ••
	• Sitting at the right hand of God is the highest degree of his exaltation
	• He enjoys the highest glory of his mediation in this position

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Christ's Glory and Majesty••
	• Christ's sitting at the right hand of the Father is a unique glory and majesty
	• This glory is properly and formally a kingly glory (Act 2:36)

â€¢ ••Kingly Glory••
	• The kingly glory is a fullness of power and majesty whereby Christ governs all things for the good of his people (Psa 110:1, 1Cor 15:25)
	• This glory is a fulfillment of Christ's reign until he has put down all his enemies under his feet (1Cor 15:25)

â€¢ ••Christ's Majesty and Power••
	• Christ's majesty and power properly agree to the person of Christ the Mediator
	• The human nature of Christ has now so much eminence of dignity and ruledom that he is set above and over all created things (Eph 1:20)

â€¢ ••Limitations of Christ's Power••
	• Christ's human nature remains finite and created
	• Christ's power is not omnipotent or omnipresent in the same way as God's power

â€¢ ••Christ's Offices••
	• Christ exercises a kingly priesthood, pleading our cause by gloriously representing his death and resurrection (Heb 9:24)
	• Christ exercises a kingly prophecy, sending his ambassadors and working together with them (Mat 28:18-20, Mar 16:20)

â€¢ ••The Application of Christ's Redemption••
	• The application of Christ's redemption is the making of his work actually effectual in some certain men
	• This application is attributed to the Holy Spirit (1Cor 12:13)

â€¢ ••The Role of the Father••
	• The Father's decree and donation are necessary for the application of Christ's redemption
	• The Father has given some certain men to Christ to be redeemed and saved (Joh 6:36-37)

â€¢ ••The Role of Christ••
	• Christ's intention is necessary for the application of his redemption
	• Christ has determined his satisfaction for the good of those whom he has appointed to be redeemed (Joh 6:37)

â€¢ ••The Role of the Holy Spirit••
	• The Holy Spirit is the one who applies Christ's redemption to individuals
	• The Holy Spirit baptizes believers into one body (1Cor 12:13)

â€¢ ••The Effectiveness of Christ's Work••
	• Christ's work is effective in making believers holy and blameless (Eph 1:4)
	• Christ's work is effective in gathering his own out of the world and protecting, building up, and preserving them forever (Mat 28:18-20, Mar 16:20)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's Plan for Redemption••
	• God's plan for redemption was determined in the decree of God (Rom 5:10-11)
	• Christ's death was the means of reconciliation between God and humanity (2Cor 5:19)

â€¢ ••Christ's Satisfaction••
	• Christ's satisfaction was a certain and effective application of redemption to humanity (Joh 17:9, 11, 12, 19)
	• Christ's satisfaction was a representation of the redemption that would be applied to humanity (Joh 17:9, 11, 12, 19)

â€¢ ••The Role of the Holy Spirit••
	• The Holy Spirit is sent by both the Father and the Son to perform the application of redemption (Joh 14:16; 16:7)
	• The Holy Spirit is the means by which God communicates spiritual blessings to humanity (Eph 1:3, 5, 11)

â€¢ ••The Source of Spiritual Blessings••
	• All spiritual blessings come from God the Father (Jas 1:17)
	• Christ obtained redemption through his merit, and it is applied to humanity through Christ (Eph 1:3, 5, 11)

â€¢ ••The Connection between Impetration and Application••
	• Impetration (Christ's satisfaction) is the means by which application (redemption) is achieved
	• The end of impetration (redemption) is intended by God the Father and Christ

â€¢ ••The Latitude of Application••
	• Application is of the same latitude as redemption itself
	• Redemption is applied to all and only those for whom it was obtained by the intention of Christ and the Father

â€¢ ••The Intention of Application••
	• Christ satisfied only for those whom the Father gives to him (Joh 6:37)
	• The Father draws people to Christ, and Christ raises them up at the last day (Joh 6:44)

â€¢ ••The Sufficiency of Christ's Satisfaction••
	• Christ satisfied for all, but only those who are saved by him are included in his satisfaction
	• It is agreeable to charity to judge well of every individual, even though we may not know their fate collectively

â€¢ ••The Way of Application••
	• The way of application is called the New Covenant (Heb 8:8-10)
	• The New Covenant is a covenant of life, salvation, and grace (Rom 4:16; Gal 3:18)

â€¢ ••The Gospel as the Means of Application••
	• The Gospel is the means by which the New Covenant is applied to humanity (Rom 1:16)
	• The Gospel is the good news of salvation and redemption through Christ (Rom 1:16)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of the New Covenant••
	• Called the Word of God, worthy of all acceptance, and the Gospel of peace
	• Also referred to as the Covenant, Testament, and the Word of Reconciliation

â€¢ ••Nature of the New Covenant••
	• A firm promise, like a covenant, but also a free donation
	• Confirmed by the death of the giver, making it a testament

â€¢ ••Differences from the Old Covenant••
	• ••Kind••: Old Covenant was a covenant of friendship, while the New Covenant is a covenant of reconciliation between enemies

â€¢ ••Efficient Cause••
	• Only God covenants in the New Covenant, as man is dead in sin and unable to contract a spiritual covenant
	• Christ's sacrifice is infinite and sufficient to atone for all men, but it is applied only to the elect

â€¢ ••Hebrews 8:8-10••
	• Quotes from Hebrews 8:8-10, which describes the New Covenant as a new agreement with Israel and Judah
	• Highlights the differences between the old and new covenants

â€¢ ••Romans 4:16 and Galatians 3:18, 21, 22••
	• Quotes from Romans 4:16 and Galatians 3:18, 21, 22, which emphasize the promise of salvation by faith
	• Highlights the idea that the promise is for all the seed, not just those under the law

â€¢ ••Hebrews 9:16••
	• Quotes from Hebrews 9:16, which states that where there is a testament, there must also be the death of the testator

â€¢ ••Object of the New Covenant••
	• The New Covenant is extended to all men, but belongs to some certain ones in a special manner
	• Those who belong to it are called sons and heirs of the promise and salvation

â€¢ ••Beginning or Moving Cause••
	• The Old Covenant was based on God's wise and just counsel, while the New Covenant is based on God's love and mercy

â€¢ ••Purpose of the New Covenant••
	• To put God's laws in the minds and hearts of believers, and to make them His people
	• To reconcile believers to God and make them sons and heirs of the promise and salvation

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Covenant of Works vs. the Covenant of Grace••
	• The Covenant of Works was based on human ability and obedience
	• The Covenant of Grace is based on Christ Jesus and promises righteousness and life

â€¢ ••Key Differences between the Two Covenants••
	• Foundation: human ability vs. Christ Jesus
	• Promises: life only vs. righteousness and all means of life
	• Conditions: perfect obedience required vs. no conditions required, but faith is communicated by grace

â€¢ ••Effects of the Two Covenants••
	• The Covenant of Works teaches what is righteous, but does not bestow righteousness
	• The Covenant of Grace bestows righteousness and brings salvation to those who receive it

â€¢ ••Salvation through the Covenant of Grace••
	• It brings assured salvation to all those who receive it
	• It does not bring death or condemnation to anyone

â€¢ ••Continuance of the Covenant of Grace••
	• It is everlasting in respect to its substance and those who receive it
	• The grace of this covenant continues forever with those who are truly in covenant

â€¢ ••Predestination and the Covenant of Grace••
	• Predestination is the decree of God, manifesting his special glory in the eternal condition of men
	• Predestination is not an inward difference in the predestined, but rather a manifestation of God's glory

â€¢ ••The Application of Redemption••
	• The application of redemption is made to some certain men, not all
	• This shows a manifest difference between men in respect to the dispensation of grace

â€¢ ••The Nature of Predestination••
	• Predestination was from eternity and worked from the beginning of God's works
	• It puts nothing in the person that is predestined, but rather lies hidden in the one who predestines

â€¢ ••The Purpose of Predestination••
	• To manifest God's special glory in the eternal condition of men
	• To show God's wrath and power, as well as his riches of glory

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Predestination and the Covenant of Grace••
	• Predestination is the foundation for the Covenant of Grace
	• The Covenant of Grace is the means by which God's predestination is fulfilled in the lives of men

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Predestination••
	• Predestination is a certain determination of the order of means to the end
	• It is a decree that contains a definite sentence to be executed by certain counsel
	• It is also called a purpose and counsel, because it propounds an end to be attained

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Predestination••
	• It has the greatest wisdom, freedom, firmness, and immutability
	• These characteristics are found in all the decrees of God

â€¢ ••Reason for Predestination••
	• The reason for Predestination is unmovable and indissoluble
	• It is based on God's certain knowledge of who are his and the number of the predestined

â€¢ ••Nature of Predestination••
	• It does not necessarily presuppose its limit or object as existing
	• It makes it exist, so that by the force of predestination, it is ordered that it should be

â€¢ ••Cause of Predestination••
	• It depends on no cause, reason, or outward condition
	• It proceeds purely from the will of him that predestines

â€¢ ••Object of Predestination••
	• Men are the object of this decree
	• The difference of the decree does not depend on man, but rather follows upon the decree

â€¢ ••Foreknowledge and Predestination••
	• Foreknowledge is sometimes used to signify Predestination, but less properly so
	• Foreknowledge is an act of the will in God, which presupposes an act of the will

â€¢ ••Single Act of Will in God••
	• There is only one act of will in God, properly speaking
	• All things in God are together, with nothing before or after

â€¢ ••Purpose of Predestination••
	• It is an act of God's Will whereby it is exercised about a certain object
	• It determines to bring to a certain end by certain means

â€¢ ••Scriptural Basis for Predestination••
	• It is based on Scripture, including passages such as 1 Thessalonians 5:9, 2 Timothy 2:19, and Ephesians 1:5-9

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Decree of Predestination••
	• There is only one decree about the end and means of predestination.
	• God wills the end before the means in his order of intention.
• ••Means and Ends••
	• Some things are both means and ends, and also causes of other means.
	• These things are not causes of the act of predestination itself.
• ••Order of Predestination••
	• God's order of intention is to will the end before the means.
	• In order of execution, God wills the means first, before directing them to the end.
• ••Kinds of Predestination••
	• There are two kinds of predestination: Election and Reprobation.
	• Election is the predestination of some certain men to manifest God's glorious grace.
• ••Election••
	• Election is an act of God's will, which presents itself as diverse acts in our conception.
	• The first act of election is to will the glory of God's grace in the salvation of some men.
	• The second act of election is to appoint certain men to be made partakers of salvation.
	• The third act of election is to prepare and direct means to lead the elected to salvation.
• ••Reason for Election••
	• The proper reason for election is in the second act, which contains love, love for a supernatural good, and love by separating some from others.
• ••Predestination••
	• Predestination is a purpose or intention to prepare and direct means to lead the elected to salvation.
	• Predestination is sometimes distinguished from election, specifically as it respects the elect above.
• ••Knowledge and Election••
	• A certain knowledge accompanies the acts of will in election, whereby God knows the heirs of eternal life.
	• This knowledge is also called foreknowledge, and retains the distinct names of those to be saved.
• ••The Book of Life••
	• The knowledge of God is called the Book of Life, where the names of the saved and their good things are written.
• ••Single Election••
	• There was only one election in God respecting the salvation of men.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Election and Reprobation••
	• Election is the act of God choosing certain individuals for salvation
	• Reprobation is the act of God choosing certain individuals for damnation
• ••Scriptural References••
	• Romans 9:13, Jeremiah 31:3, Ephesians 5:25, John 17:6, 1 Corinthians 1:27-28
	• These passages highlight God's love and election of certain individuals
• ••Christ's Role in Election••
	• Christ is the Head of the elect, and individuals are elected as members in Him (Ephesians 1:4-5)
	• Christ is the cause of salvation, and individuals are the effects of His redemption
• ••Three Acts of Election••
	• The first act is the election of Christ as the Head
	• The second act is the election of individuals as members in Christ
	• The third act is the bestowal of spiritual blessings in Christ (Ephesians 1:3)
• ••Three Acts of Reprobation••
	• The first act is the will to set forth God's justice
	• The second act is the appointment of certain individuals to manifest God's justice
	• The third act is the rejection of those individuals from God's love and salvation
• ••Differences between Election and Reprobation••
	• Election is based on love and salvation, while reprobation is based on the denial of love and damnation
	• Election has a positive end (salvation), while reprobation has a negative end (damnation)
• ••God's Justice in Reprobation••
	• The end of God's justice in reprobation is to manifest His justice, not to destroy the creature
	• Reprobation is not the destruction of the creature, but the upholding of God's justice
• ••Love and Discernment in Election and Reprobation••
	• Election is based on love with discernment, while reprobation is based on the denial of love
	• Reprobation puts a difference between those who are elected and those who are reprobated
• ••Christ's Role in Reprobation••
	• Christ is not the cause of reprobation, but rather the means of salvation
	• Reprobation is not the effect of Christ's redemption, but rather the rejection of those who are not elected
• ••The Purpose of Reprobation••
	• Reprobation is not the destruction of the creature, but rather the manifestation of God's justice

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••Election and Reprobation••

• ••Election••
	• 1st Act: God wills the glory of His grace in the salvation of some men (Rom 9:22-23)
	• 3rd Act: God prepares and directs means to lead elected men to salvation (thesis 23)
• ••Reprobation••
	• Basis of difference: God's love for some and denial of love to others (thesis 35)
	• 2nd Act: God wills that some should be deprived of eternal life (thesis 35)
	• 3rd Act: God directs means to manifest Justice in the reprobate (Rom 9:18; 2Thes 2:11-12)

••Differences between Election and Reprobation••

• ••1st Difference••: Election bestows good immediately, while reprobation only denies good
• ••2nd Difference••: Reprobation is not a cause of damnation or sin, but an antecedent
• ••3rd Difference••: Election is a cause of salvation and all things considered a cause for salvation, while reprobation is not
• ••4th Difference••: The means of reprobation do not always have a cause-and-effect relationship with the outcome

••Calling••

• ••Union with Christ••: A spiritual relation of men to Christ that obtains the right to all blessings prepared in Him (1Joh 5:12; 1Joh 3:24)
• ••Calling••: A gathering of men together to Christ, that they may be united with Him (1Pet 2:4-5; Eph 4:12)
• ••Union with God the Father••: Follows from union with Christ (1Thes 1:1; 2:1-2.3)

Here are 15 concise bullets and sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••1. Election and Calling••
• Election is the first act of God's will, making a person chosen for salvation.
• Calling is the exercise of election in a person's life, making them aware of their election.

••2. Sin and Election••
• Sin is sufficient cause for God's justice, and it is not undeserved.
• God has mercy on whom He wills, and hardens whom He wills (Rom 9:18).

••3. Calling and Salvation••
• The calling of men does not depend on their dignity, honesty, industry, or endeavors, but on God's election and predestination.
• The Lord adds to the Church those who would be saved (Act 2:47).

••4. Parts of Calling••
• The two parts of calling are:
	• The offer of Christ
	• Receiving Christ

••5. The Offer of Christ••
• The offer of Christ is an objective propounding of Christ as a sufficient and necessary means to salvation.
• The offer is made through preaching and the Gospel (1 Cor 1:23-24).

••6. The Nature of the Offer••
• The offer of Christ should not include anything that is not plainly and absolutely true.
• This is because the Gospel is the word of truth (Eph 1:13).

••7. Types of Offer••
• The offer of Christ can be:
	• Outward: a propounding or preaching of the Gospel
	• Inward: a spiritual enlightening, whereby the promises are propounded to the hearts of men

••8. Outward Offer••
• The outward offer is a propounding or preaching of the Gospel or promises of Christ.
• The application of the Law often precedes the outward offer to reveal sin and the inexcusableness of the sinner.

••9. Propriety of the Offer••
• The promises of Christ are propounded to all without difference, but they belong only to the elect.
• The elect are called the sons and heirs of the promise (Rom 9:8).

••10. Inward Offer••
• The inward offer is a spiritual enlightening, whereby the promises are propounded to the hearts of men.
• This is granted to those who are not elected in a limited manner (Heb 6:4; 10:29; Mat 13:20).

••11. Limited Inward Offer••
• The inward offer is sometimes granted to those who are not elected, but it is not a guarantee of salvation.

••12. Purpose of the Inward Offer••
• The purpose of the inward offer is to enlighten the minds of men, that they may know the hope of their calling (Eph 1:17).

••13. Response to the Offer••
• Those who receive Christ are given the right to become children of God (Joh 1:12).

••14. Importance of the Offer••
• The offer of Christ is essential for salvation, and it is not something that should be taken lightly.

••15. Conclusion••
• The calling of men is a sovereign act of God, and it is not dependent on human endeavors or dignity.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Elicited Act••
	• An act called or drawn forth from someone who might not otherwise have acted
	• A free act of the will, a chosen act of the mind, but incited or provoked from outside
	• Presupposes an ability to choose and act

â€¢ ••Faith and Repentance••
	• True faith leads to repentance naturally and expectedly
	• Repentance is not true if it does not turn a man from all known sin to every known good
	• Repentance continues and is renewed as often as needed from conversion to the end of life

â€¢ ••Repentance and Faith Order••
	• Repentance is usually perceived before faith
	• A sinner cannot easily persuade himself of reconciliation with God in Christ before feeling himself to have forsaken sin

â€¢ ••Justification••
	• Communion of blessings flowing from Union with Christ
	• Makes believers partakers of all things needed to live well and blessedly with God

â€¢ ••Change of State••
	• A translation and change of condition from sin and death to righteousness and eternal life
	• A twofold change: relative (God's imputation) and absolute (real)

â€¢ ••Relative Change of State••
	• Consists in God's imputation (e.g., Rom 4:5, 2 Cor 5:19)
	• Allows for no degrees of justification, but is perfect in one act

â€¢ ••Justification as a Sentence••
	• A gracious sentence of God, whereby for Christ's sake, God absolves the believer from sin and death
	• Accounts the believer righteous unto life (Rom 3:22, 24)

â€¢ ••Nature of Justification••
	• A judicial or moral change, not a physical or real change
	• Consists in pronouncing sentence and restoring reputation (Pro 17:15, Rom 8:33)

â€¢ ••Error in Understanding Justification••
	• Thomas Aquinas and followers err in making justification a physical motion or real transmutation

â€¢ ••Degrees of Justification••
	• Although justification is perfect in one act, its manifestation, sense, and effects have different degrees
	• Pertains to justification and adoption

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Justification••
	• Inherent righteousness is claimed to be partly a remission of sin and partly the infusion of righteousness.
	• This inherent righteousness is what justifies the believer by God's grace.

â€¢ ••Perfection in this Life••
	• Virtually means incompletely; there is no perfection in this life, except as it is imputed from Christ.
	• Perfection is not achieved in this life, but is rather imputed from Christ.

â€¢ ••Thomas Aquinas' View••
	• Thomas Aquinas claimed that at conversion, there is a renovation of the soul, and righteousness is infused in the believer by God.
	• This inherent righteousness is what justifies the believer by God's grace.

â€¢ ••Decree of Justification••
	• The decree of justification was conceived in the mind of God.
	• It was pronounced in Christ, and is now virtually pronounced upon believers.

â€¢ ••Justification through Faith••
	• Justification is pronounced upon believers through faith.
	• The love of God is shed abroad in our hearts by the Holy Spirit, witnessing to our spirits our reconciliation with God.

â€¢ ••Gracious Sentence••
	• Justification is a gracious sentence, not given by the justice of God, but by his grace.
	• It is not properly given by the justice of God, but by his grace.

â€¢ ••Righteousness of Christ••
	• The righteousness of Christ is imputed to believers in justification.
	• This righteousness is the obedience of Christ, which is the righteousness in respect to which the grace of God justifies us.

â€¢ ••Justification for Christ's Sake••
	• Justification is for Christ's sake.
	• The righteousness of Christ is the righteousness in respect to which the grace of God justifies us.

â€¢ ••Righteousness of God••
	• The righteousness of God is the righteousness of Christ, ordained by God to that end.
	• It is approved and confirmed by God's grace, so that sinners can stand before him through this righteousness.

â€¢ ••Justification through Faith••
	• Justification is through faith, not absolutely considered, but as apprehended by our faith.
	• The righteousness of Christ is apprehended by faith, and justification follows.

â€¢ ••Justification through one Man's act••

â€¢ ••Object of justifying faith••

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Righteousness and Justification••
	• Righteousness is deserved or merited, but justification is a gift of God's grace.
	• Justification is not the same as righteousness, but rather a declaration of being right with God.

â€¢ ••Absence of Sin••
	• A total absence of sin does not necessarily mean there is righteousness.
	• Righteousness is not just the absence of sin, but a positive declaration of being right with God.

â€¢ ••Biblical Examples••
	• Leviticus 20:4: God's people are to condemn those who worship Molech, even if they hide their eyes.
	• Numbers 23:20-21: God has blessed Israel and will not observe iniquity in them.
	• John 5:24: Those who hear and believe in Jesus have everlasting life and will not come into judgment.

â€¢ ••Christ's Righteousness••
	• Christ's righteousness is not just about His purity, birth, and life, but also about His obedience and remission of sins.
	• Christ's disobedience in Adam has robbed us of original righteousness and made us subject to condemnation.

â€¢ ••Adoption••
	• Adoption is the gracious sentence of God that accepts believers as His children.
	• It is a declaration of God's love and acceptance of us as His sons.

â€¢ ••Degrees of Adoption••
	• Adoption is pronounced with the same difference in degrees as justification.
	• It was first in God's predestination, then in Christ, and finally in believers themselves.

â€¢ ••Faith and Adoption••
	• Adoption is properly said only of the faithful who are called and justified.
	• Faith is the foundation of adoption, and it is obtained through the righteousness of faith.

â€¢ ••Relationship between Adoption and Justification••
	• Adoption follows upon faith, but it does not immediately follow justification.
	• Justification is a necessary foundation for adoption, and it is obtained through the reconciliation found in justification.

â€¢ ••Double Title to Heaven••
	• The faithful expect heaven by a double title: redemption and sonship.
	• Redemption is the foundation of this right, and adoption adds a certain manner of excellence and dignity to it.

â€¢ ••Understanding Adoption••
	• Adoption is a gracious sentence of God that manifests His will towards men.
	• It is a declaration of God's love and acceptance of us as His sons, and it adds a certain manner of excellence and dignity to our redemption.

• ••The Righteous Acts of the Saints••

	• The law has positive requirements in addition to negative prohibitions (1)

	• Divine adoption communicates a new inward life, whereas human adoption is only an extrinsic denomination (4)

	• Human adoption was brought in for lack of a natural Son, whereas divine adoption is out of an abundant goodness (15)
	• Divine adoption communicates a likeness of a natural Son and a mystical conjunction with Christ (15)
• ••The Dignity of Divine Adoption••
	• The dignity of divine adoption far exceeds the common relation of God as the Father of every creature (16)
	• It also exceeds the relation we had before the Fall, which was weak (16)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Definition of Sanctification••
	• Refers to the change of a man from sin to holiness
	• Involves the removal of filthiness, corruption, or the blot of sin
• ••Types of Sanctification••
	• Objective (in God's sight): completely and finally sanctified in Christ
	• Subjective (in our experience): progressively sanctified by the Spirit
• ••Sanctification and Justification••
	• Justification is the declaration of righteousness, while sanctification is the manifestation of that righteousness
	• Sanctification is a resulting or responsive righteousness
• ••Sanctification and the Whole Man••
	• Sanctification is of the whole man, not just one part of him
	• The soul is the primary part of the man that is sanctified, and then the body follows
• ••Degrees of Sanctification••
	• There are two degrees of sanctification: infancy (in this life) and maturity (in the next life)
	• Infancy is characterized by a variety of sanctification, while maturity is characterized by a more complete and perfect sanctification
• ••Sanctification and the Image of God••
	• Sanctification involves the creation of the image of God in man
	• The image of God is characterized by knowledge, righteousness, and holiness
• ••Sanctification and the Law of God••
	• Sanctification involves conformity to the Law of God
	• The Law of God is characterized by newness of life and the new creature
• ••Sanctification and the Divine Nature••
	• Sanctification involves the communication of the Divine Nature to man
	• The Divine Nature is characterized by purity and holiness
• ••Sanctification and the Removal of Sin••
	• Sanctification involves the removal of filthiness, corruption, or the blot of sin
	• The removal of sin is a necessary condition for sanctification
• ••Sanctification and the New Creature••
	• Sanctification involves the creation of the new creature
	• The new creature is characterized by newness of life and the new creature

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Sanctification••
	• Refers to the process of putting off the old, sinful self and putting on the new, godly self (Col 3:9-10)
	• Requires a change in heart and mind, where the old is discarded and the new is adopted
• ••Putting off the old••
	• Involves mortifying (killing) the old, sinful self (Col 3:3-5)
	• Requires the death of Christ as the meritorious cause (Rom 6:5-6)
	• The Spirit of God communicates the efficacy of Christ's death to the faithful (Rom 8:13)
	• Faith is the administering cause of mortification (Rom 6:17)
• ••Putting on the new••
	• Involves vivification (restoring the image of God in man) (Col 3:10)
	• Requires the resurrection of Christ as the exemplary cause (Col 3:1)
	• The Spirit of God, which raised Christ from the dead, is the principal cause at work (Rom 8:11)
• ••Sanctification in two parts••
	• Mortification: the process of killing the old, sinful self (Col 3:3-5)
	• Vivification: the process of restoring the image of God in man (Col 3:10)
• ••Characteristics of mortification••
	• Denial of oneself and the world (Luk 9:23; Gal 6:14)
	• Sin is broken, subdued, and mortified in the faithful (Luk 9:23; Gal 6:14)
• ••Characteristics of vivification••
	• Restoration of the image of God in man (Col 3:10)
	• Transformation of the mind through the renewing of the mind (Rom 12:2)
• ••Growth in sanctification••
	• Requires growth and maturity in the faith (Heb 5:13-14)
	• Involves exercising one's senses to discern good and evil (Heb 5:14)
• ••Sanctification in this life and the life to come••
	• In this life, sanctification is a process of growth and maturity (Eph 4:15)
	• In the life to come, sanctification ceases, and only holiness remains (Heb 5:13-14)
• ••The role of faith in sanctification••
	• Faith is the administering cause of mortification (Rom 6:17)
	• Faith is necessary for growth and maturity in the faith (Heb 5:13-14)
• ••The role of the Spirit in sanctification••
	• The Spirit of God communicates the efficacy of Christ's death to the faithful (Rom 8:13)
	• The Spirit of God raises Christ from the dead and dwells in believers (Rom 8:11)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The struggle between flesh and Spirit••
	• The flesh and Spirit are in constant conflict (Gal 5:17)
	• The flesh leads to death, while the Spirit leads to life and peace (Rom 8:5-6)

â€¢ ••Mortification of sin••
	• Sin must be put to death through the Spirit (Rom 8:13)
	• John Owen said, "Either be killing sin, or sin will be killing you"

â€¢ ••Following Christ••
	• To follow Christ, one must deny themselves and take up their cross daily (Luk 9:23)
	• The cross of Christ is the means of crucifying the world and sin (Gal 6:14)

â€¢ ••Renewal and transformation••
	• The new man is renewed in knowledge and created in the image of God (Col 3:10, Eph 4:24)
	• The mind must be transformed to prove the will of God (Rom 12:2)

â€¢ ••Faith as the administering cause••
	• Faith is the means by which we live in the flesh (Gal 2:20)
	• Faith in the Son of God is the source of life and vitality

â€¢ ••Sanctification and sin••
	• Sanctification is imperfect in this life, and all believers are informed with both sin and grace (1Joh 1:8)
	• The perfection of sanctification is not found in this life, except in fantasy

â€¢ ••The old and new man••
	• The old man is the corrupt part that remains in the sanctified (Rom 7:5)
	• The new man is the renewed part, created in the image of God (Col 3:10, Eph 4:24)

â€¢ ••The spiritual war••
	• The flesh lusts against the Spirit, and the Spirit against the flesh (Gal 5:17)
	• This conflict leads to a daily renewing of repentance

â€¢ ••The flesh in the regenerate••
	• The flesh remains in the regenerate, including the will and reason (1Thes 5:23)
	• The flesh is the true and proper reason for sin in the regenerate

â€¢ ••The defilement of good works••
	• Even the best works of the saints are infected with corruption (Rom 7:5)
	• This defilement does not keep them from being accepted by God and rewarded

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Sanctification and Glorification••
	• Sanctification is one part of the alteration of qualities that respect good, which is just and honest.
	• Glorification is the other part, which respects good that is profitable and honorable.
• ••Glorification••
	• Glorification is a real transmutation of a man from misery to eternal happiness (Rom 8:30).
	• It is called a real transmutation to distinguish it from blessedness that is virtual or declarative.
• ••Redemption••
	• Glorification is also called redemption, which is a real delivering from the evils of punishment.
	• Redemption is the execution of the sentence of Justification, making life that was judged and pronounced ours, actually and by possession.
• ••Types of Redemption••
	• There are two types of redemption: real and virtual.
	• Real redemption is the execution of the sentence of Justification, while virtual redemption is the payment of the price of redemption and its application to justification.
• ••Scriptural Terms for Glorification••
	• Glorification is also called deliverance and preservation from the wrath of God, death, and the kingdom of darkness.
	• It is also called beatification, blessing, eternal life, glory, and an immortal inheritance.

• ••The Church is Mystically Called Christ••

	• The Church is compared to a bride, and Christ is compared to the bridegroom

• ••Union and Communion••
	• The comparisons between the Church and Christ signify the union and communion between them
	• Christ is the beginning of all dignity, life, power, and perfection to the Church
• ••The Church is Mystically One••
	• The Church is one, but not in a general sense
	• The Church is a specialized species, an individual or single member of a species
• ••The Church is Called Catholic••
	• The Church is called catholic because it contains the faithful of all nations, places, and times
	• The Church is not called catholic because it is a general thing, but because it is universal
• ••The Church is Divided into Members••
	• The Church is divided into members according to the degrees of communion with Christ
	• The Church is called either militant or triumphant
• ••The Church Militant••
	• The Church militant is that which partakes only of communion begun
	• The Church militant wrestles with enemies in the field of this world
• ••The Church Triumphant••
	• The Church triumphant is that which is already perfected
	• The Church triumphant is already in the measure of the full stature of Christ
• ••Invisibility and Visibility••
	• The Church is both invisible and visible
	• Invisibility is an affection or manner of the Church in respect to its essential and internal form
	• Visibility is an affection or manner of the Church in respect to its accidental and outward form
• ••The Essential and Accidental Forms••
	• The essential form of the Church is invisible because it is a relation that does not enter the senses
	• The accidental form of the Church is visible because it is an outward profession of inward faith

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of the Church••
	• The Church is a community of believers that presents itself to God in Christ to receive blessings and give glory to him.
	• The Church is also a community of believers that cares for each other and furthers their communion with God.

â€¢ ••Acts of Communion••
	• Acts of communion with Christ include visible acts of worship and devotion.
	• Acts of communion among believers include caring for each other and promoting their communion with God.

â€¢ ••Church Visibility••
	• The Church is visible in two ways: as a whole and in its parts.
	• The Church is visible in its parts through individual members and congregations.

â€¢ ••Manifest and Hidden Church••
	• The Church can be manifest (visible and public) or hidden (less visible and less public).
	• The manifest Church is characterized by a larger number of members and a more public profession of faith.

â€¢ ••Church Purity••
	• The Church can be pure or impure depending on the perfection of its profession.
	• The Church's profession is not limited to confession and preaching, but also includes receiving and obeying the Word.

â€¢ ••Church Continuity••
	• The Church has never totally failed since its beginning and will not fail until the end of the world.
	• The Church must always exist in the midst of its enemies until Christ makes them his footstool.

â€¢ ••Church Visibility in Parts••
	• The Church is visible in its parts through individual members and congregations.
	• These congregations are parts of the larger Catholic Church and share its name and nature.

â€¢ ••Particular Churches••
	• A particular Church is a species of the Church in general and a member of the Catholic Church.
	• A particular Church is a society of believers joined together by a special bond for the exercise of communion among themselves.

â€¢ ••Faith and Holiness••
	• True faith is joined with holiness and is the inward and real nature of the Church.
	• Faith is the thing that makes a Church visible in profession and mystical in nature.

â€¢ ••Congregations and the Church••
	• Congregations are parts of the Church and share its name and nature.
	• Congregations are visible churches that make up the larger Church.

• ••The Nature of the Church••
