Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Church and its Importance••
	• The Church is the body of Christ, established by God's will and ordinances.
	• The Church observes Christ's ordinances in order and decency, with the greatest fruit of edification.
• ••Promises of God in the Church••
	• God's promises of presence and blessing are made to the Church through Christ's ordinances.
	• The Church is promised the presence of Christ (Mat 18:20, 1Cor 5:4).
	• The Church is also promised the presence of the Holy Spirit (Isa 59:21).
• ••The Church's Blessings••
	• A more ample and certain blessing of God is expected in the Church than in solitary life.
	• The Church is the means by which God's blessings are conferred on believers.
• ••Neglecting the Church••
	• Those who neglect the Church sin against God and their own souls.
	• Neglecting the Church is a grievous sin, especially for those who have the opportunity to join.
• ••The Church's Government••
	• The Church has a form of government, with elders and leaders appointed by God.
	• These leaders are to be counted worthy of double honor (1Tim 5:17).
• ••The Church's Profession of Faith••
	• The profession of the true faith is the most essential note of the Church.
	• This profession may precede the solemn preaching of the Word and the administration of the Sacraments.
• ••The Manner of Applying Redemption••
	• The manner of applying redemption consists in means that prepare a person to receive God's grace.
	• The Spirit itself applies all saving things to us internally and immediately.
• ••The Means of Applying Redemption••
	• The two principal means of applying redemption are the Ministry and the Holy Signs.
	• Ecclesiastical Discipline is necessary to join these means together.
• ••The Ministry••
	• The Ministry is an Ecclesiastical function whereby a man is appointed to serve the Church.
	• The Ministry is a means of applying redemption, but it does not confer grace by itself.
• ••The Holy Signs••
	• The Holy Signs are a means of applying redemption, but they do not confer grace by themselves.
	• The Holy Signs are necessary to join the Ministry and Ecclesiastical Discipline together.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Ministry of the Church••
	• A special right to dispense holy things (2Cor 4.1)
	• A power of doing only by the command of Christ and obedience to him (1Cor 4.1-2.6)
• ••Characteristics of the Ministry••
	• Not a power of government, but a spiritual or regal power of Christ alone
	• Not a power of doing anything, but a power of right (Potestas jurie)
• ••The Nature of the Ministry••
	• A special right that respects special duties and undertakes common duties in a special manner
	• Depends on calling (Heb 5.4)
• ••Calling and Fitness••
	• A calling is an action whereby an office is committed to someone with the authority to minister
	• Fitness for the Ministry is a necessary adjunct of a calling
	• Fitness arises from a fit measure of gifts and a ready will to undertake and execute the office
• ••The State of the Church••
	• The Church has three states: essential (by faith), integral (by combination), and organic (by the Ministry)
	• The Ministry gives the Church the ability to exercise operations that pertain to the good of the whole
• ••Ecclesiastical Polity••
	• The course and direction of the Church's operations is governed by Ecclesiastical polity
	• The form of this polity is monarchical in respect to Christ, but mixed in its visible and vicarious administration
• ••Ministers and Vicars••
	• A Minister who appoints vicars is not ministering by God's ordaining, but by human ambition and presumption
	• A Minister who is a constant Minister of different churches is not fulfilling their office by God's ordaining
• ••The Power of the Ministry••
	• The power of the Ministry is not absolute, but relative
	• It is a power of right, not a power to do anything
• ••Ordination and Authority••
	• Those who ordain Ministers but do not give them power to preach the Word are ridiculous
	• A Minister's authority comes from their calling, not from a new grant of authority
• ••The Role of the Minister••
	• A Minister is a servant of Christ and a steward of the mysteries of God (1Cor 4.1)
	• A Minister is bound to execute their office by themselves, without appointing vicars.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Ministry of the Church••
	• Is a holy service, not a hierarchical principality
	• All ministers depend directly on Christ, not on each other

â€¢ ••Ministerial Authority••
	• A minister's authority comes from Christ, not from other humans
	• This is similar to how angels are subject to God, not to other angels

â€¢ ••Types of Ministry••
	• There are two types of ministry: ordinary and extraordinary
	• Ordinary ministry is what most ministers perform in their daily duties

â€¢ ••Extraordinary Ministry••
	• This type of ministry has a higher and more perfect direction than ordinary means
	• Extraordinary ministers have gifts and assistance from God to minister without error

â€¢ ••Calling to Extraordinary Ministry••
	• The calling to extraordinary ministry is immediate and direct from God
	• This excludes all ministry that is not infallible in its direction

â€¢ ••Examples of Extraordinary Ministry••
	• Elisha and Matthias were called to extraordinary ministry
	• This type of ministry was necessary for the Church to reveal God's will

â€¢ ••Means of Revelation••
	• God reveals his will to extraordinary ministers through various means, including:
		- A lively voice
		- Vision
		- Dreams
		- Special familiarity with God

â€¢ ••Power of Revelation••
	• The manner of this revelation is so powerful that it can draw men into an ecstasy or trance
	• This allows them to perceive only what is being propounded, without distraction

â€¢ ••Purpose of Extraordinary Ministry••
	• This type of ministry is necessary for the Church to reveal God's will
	• It requires men who are stirred up and sent by God to manifest his will to others

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Extraordinary Ministers••
	• They are called directly by God and have gifts and assistance from him
	• They are able to minister without error and are a substitute for God himself in the eyes of others

	• Miracles do not necessarily confirm the doctrine of a person, even if they seem to be confirmed with miracles

â€¢ ••The Importance of Doctrine••
	• Doctrine that does not agree with the known will of God should not be admitted, even if it seems to be confirmed with miracles
	• The Gospel should not be preached by anyone, even an angel, if it is different from the original Gospel

â€¢ ••Extraordinary Ministers••
	• Extraordinary Ministers were Prophets, Apostles, and Evangelists
	• They were called to perform extraordinary tasks, such as restoring the Church

â€¢ ••The Ministry of Conservation and Restoration••
	• The Ministry of conserving and restoring the Church does not always require a testimony of miracles
	• Many in the Old Testament and John the Baptist were examples of this type of Ministry

â€¢ ••Wycliff, Luther, and Zwinglius••
	• They were not extraordinary Ministers, but were called to restore the Gospel
	• They performed extraordinary tasks, but did not require a testimony of miracles to confirm their doctrine

â€¢ ••The Role of Miracles••
	• Miracles are not necessary to confirm the doctrine of all extraordinary Ministers
	• The doctrine of a person should be judged based on its agreement with the known will of God, not on the presence of miracles

â€¢ ••The Importance of Order••
	• When order is disturbed and decayed, extraordinary measures may be necessary
	• This does not mean that miracles are required to confirm the doctrine of a person.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Role of Extraordinary Ministers••
	• They were raised up by God to instruct the Church through Divine writings.
	• They were free from error in committing the rule of Faith and Practice to writing.
• ••The Command to Write••
	• They received a command from God to write, both externally and internally.
	• They were inspired by the Holy Spirit to write.
• ••The Inspiration of Scripture••
	• Scripture is inspired by God (2Tim 2:16).
	• Holy men spoke as they were moved by the Holy Spirit (2Pet 1:21).
• ••The Variety of Divine Inspiration••
	• Some things were unknown to the writer before writing.
	• Some things were known to the writer before, and some were known through natural knowledge and supernatural knowledge.
• ••The Role of Divine Inspiration••
	• Divine inspiration performed all things that were hidden and unknown.
	• In things that were known, there was also added a religious study, assisted by God.
• ••The Authorship of Scripture••
	• The Scripture is often attributed to the Holy Spirit as the author.
	• The Scribes are not mentioned as authors.
• ••The Authority of Scripture••
	• A book is not considered holy writ unless it is publicly given to the Church by divine authority.
	• A book must be sanctified to be a Canon or rule of the Church.
• ••The Importance of Public Recognition••
	• Public recognition by the Church is necessary for a book to be considered holy writ.
	• A book must be publicly given to the Church by divine authority.
• ••The Difference between Inspired and Authentic Writings••
	• Inspired writings are those that are written by the Holy Spirit.
	• Authentic writings are those that are written by extraordinary servants of God, but may not be inspired.
• ••The Canon of Scripture••
	• The Canon of Scripture is the collection of writings that are considered holy writ.
	• The Canon is established through divine authority and public recognition by the Church.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Scripture is the revealed will of God••
	• It is the rule of Faith and Practice
	• It is the doctrine revealed from God

â€¢ ••The Scripture is the same as the doctrine of life revealed from God••
	• It was spoken about in the first disputation
	• It is properly called the Holy Scripture

â€¢ ••The Scripture came before the Church in terms of its subject and object••
	• It is the true and saving will of God
	• It came after the first Church in terms of its manner of writing

â€¢ ••The Scripture is called Holy and is attributed to holy writers••
	• The writers are called holy in respect to the matter and object of the Scripture
	• They are also called holy in respect to the direction in which it was committed to writing

â€¢ ••The Scripture is intended for all faithful people through all ages••
	• It was written for some special occasion, but intended for all
	• It is directed to all faithful people, regardless of time or place

â€¢ ••The Scripture contains all things necessary for salvation and the instruction of the Church••
	• It contains all things necessary for salvation
	• It contains all things necessary for the instruction and edification of the Church

â€¢ ••The Scripture is a perfect rule of Faith and Practice••
	• It is not a partial rule
	• It contains all things necessary for the Church of God

â€¢ ••Not all things were committed to writing at once••
	• The state of the Church and the wisdom of God required otherwise
	• Things were committed to writing successively, as necessary in each age

â€¢ ••The Articles of Faith did not increase in essence over time••
	• They only increased in explication
	• Their essence remained the same, but their explanation evolved

â€¢ ••The Scripture explains the will of God in a particular manner••
	• It uses narrations, examples, precepts, exhortations, admonitions, and promises
	• This manner makes the will of God most clear and understandable

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Purpose of Scripture••
	• The common use of Scripture affects the will and stirs up godly motions
	• The will of God is revealed in Scripture in a clear and perspicuous manner
	• Scripture gives light to itself, which is to be diligently drawn out by men

â€¢ ••Interpretation of Scripture••
	• There is only one sense of one place of Scripture
	• Other interpretations would make the sense of Scripture unclear and uncertain
	• Scripture should be interpreted in its original context and language

â€¢ ••Determining Controversies in Divinity••
	• There is no visible power appointed in the Church to determine controversies
	• Men are duty-bound to inquire and seek the truth
	• A gift of discerning is bestowed on men, and a desire to further knowledge and practice is commanded

â€¢ ••The Language of Scripture••
	• Scripture was written in the most common languages of the Church at the time
	• Hebrew was used for books written before Christ, and Greek was used for books written afterward

â€¢ ••Understanding Scripture••
	• Knowledge of the original languages is necessary for an exact understanding of Scripture
	• Other human writings are understood through logic, rhetoric, grammar, and language skills
	• A singular light of the Spirit is always to be sought in Scripture

â€¢ ••Translation of Scripture••
	• Scripture is not tied to its original languages and can be translated into other languages
	• Translations are necessary for the common use of the Church
	• However, translators are not infallible and can make errors

â€¢ ••The Authority of Translations••
	• No persons are absolutely authentic, except insofar as they express the fountains by which they are to be tried
	• There is no authority on Earth that can make a translation solely authentic
	• The preservation of the original texts is a gift of God to be adored

â€¢ ••The Preservation of Scripture••
	• The provision of God in preserving the original texts has always been famous
	• The original texts have not wholly perished, and no book has been lost or deformed by grievous fault

â€¢ ••The Role of Men in Interpreting Scripture••
	• Men are duty-bound to inquire and seek the truth in Scripture
	• A gift of discerning is bestowed on men, and a desire to further knowledge and practice is commanded
	• Men should seek the singular light of the Spirit in Scripture

â€¢ ••The Importance of Original Languages••
	• Knowledge of the original languages is necessary for an exact understanding of Scripture
	• Other human writings are understood through logic, rhetoric, grammar, and language skills
	• A singular light of the Spirit is always to be sought in Scripture

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Translation and Canon••
	• The Church should strive for the purest and least faulty translation of the Bible.
	• The original Greek and Hebrew manuscripts are the most authoritative versions.
	• The Roman Catholic Church demanded that William Tyndale translate from Jerome's Latin Vulgate, rather than the original manuscripts.

â€¢ ••The Canon of Scripture••
	• The Canon of Scripture is made up of books delivered by God and placed in the Church.
	• The Prophets made the Canon of the Old Testament, and Christ approved it.
	• The Apostle John approved the Canon of the New Testament, which was sealed with Divine Authority.

â€¢ ••Apocryphal Books••
	• Apocryphal books do not pertain to the divine Canon.
	• They contain manifest fables, contradictions, and were not written in Hebrew.
	• They were not approved by Christ, the Apostles, or the first Christian Church.

â€¢ ••Judging Translations••
	• Every translation should be tested against the best original copies.
	• The Church should ensure that the purest translation is provided.

â€¢ ••The Role of Ordinary Ministers••
	• Ordinary Ministers are guided by the will of God revealed in the Scriptures.
	• They depend on extraordinary Ministers and are their successors in administration.

â€¢ ••The Limitations of Ordinary Ministers••
	• Ordinary Ministers should not propose or do anything not prescribed in the Scriptures.
	• They should follow the fixed rule of the Scriptures in their administration.

â€¢ ••The Authority of Scripture••
	• The words of the Lord are pure and preserved forever (Psalm 12:6-7).
	• Scripture is the ultimate authority for the Church.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Scripture••
	• Scripture is the foundation of the Church's faith and practice.
	• It is the source of the Church's authority and guidance.

â€¢ ••The Role of Extraordinary Ministers••
	• Extraordinary Ministers have no successors in terms of manner and degree.
	• However, ordinary Ministers depend on them and are their successors in essence.

â€¢ ••The Perpetual Edification of the Church••
	• The Church's perpetual edification is guided by the will of God revealed in the Scriptures.
	• Ordinary Ministers play a crucial role in this process.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Ministry of the Church••
	• The right to administer divine things is immediately communicated from God to all lawful ministers.
	• The Church appoints persons to receive this authority.
• ••The Role of the Church••
	• The Church cannot confer gifts necessary for the ministry or prescribe to God who should receive them.
	• The Church can only choose those whom they see fit beforehand.
• ••The Calling of a Minister••
	• A lawful test must precede the calling of a minister (1 Tim 3:10).
	• The minister must be blameless and tested before being appointed.
• ••The Duties of an Ordinary Minister••
	• To preserve, propagate, and restore the Church through ordinary means.
	• To preach the Word in the name of God and the people.
• ••The Preaching of the Word••
	• The preaching of the Word is of perpetual use in the Church.
	• The duty of a preacher is to propound the Will of God out of the Word to the edification of the hearers.
• ••The Qualifications of a Preacher••
	• A preacher must have a serious desire to edify the Church.
	• They must have prepared their heart to seek the Law of the Lord and teach it.
• ••The Scope of Edification••
	• A preacher must edify all hearers, regardless of age or order.
	• They must do this both publicly and privately.
• ••The Importance of Scripture••
	• A preacher must have their senses exercised in the Holy Scriptures.
	• They must be mighty in the Scriptures, like Apollos (Acts 18:24).
• ••The Application of Scripture••
	• A preacher must make a declaration of the things contained in the Text.
	• They must apply those things to the consciences of the hearers as their condition requires.
• ••The Fruit of Edification••
	• The Will of God must be propounded with the fruit of edification.
	• This requires a declaration of the Text and its application to the hearers.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Proper Preaching••
	• Deceitful preachers propound a text but fail to draw out its meaning.
	• They confuse the explanation of the text with its application.
• ••Order of Preaching••
	• First, explain the text.
	• Then, apply its meaning to the hearers' lives.
• ••Scripture's Purpose••
	• Scripture is profitable for doctrine, reproof, correction, and instruction in righteousness.
• ••Preachers' Responsibility••
	• Preachers should teach themselves before teaching others.
	• They should remind their hearers of the truth.
• ••Fruitless Talk••
	• Idle, empty, and fruitless talk is vain babbling or blathering.
	• It has no eternal value.
• ••Sound Doctrine••
	• Preachers should hold fast to faithful words and sound doctrine.
	• They should show integrity, reverence, and incorruptibility in their teaching.
• ••Doctrinal Axioms••
	• Doctrine is a theological axiom that consists of the express words of Scripture or flows from them.
• ••Finding and Handling Doctrine••
	• Doctrine must be found out through logical analysis.
	• It must be handled by proving and illustrating its meaning.
• ••Proving and Illustrating Doctrine••
	• Proving doctrine should be done using clearer testimonies of Scripture.
	• Illustrating doctrine can be done using various methods, including contrasting and comparative arguments.
• ••Applying Doctrine••
	• Doctrine should be applied to the hearers' lives.
	• This is the end and good of the sermon, and it is essential for edification.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Preaching should be guided by conscience and circumstances••
	• Preachers should only choose doctrines and uses that are necessary for the audience
	• The goal is to stir up or confirm the life of Religion in the hearers

â€¢ ••Preachers should not be careless with their words••
	• They should not try to say many things just to seem learned
	• This can lead to misinterpretation and subversion of the hearers

â€¢ ••Doctrine and use should be connected••
	• The mind is not drawn from one thing to another without disprofit
	• An order of deduction helps memory and understanding

â€¢ ••A use is a theological axiom••
	• It shows the profit, goodness, or end of a doctrine
	• It is a practical application of a doctrinal truth or principle

â€¢ ••The reason for deduction should be clarified••
	• A proof or illustration should be provided if necessary
	• The needs of the hearers and the prudence of the speaker should guide this

â€¢ ••A use can pertain to judgment or practice••
	• Judgment involves information and reformation of the mind
	• Practice involves direction, which consists of instruction and correction

â€¢ ••Information and reformation are important••
	• Information proves some truth
	• Reformation confutes some error

â€¢ ••Not every error needs to be refuted••
	• Old heresies and wicked blasphemies should not be repeated or refuted unnecessarily
	• This can trouble and offend the hearers

â€¢ ••Direction in practice involves instruction and correction••
	• Instruction demonstrates the life to be followed
	• Correction condemns the life to be shunned

â€¢ ••Application of doctrine is essential••
	• It involves whetting and clothing a general truth with special accommodation
	• It should pierce the minds of the hearers with godly affections

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Understanding the Text••
	• The declaration of things contained in the text (thesis 17)
	• The text is a guide for understanding the Word of God

â€¢ ••Whet••
	• To make keen or more acute
	• To sharpen or bring into tight focus

â€¢ ••The Word of God••
	• Living and powerful (Heb 4:12)
	• Sharper than any two-edged sword
	• Piercing to the division of soul and spirit

â€¢ ••Application of the Word••
	• Can be applied to:
		- An oppressed mind (consolation)
		- Fainting in the prosecution of good (exhortation)
		- Avoiding evil (admonition)

â€¢ ••Consolation••
	• Application of an argument to take away or mitigate grief and fear
	• Marks are joined to assure the conscience of a benefit

â€¢ ••Exhortation••
	• Application of an argument to beget, cherish, and excite virtue
	• Show means to beget virtue, but prove by Scripture and examples

â€¢ ••Admonition••
	• Application of an argument to correct viciousness
	• Remedies are joined from Scripture to prevail against vices

â€¢ ••Working with the Word••
	• No ostentation of human wisdom or mingling of human affections
	• Demonstration of the Spirit should be manifest everywhere

â€¢ ••Avoiding Human Testimonies••
	• Human testimonies and histories should not be intermingled except rarely
	• Words or sentences in Latin, Greek, or Hebrew should not be used

â€¢ ••Purity of the Word••
	• The purity, perfection, and majesty of the Word of God is violated by human mixture
	• Scandal is given to the hearers, who may loathe the simplicity of the Gospel

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Avoiding unnecessary elements••
	• Avoid citing human testimonies frequently or for display of learning
	• Avoid using far-fetched proems or persuasive words of orators
	• Avoid digressions or excursions that favor a human spirit

â€¢ ••Spiritual speech and action••
	• Speech and action should be wholly spiritual and flow from the heart
	• Show a man conversant in exercises of piety and persuaded in his own conscience
	• Display zeal, charity, mildness, freedom, and humility with grave authority

â€¢ ••Proper pronunciation••
	• Pronounce speech naturally, familiarly, clearly, and distinctly
	• Agreeable to the matter to move the affections
	• Avoid heavy, slow, singing, and drowsy voices
	• Avoid hasty and swift voices that hinder understanding

â€¢ ••Avoiding ridiculous speech••
	• Avoid speech that would be ridiculous in a senate, court, or place of judgment
	• Avoid using a voice that is too formal or affected

â€¢ ••The power of simplicity••
	• The efficacy of the Holy Spirit appears in a naked simplicity of words
	• Avoid affectation and use genuine simplicity
	• Elegance and neatness are not as important as spiritual edification

â€¢ ••Spiritual edification••
	• Nothing should be admitted that does not make for the spiritual edification of the people
	• Anything that can be omitted to attain this end should be omitted

â€¢ ••Prayer in the sermon••
	• An appendix of the sermon is prayer, both before and after
	• Prayer should stir up the minds of the people to seek and observe the Will of God

â€¢ ••Content of prayer••
	• Propound general things to bring to remembrance the end and use of the word and preaching
	• Mention wants, unworthiness, and duty, as well as gracious promises of God

â€¢ ••Importance of prayer••
	• Prayer is essential to the sermon and should be taken seriously
	• Prayer should be a natural part of the sermon, not an afterthought

â€¢ ••Authenticity and authority••
	• The speaker should be authentic and genuine in their speech and action
	• Display grave authority and conviction in their words and actions

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Definition of a Sign••
	• A sign is a sensible thing that carries an immediate show to the senses
	• It also makes another thing come into the mind along with it
• ••Types of Signs••
	• Natural signs
	• Signs by institution (created by God or humans)
	• Ordinary and perpetual signs
	• Extraordinary and temporary signs
• ••Classification of Extraordinary and Temporary Signs••
	• Ekdh (Rememorativum): sign of things past
	• Diagnwv (Demonstrativum): sign of things present
	• P 5 (Proenunciativum): sign of things to come
	• Combination of all three
• ••Purpose of Signs••
	• Notificans (NOTIFYING sign): serves the understanding
	• Commonefaciens (ADMONISHING sign): serves the memory
	• Obsignans (SEALING sign): serves faith
	• Combination of all three
• ••Holy Signs••
	• Bare sign: represents something
	• Seal: represents and exhibits something by sealing
• ••Sacraments••
	• A sacrament is a holy sign that seals the Covenant of God
	• It is a divine institution that represents, exhibits, and applies the blessings of the New Covenant
• ••Characteristics of Sacraments••
	• Respect to a secondary divine testimony
	• Special application of God's favor and grace
	• Confirmation and furthering of true faith
• ••Components of Sacraments••
	• Sensible thing (sign)
	• Spiritual thing (thing represented and applied)
• ••Relationship between Sign and Thing Signified••
	• The sign does not physically inhering or adhere to the thing signified
	• The sign communicates the thing itself, testifying and exhibiting it
• ••Institution of Sacraments••
	• Only God can institute a holy sign (sacrament)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••What are Sacraments?••
	• Sacraments are signs that confirm faith and confidence
	• They are used to stir up spiritual grace in us
• ••Types of Signs••
	• Representing signs (e.g. bread and wine in the Supper)
	• Applying signs (e.g. distributing, receiving, eating, and drinking)
• ••Existence of Sacraments••
	• Sacraments do not exist outside of their use
	• They are not Sacraments before or after they are applied
• ••Spiritual Thing Signified••
	• The New Covenant itself (Christ and his blessings)
	• Different Sacraments represent different aspects of the Covenant
• ••Union between Sign and Thing Signified••
	• A spiritual relation that communicates the thing signified to those who use the sign
	• Not corporal or imaginary, but real and spiritual
• ••Communication of Predication••
	• The sign is predicated by the thing signified (e.g. sanctification is called circumcision)
	• The thing is signified by the sign (e.g. circumcision is called the Covenant)
	• The effect of the thing signified is predicated by the sign (e.g. Baptism regenerates)
	• A property of the sign is predicated by the thing signified (e.g. breaking bread is attributed to Christ)
	• A property of the thing signified is attributed to the sign (e.g. sacramental eating and drinking is called spiritual)
• ••Foundation of the Relation between Sign and Thing Signified••
	• Similitude or proportion of the sign to the thing signified
	• The word of institution (command and promise)
	• Observation and use of the sign
• ••Primary End of a Sacrament••
	• To seal the Covenant
	• To seal God's promises and our thankfulness and obedience
• ••Institution of Sacraments••
	• Only God can institute Sacraments
	• Man cannot institute mystical signs of holy things without violating the Sacraments
• ••Secondary End of a Sacrament••
	• The profession of faith and love
	• Representing the union we have with God

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Importance of Saving Faith••
	• Baptism and Communion without saving faith do not signify new birth or seal salvation.
	• 1 Corinthians 11:27-29 warns against taking Communion in an unworthy manner.

â€¢ ••Declaration of Attributes••
	• A declaration is a statement that is self-evident in the sign, like an attribute or quality of what is signified.
	• Examples include the declaration of the new covenant in Jesus' blood (Luke 22:20).

â€¢ ••Circumcision of the Heart••
	• Romans 2:29 says that true circumcision is of the heart, in the Spirit.
	• This emphasizes the importance of spiritual renewal and regeneration.

â€¢ ••Sacraments and Spiritual Food••
	• 1 Corinthians 10:16 says that the bread we break is the communion of the body of Christ.
	• This highlights the spiritual significance of the Sacraments.

â€¢ ••Discipline in the Church••
	• Discipline is a personal application of the Will of God by censures, for the prevention or removal of scandals.
	• It is an adjunct of the Word and Sacraments, and is ordained and prescribed by Christ.

â€¢ ••Purpose of Discipline••
	• Discipline is exercised to remove vices contrary to faith and obedience.
	• It is a means of spiritual reformation, given only to the Church.

â€¢ ••Scope of Discipline••
	• Discipline is exercised among members of visible instituted Churches.
	• It does not apply to others, but only to those who have the right to partake of the Sacrament.

â€¢ ••Authority of Discipline••
	• Discipline is ordained and prescribed by Christ himself.
	• It cannot be taken away, diminished, or changed by men at their pleasure.

â€¢ ••Responsibility to Establish Discipline••
	• Whoever does not do as much as they can to establish and promote Discipline in the Churches of God sins against Christ.
	• This emphasizes the importance of upholding Discipline in the Church.

â€¢ ••Relationship between Discipline and the Word and Sacraments••
	• Discipline is usually joined with the Word and Sacraments in the notes of the Church.
	• It is not an essential and reciprocal note, but it should necessarily be present for the complete estate of a Church.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Authority of Discipline••
	• Discipline is based on the authority of Christ (Mat 18:15)
	• It is a means of promoting the Gospel and the Kingdom of Christ
• ••The Purpose of Discipline••
	• To bind and loose on earth, and have it bound and loosed in heaven (Mat 18:15)
	• To promote obedience to Christ and repress sin
• ••The Process of Discipline••
	• Go to the brother alone to correct the sin (Mat 18:15)
	• If he refuses, take one or two more witnesses (Mat 18:16)
	• If he still refuses, tell it to the church (Mat 18:17)
• ••The Importance of Discipline••
	• It is a necessary part of the Kingdom of Christ
	• It is a means of promoting the Gospel and the Church
• ••The Role of Excommunication••
	• It is a part of the Discipline of Christ
	• It is a means of removing sin from the Church
• ••The End of Reproof••
	• To prevent the necessity of Excommunication
	• To encourage the sinner to repent and be retained in the Church
• ••The Use of Correction••
	• It should be used in every sin that is subject to Discipline
	• It should be used in various ways according to the nature of the sin
• ••The Three Degrees of Correction••
	• For hidden sins, three degrees of correction should be observed:
		- Private correction
		- Public correction
		- Excommunication
• ••The Importance of Private Care••
	• In churches lacking Discipline, private care and watching over one another is necessary
	• This is to offset the public defect of lacking Discipline
• ••The Relationship between Discipline and the Gospel••
	• Discipline is a part of the Gospel
	• Those who reject Discipline do not receive the whole Gospel
• ••The Necessity of Discipline••
	• Every part of the Kingdom of Christ is necessary
	• Discipline is necessary to repress sin and promote obedience to Christ

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Purpose of Excommunication••
	• To save the sinner's spirit in the day of the Lord Jesus (Matthew 28:20)
	• To separate the obstinate sinner from the faithful (2 Thessalonians 3:14)

â€¢ ••Causes of Excommunication••
	• Public sins (1 Timothy 5:20)
	• Contumacy (Matthew 18:17)
	• Failure to repent after being admonished (Matthew 18:15-17)

â€¢ ••Types of Excommunication••
	• Lesser Excommunication: suspension from the Supper and similar privileges
	• Greater Excommunication: complete severing from the communion of the faithful

â€¢ ••Procedure for Excommunication••
	• Admonition and warning (Matthew 18:15-17)
	• Suspension from the Supper and similar privileges (lesser Excommunication)
	• Complete severing from the communion of the faithful (greater Excommunication)

â€¢ ••Avoiding the Excommunicated••
	• Avoid them in respect to conversation and familiarity (2 Thessalonians 3:14)
	• Do not confer, pray, feast, or eat with them (Os, orare, vale, conviva, mensa, negatur)

â€¢ ••Loosing from Excommunication••
	• Only the penitent should be loosed from the bond of Excommunication
	• Loosing should not be denied to anyone who is truly repentant

â€¢ ••Judging Repentance••
	• Serious repentance should appear to be satisfied in the offender
	• Hypocrisy should not be nourished, and the Church should not be mocked

â€¢ ••Power of Discipline••
	• The power of discipline pertains to the Church of which the offender is a member
	• The Church should execute discipline with the consent of its members

â€¢ ••Consent of the Church••
	• Discipline should be executed with the consent of the Church
	• The Church should not be mocked or deceived by hypocrisy

â€¢ ••Repentance and Excommunication••
	• A weak repentance may be allowed in some cases
	• True repentance should be evident in the offender's actions and behavior

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Excommunication••
	• A full or comprehensive excommunication is treated as a non-believer
	• Refusal to accept Church correction or judgment is considered obstinate rebelliousness
	• Excommunication involves forbidding speech, prayer, greeting, association, and food

â€¢ ••Role of Elders••
	• Elders have a key role in administering discipline
	• They direct public action and pronounce sentence
	• They also provide preceding admonitions to correct private persons

â€¢ ••Censure of Church Leaders••
	• Popes, bishops, and their officers are guilty of profanation and injustice
	• They are seen as snares to catch money, not spiritual remedies
	• Their actions are considered grievous censures

â€¢ ••Indulgences and Commutations••
	• Indulgences, commutations, and human transactions are considered wages of the great Whore
	• These practices are seen as contrary to Christ's ordinances

â€¢ ••Administration of the Covenant of Grace••
	• The manner of administering the New Covenant has varied throughout history
	• There has been progress from imperfect to perfect administration

â€¢ ••Variety of Administration••
	• The mystery of the Gospel was manifested more darkly at first, then more clearly
	• The administration of Christ has been exhibited in two ways: as to be exhibited and as exhibited

â€¢ ••Old and New Testaments••
	• The Old Testament promises the coming of Christ, while the New Testament testifies to his arrival
	• The Old Testament was more outward and carnal, while the New Testament is more inward and spiritual

â€¢ ••Church as Heir and Infant••
	• The Church has been considered both as an heir and as an infant
	• As an heir, the Church is free, spiritual, and has the spirit of adoption
	• As an infant, the Church is servile, carnal, and has the spirit of fear and bondage

â€¢ ••Progress of the Church••
	• The Church has progressed from a more imperfect to a more perfect administration
	• The manner of administration has changed over time, with a greater emphasis on spiritual matters

â€¢ ••Typology of the Church••
	• The Church is seen as a type of the great Whore, Babylon the Great
	• The Church's practices and actions are seen as contrary to Christ's ordinances and the spirit of adoption

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Dispensation from Adam to Abraham••
	• Redemption by Christ was promised
	• Calling was exercised through distinction between the seed of the Woman and the seed of the Devil
	• Justification was set forth through expiatory sacrifices
	• Adoption was declared through the title of sons and the translation of Enoch
	• Sanctification was both explicitly inculcated and typically shadowed out by sacrifices
	• Glorification was publicly sealed through the example of Enoch and the conservation of Noah

â€¢ ••Ordinary and Extraordinary Sacraments••
	• There was no ordinary Sacrament in this period
	• Sacrifices had a sacramental aspect, with those who sacrificed participating in the holy banquet
	• The building and conserving of the Ark was an extraordinary Sacrament

â€¢ ••Key Events and Figures••
	• The seed of the Woman would break the Serpent's head (Gen 3.15)
	• Christ would tread Satan underfoot (Rom 16.20)
	• The Son of God was manifested to dissolve the works of the Devil (1Joh 3.8)

â€¢ ••Period of Time from Abraham to Moses••
	• The Church chiefly consisted in Abraham's family and posterity
	• All the benefits of the New Covenant were more clearly and distinctly set forth
	• Election was represented in the persons of Isaac and Jacob

â€¢ ••Key Concepts••
	• Redemption
	• Calling
	• Justification
	• Adoption
	• Sanctification
	• Glorification
	• Election

â€¢ ••Scriptural References••
	• Gen 3.15
	• Rom 16.20
	• 1Joh 3.8
	• Gen 6.2
	• Eph 5.2
	• Gen 5.24
	• Heb 11.5
	• Rom 12.1

	• 1Pet 3.20-21
	• Heb 11.7
	• Exo 18.12
	• Rom 9.11

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Redemption••
	• Exhibited in the person and blessing of Melchizedek
	• Promised and covenanted to all nations through the seed of Abraham
• ••Calling••
	• Illustrated by God leading Abraham out of Ur to a new and heavenly country
	• Demonstrated in Abraham's obedience to God's call
• ••Justification••
	• Illustrated by God's testimony that faith was imputed to Abraham for righteousness
	• Demonstrated through the sacrament of circumcision
• ••Adoption••
	• Set forth by God pronouncing His name upon Abraham and his sons
	• Illustrated by the inheritance assigned to the sons of the promise
• ••Sanctification••
	• Figured by circumcision, which symbolized the removal of sin and corruption
	• Demonstrated in the new creature that was established through circumcision
• ••Glorification••
	• Shown in the blessing promised to Abraham and the land of Canaan
	• Typified the heavenly country that is to come
• ••Redemption and its application••
	• Extraordinarily signified by the deliverance out of Egypt through Moses
	• Demonstrated in the bringing into the Promised Land
• ••Redemption and its application (continued)••
	• Extraordinarily signified by the ministry of Moses as a type of Christ
	• Demonstrated in the bringing into the Promised Land
• ••Redemption and its application (continued)••
	• Extraordinarily signified by the deliverance from Egypt and the Promised Land
	• Demonstrated in the ministry of Christ
• ••Continuity of themes••
	• All these themes were further shadowed from the time of Moses to Christ
	• Demonstrated through both extraordinary and ordinary means

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Type of Christ••
	• The land of Canaan is a type of Christ, as it was given to God's people by the Ministry of Joshua.
	• The bronze serpent is a type of Christ, as it restored health to those who beheld it (Joh 3:14, 12:32).

â€¢ ••Cloud and Protection••
	• The Cloud is a type of Christ, as it covered the Israelites from their enemies and the heavens (1Cor 10:2, Isa 4:5).
	• The Cloud provided light and refreshed the Israelites' strength day and night.

â€¢ ••Passing through the Red Sea••
	• Passing through the Red Sea is a type of Christ, as it provided a way to the Promised Land and destroyed their enemies (1Cor 10:2).

â€¢ ••Manna and Water••
	• Manna from Heaven and water from the Rock are types of Christ, as they provided continual nourishment to the Israelites (1Cor 10:3-4, Joh 6:32-33).

â€¢ ••Types of Redemption••
	• The high priest, authors, and sacrifices for sins are types of Christ's redemption (Heb 12:2).
	• The Passover is a type of Christ's justification (1Cor 5:7).

â€¢ ••Types of Adoption••
	• The firstborn are a type of Christ's adoption, as they were dedicated to God (Exo 13:15).

â€¢ ••Types of Sanctification••
	• Offerings and gifts are types of Christ's sanctification, as they showed cleanliness and purity (Num 8:6-7).

â€¢ ••Types of Glorification••
	• The inheritance of the Promised Land is a type of Christ's glorification (Exo 26:33).
	• Communion with God in the Most Holy Place is a type of Christ's glorification (Jos 18:1).

â€¢ ••The Church••
	• The Church is the fulfillment of the types and shadows of the Old Testament (36. The Church).

â€¢ ••New Testament References••
	• The New Testament references the Old Testament types and shadows, such as Christ being the true bread from heaven (Joh 6:32-33).

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Church of the Jews••
	• Was a national Church
	• Was a single Church in terms of its outward gathering
	• Depended on the Temple for Communion and worship

â€¢ ••The Synagogues••
	• Were not complete Churches
	• Could not exercise the whole worship of God or Holy Communion

â€¢ ••The Church of the Jews was universal••
	• Believing proselytes from every nation were bound to join the Church
	• This was because the Church was a single entity under the Temple

â€¢ ••Ministers of the Church••
	• Were primarily the Priests of the family of Aaron
	• Were joined by other Levites
	• Had to be tested and qualified in body, age, and mind

â€¢ ••Ecclesiastical Discipline••
	• Was ceremonial in nature
	• Was designed to preserve holy things pure

â€¢ ••The Administration of the Covenant••
	• Has changed from the time of Moses to the present day
	• Is now double: one until the end of the world and the other in the end itself

â€¢ ••The New Testament••
	• Is the new manner of administering the Covenant
	• Is perfect and will not be altered or replaced
	• Is new in terms of its manner, not its essence

â€¢ ••The Covenant of Works••
	• Had a representation of the New Testament
	• Differed from the New Testament in its manner of administration

â€¢ ••The Church from Christ to the end of the world••
	• Has a single manner of administration
	• Is the New Testament in terms of its covenant and promise

â€¢ ••The Church in the end itself••
	• Will have a different manner of administration
	• Will be discussed in Chapter 41

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Differences between the Old and New Covenants••
	• The New Covenant differs from the Old in quality and quantity
	• The New Covenant is an improvement over the Old

â€¢ ••Quality of the New Covenant••
	• ••Clearness••: the doctrine of salvation by faith in Christ is more distinct and express
	• ••Clearness••: the doctrine is no longer declared in types and shadows, but in a clear and obvious way

â€¢ ••Clearness in the New Covenant••
	• Christ is now offered with an open and unveiled face, unlike under the Old Covenant
	• The veil that obscured the truth is now lifted, and believers can see clearly

â€¢ ••Freedom in the New Covenant••
	• The government of the Law, or the Covenant of Works, is now taken away
	• The Spirit of Adoption is now more fully communicated, and believers are free from bondage

â€¢ ••Freedom in the New Covenant (continued)••
	• The yoke of the Ceremonial Law is now wholly taken away
	• The Ceremonial Law was a burdenome and restrictive system that veiled the truth

â€¢ ••Implications of the New Covenant••
	• Those who impose Jewish or other ceremonies on the Christian Church offend against the liberty of Christ
	• Divine ceremonies are not taken away to be replaced by human ceremonies

â€¢ ••Christ's intention••
	• Christ did not leave mysteries to the will of men after his coming
	• Christ could have provided for human ceremonies if he judged them necessary or profitable

â€¢ ••The authority to impose ceremonies••
	• Christ nowhere granted authority to impose ceremonies on the Church
	• The Church is free to stand in the liberty that Christ has made available

â€¢ ••The importance of liberty••
	• Believers are to stand fast in the liberty with which Christ has made them free
	• They are not to be entangled with a yoke of bondage by going back to the Old Covenant

â€¢ ••The fulfillment of the Law••
	• Christ did not come to destroy the Law or the Prophets, but to fulfill them
	• The New Covenant is an improvement over the Old, and believers are free to live in the liberty of Christ.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The New Covenant Administration••
	• Differing from the Old Testament in quantity (intensively and extensively)
	• More effectual and perfect in its application by the Spirit
• ••Intensive Differences••
	• Application by the Spirit is more effectual and perfect
	• Gifts of the Spirit are more perfect
	• Begets a more spiritual life
• ••Extensive Differences••
	• Not limited to one people, but diffused through the whole world
	• Has no term of duration before the consummation of the whole mystical Church
• ••Perfection of the New Administration••
	• Requires a perfect communion of Saints in the Church
	• All holy ordinances should be observed in every Church
• ••One Church, One Right••
	• All Christian Churches have one and the same right
	• No church depends on another
• ••Size of a Church••
	• A church should not consist of more members than can meet together in one place
	• To exercise communion together in worship, sacraments, and other duties
• ••Aberration in Larger Cities••
	• In some cities, believers are not distributed into various churches
	• Instead, they form one large church
• ••Importance of Congregationalism••
	• Ames believed in independent, voluntary congregations
	• Not separatism, but rather a principle of Congregationalism
• ••Edification of the Church••
	• The edification of the Church is hindered by large churches
	• Believers should be distributed into various churches for communion and worship
• ••Unity of the Faith••
	• The ultimate goal is to come to the unity of the faith and the knowledge of the Son of God
	• To a perfect man, to the measure of the full stature of Christ.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Church is not a single entity••
	• The Church is not a single, universal entity that encompasses all believers.
	• Instead, it is composed of many individual churches, each with its own distinct identity.

â€¢ ••The Church is instituted by God••
	• The Church is not a human institution, but rather one instituted by God.
	• This means that the Church has a divine nature and purpose.

â€¢ ••The Church is not national, provincial, or diocesan••
	• The Church is not defined by national, provincial, or diocesan boundaries.
	• These forms of organization were introduced by humans, not by God.

â€¢ ••The Church is parochial••
	• The Church is properly understood as a local, parochial community.
	• This means that the Church is composed of a specific group of believers who gather together in one place.

â€¢ ••The word "Church" has a specific meaning••
	• The word "Church" (Ekklesia) refers specifically to a local, visible community of believers.
	• It does not have a broader meaning in the New Testament.

â€¢ ••There are many churches, not one••
	• The New Testament refers to many individual churches, rather than a single, universal Church.
	• These churches are often referred to in the plural, as "churches".

â€¢ ••Local churches are called "Churches" in the plural••
	• Even in the New Testament, local churches are referred to in the plural, as "churches".
	• This is true even in Judea, which was considered a single national Church.

â€¢ ••Local churches often gather together••
	• Local churches often come together to meet and worship as a larger community.
	• This is seen in the New Testament, where churches gather together for various purposes.

â€¢ ••The Church is composed of many individuals••
	• The Church is composed of many individual believers, each with their own unique identity.
	• These individuals are united together in a common bond of faith.

â€¢ ••The Church is a diverse, yet unified, entity••
	• The Church is composed of many diverse individuals and communities, yet is still a unified entity.
	• This diversity is reflected in the many different churches that exist throughout the world.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Church Governance••
	• The New Testament does not establish a larger church that lesser congregations depend on.
	• There is no prescribed worship or holy ordinance that is not to be observed in every congregation.

â€¢ ••Church Confederacy••
	• Particular churches may enter into mutual confederacy and fellowship among themselves in classes and synods.
	• This confederacy does not constitute a new form of church and does not diminish the liberty and power of individual churches.

â€¢ ••Ordinary Ministers••
	• Ordinary ministers follow the form of the church instituted by Christ.
	• They are not ecumenical, national, provincial, or diocesan bishops, but rather elders of one congregation.

â€¢ ••Transcendent Members of the Hierarchy••
	• These members were created by humans and have no divine precept or example.
	• They cannot fulfill the office of a pastor in multiple congregations and exercise a tyrannical dominion over churches.

â€¢ ••Calling and Ordination••
	• The right to call an ordinary minister is in the church itself to whom he must serve.
	• The essence of the calling is in election by the church and acceptance by the elected.

â€¢ ••Examination and Ordination••
	• Examination or trial is an antecedent adjunct of the calling.
	• Ordination is a solemn entrance of the minister into the free execution of his function, often signified by the laying on of hands.

â€¢ ••Episcopal Ordination••
	• Episcopal ordination without a title (i.e., without a church to which and in which he should be ordained) is ridiculous.
	• A minister called to a church cannot forsake it at his own will or be thrown out without just cause.

â€¢ ••Church Meetings••
	• When the church comes together in one place, each member has a role to play (e.g., psalm, teaching, tongue, revelation, interpretation).

â€¢ ••Church Liberty••
	• Christ has left the church with liberty and power to direct and further its own affairs.
	• This liberty and power should not be diminished by external authorities.

â€¢ ••Hierarchy and Antichrist••
	• The Roman Antichrist has been brought into the church through the hierarchy.
	• The hierarchy has introduced oppressive structures and practices that undermine the liberty and power of the church.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Instructions for the Church••
	• Paul instructs the Corinthians to come together for the better, not for the worse (1 Cor 11:17)
	• When eating together, wait for one another (1 Cor 11:33)

â€¢ ••Appointing Elders••
	• Paul and Barnabas appointed elders in every church (Acts 14:23)
	• They prayed with fasting and commended the elders to the Lord

â€¢ ••Ordinary Ministers••
	• There are three types of ordinary ministers: Pastors and Teachers, ruling Elders, and Deacons
	• Deacons take care of the poor, including Deaconesses and Widows

â€¢ ••Christ's Provision for the Church••
	• Christ has provided for the Church's needs through these offices
	• Teachers instruct in the knowledge of the truth
	• Pastors stir up the practice of piety
	• Rulers preserve and call back to repentance
	• Deacons help against poverty

â€¢ ••Sacraments of the New Testament••
	• The New Testament has few sacraments, which are easy to obtain and understand
	• These sacraments are Baptism and the Supper of the Lord

â€¢ ••Institution of Sacraments••
	• Christ himself instituted these sacraments
	• John the Baptist was a forerunner of Christ, showing what Christ would allow and institute

â€¢ ••Baptism••
	• Baptism is the sacrament of initiation or regeneration
	• It seals the covenant of grace and represents our ingrafting into Christ

â€¢ ••Meaning of Baptism••
	• Baptism represents our justification and adoption
	• It is a representation of our consecration to the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit

â€¢ ••Supper of the Lord••
	• The Supper of the Lord is a sacrament instituted by Christ
	• It is not absolutely necessary for salvation, but should be celebrated by lawful ministers in a Church assembly

â€¢ ••Importance of Sacraments••
	• The faithful should use sacraments diligently and religiously
	• Sacraments should be joined with sound teaching to ensure right doctrine and practice

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Baptism as a Seal of Sanctification••
	• Baptism is the seal of our sanctification, marking our spiritual rebirth.
	• It is a sign of our regeneration and the renewing of the Holy Spirit.
• ••Baptism as a Seal of Eternal Glory••
	• Baptism is also the seal of eternal glory, marking our inheritance of eternal life.
	• It is a sign of our union with Christ and our future resurrection.
• ••Administration of Baptism••
	• Baptism is to be administered only once, as there is only one beginning of spiritual life.
	• It is the first sealing of the Covenant of Grace, which pertains to all who are part of it.
• ••Baptism for Infants of the Faithful••
	• Infants of the faithful are not to be forbidden baptism, as they are part of the Covenant of Grace.
	• The Covenant of Grace is the same as the one made with Abraham, which included infants.
	• Baptism brings larger and fuller consolation to the faithful than circumcision did.
• ••Comparison to Circumcision••
	• Baptism succeeded circumcision, and pertains to the children of believers just as circumcision did.
	• Infants are as capable of receiving baptism as those who are of age.
• ••Faith and Repentance••
	• Faith and repentance are not required for baptism, as they were not required for circumcision.
	• The lack of faith and repentance should not keep baptism from infants.
• ••The Sign of Baptism••
	• The sign of baptism is water, which represents the spiritual washing performed by Christ's blood.
	• Water is chosen because it is a fitting representation of spiritual cleansing.
• ••Mode of Baptism••
	• Baptism can be administered by dipping or sprinkling, as both represent the spiritual washing of Christ's blood.
	• The mode of baptism is not as important as the spiritual significance of the act.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Supper of the Lord••
	• Is the Sacrament of the nourishing and growth of the faithful in Christ (16)
	• Should be often administered to the same person (17)
• ••Administration of the Supper••
	• Is only to be administered to those who are visibly capable of nourishment and growth in the Church (18)
	• Should not be administered to infants, but only to those of age (18)
• ••Signs of Nourishment••

	• These signs are used because they conveniently express the union with Christ (21)
• ••Transubstantiation and Consubstantiation••

	• Are against the nature of a Sacrament in general (24)
	• Are against the analogy of baptism (24)
• ••Spiritual Presence of Christ••
	• Does not require that the bread and wine be changed into the blood of Christ (23)
	• Requires only that Christ be spiritually present with those who receive them in faith (23)
• ••Words of Institution••

	• Is against the revealed will of God, which says that Christ shall remain in Heaven until the Day of Judgment (24)
• ••Other Sacraments••
	• Baptism is an analogy for the Supper (24)
• ••Scriptural References••
	• The text references several scriptures, including Genesis 17, Hebrews 9, John 6, and 1 Corinthians 11 (1, 2, 3, 25)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Interpretation of the phrase "in the words of this phrase"••
	• Different interpreters have varying opinions on the meaning of the phrase
	• Some see a trope (metaphor or metonymy) in the words
	• Others argue there is no trope, only an unusual predication
• ••Lutheran interpretation••
	• Lutherans contend there is no trope, only an unusual predication
	• They argue that the phrase is literal and self-evident
• ••Alternative interpretations••
	• Some interpreters deny the presence of a trope or unusual predication
	• They argue that the phrase is an improper and mystical predication
• ••Demonstration of a trope••
	• If the phrase is an improper or unusual predication, a specific word should be identified
	• If that word is translated from its natural meaning, it becomes a trope
• ••Location of the trope••
	• The trope is not in the article or copula, but in the word "body"
	• The word "body" is used as a sign of the body of Christ
• ••Types of tropes in the word "body"••
	• Metaphor: the bread is like the body of Christ
	• Synecdoche: the body of Christ is put for the whole Christ
	• Metonymy: Christ is put for the benefits derived from Christ
• ••Tropes in the wine••
	• Other tropes are sufficiently manifested in the wine
• ••The end of the world••
	• The administration of the world will come to an end
	• The end of calling will be present to all the called
• ••The end of faith••
	• The end of faith is the salvation of souls
	• This is contained in the phrase "that is, in the salvation of souls"
• ••Justification and redemption••
	• The declaration of justification and redemption will be complete
	• The faithful will expect redemption in this life

Here are 10 bullets and sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Resurrection and Second Coming••
	• The second coming of Christ will be like the first in being real, visible, and apparent (Act 1:11)
	• It will be unlike the first in being with greater glory and power (Mat 24:30; Tit 2:13)
• ••The Purpose of the Second Coming••
	• It will be with greatest terror for the ungodly and greatest joy for the godly (2Thes 1:7-10)
	• The second coming will be a time of judgment, with the resurrection and last judgment serving as the final discerning between the godly and ungodly (2Cor 5:10)
• ••The Resurrection••
	• The resurrection is of the body, with the soul being reunited with the same body (12)
	• This is possible because it does not exceed God's power, which was manifested in creation (Phi 3:21)
• ••The Nature of the Resurrection••
	• It cannot be demonstrated by natural reason, but is a matter of faith (14)
	• The resurrection is not a natural process, but rather a supernatural one (15)
• ••The Cause of the Resurrection••
	• Neither the nature of the soul nor the body can be the cause of the resurrection (15)
	• The inseparable union of the soul and body is beyond the strength of nature (15)
• ••The Role of the Ministry and Sacraments••
	• The ministry, sacraments, and discipline will cease at the end of the world (7)
	• The mystical Church will remain in immediate communion with God (7)
• ••The Importance of the Second Coming••
	• The second coming should be expected with desire by all the faithful (8)
	• It is a time of great hope and expectation (8)
• ••The Glory of the Second Coming••
	• The second coming will be with great glory and power (Mat 24:30; Tit 2:13)
	• It will be a time of great joy for the godly (2Thes 1:7-10)
• ••The Judgment••
	• The resurrection and last judgment will serve as the final discerning between the godly and ungodly (2Cor 5:10)
	• Each person will receive what they have done in the body, according to what they have done (2Cor 5:10)
• ••The Power of God••
	• God has the power to subdue all things to himself (Phi 3:21)
	• This power is sufficient to raise the dead and restore the body (13)

â€¢ ••The Resurrection of the Faithful••

	• Will be a gathering of the elect

â€¢ ••The Sentence of Judgment••

â€¢ ••The Faithful as Judges••

	• Will assist in the judgment

â€¢ ••The Nature of Judgment••
	• Is not an evident event
	• Is a manifestation of Christ's power and authority

Here are 10 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Judgment of Wicked Men••

â€¢ ••The Role of Christ in Judgment••

	• This judgment is not a sign of universal redemption (Rev 20:12-15)

â€¢ ••The Fire of Purification••

	• Purgatory is not necessary before or after the Day of Judgment (Rev 20:12-15)

â€¢ ••The Elements and the World••
	• The elements will not be taken away, but changed (2 Pet 3:10, 1 Cor 15:51-52)
	• The world will be burned up, but God will remain (Heb 1:10-12)

â€¢ ••The Book of Life••
	• The Book of Life will be opened, and the dead will be judged according to their works (Rev 20:12-15)
	• The works of Christ will be accounted to believers through faith (Eph 2:8-9, Tit 3:5)

â€¢ ••The Authority of Christ••
	• Christ has been given authority to execute judgment (Joh 5:26-27)
	• Christ will remain King and Mediator forever (1 Kng 2:45, 1Tim 1:17, Heb 7:25)

â€¢ ••Purgatory••
	• Purgatory is not necessary before or after the Day of Judgment (Rev 20:12-15)
	• If souls are saved by Christ through faith, there is no need for Purgatory (Gal 3:3)

â€¢ ••The Second Death••
	• Death and Hades will be cast into the lake of fire (Rev 20:13-15)
	• This is the second death, and it will be a punishment for the wicked (Rev 20:13-15)

â€¢ ••The Day of the Lord••
	• The day of the Lord will come as a thief in the night (2 Pet 3:10)
	• The elements will melt with fervent heat, and the world will be burned up (2 Pet 3:10)

â€¢ ••The Remains of Christ••
	• Christ will remain King and Mediator forever (1 Kng 2:45, 1Tim 1:17, Heb 7:25)
	• Christ will always live to make intercession for those who come to God through Him (Heb 7:25)

Here are the 10 bullets and sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Obedience respects the Will of God••
	• It respects the Will of God that prescribes our duty (Deu 29:29)
	• It is not about performing the Will of God in a secret or powerful way, but about doing what is commanded (Rom 8:7)
• ••Obedience is a subjection to the Will of God••
	• It applies our will to fulfill the Will of God (Rom 8:7)
	• It is not subject to the Law of God, but rather submits to it (Rom 8:7)
• ••Obedience is called obedience because it makes the Will ready to commit to execution••
	• It makes the Will ready to commit to the command of God (Mat 26:39)
	• It is a readiness to do the Will of God with subjection (Rom 6:16-18)
• ••Obedience has a respect to service toward God••
	• To obey God and to serve him sound like one and the same thing (Luk 1:74)
	• To do the Will of God with subjection is to serve God (Eph 6:6-7)
• ••Obedience is the same as serving from obedience and righteousness••
	• To do the Will of God with subjection is to serve God (Rom 6:18, 22)
	• To serve God is entirely the same as serving from obedience and righteousness (Rom 6:18, 22)
• ••Obedience is the obedience of servants, not sons••
	• Although in respect to readiness of mind it ought to be the obedience of sons, it is the obedience of servants in respect to strict obligation to subjection (Rom 8:7)
• ••Obedience leads to conformity between the Will of God and ours••
	• There follows a conformity between the Will of God and ours (Rev 2:6)
	• There follows a certain express resemblance to that Divine perfection which God has revealed and propounded to be imitated by us (2Peter 1:4)
• ••Obedience is called holiness because it respects the Will of God with conformity and pure likeness••
	• Obedience is called holiness because it respects the same Will with conformity and pure likeness (1Pet 1:14-15)
• ••Obedience looks to the glory of God••
	• Obedience acknowledges the chief authority and power of God in commanding (1Cor 10:31)
	• Obedience represents the perfection of God and has a relation to it (1Pet 2:9)
• ••Obedience involves a respect to fear••
	• The Authority and Power of God is acknowledged in obedience (Rom 8:7)
	• Obedience involves a respect to the fear of God (Rom 8:7)

â€¢ ••The Relationship Between Obedience and Faith••

• ••The Will is the seat of virtue••
	• The will is the faculty that drives virtue (point 1)
	• Virtue is praise-worthy because it flows from the will (point 7)
• ••Virtue is distinct from other faculties••
	• The understanding cannot be the subject of virtue (point 8)
	• Sensitive appetites are not the subject of virtue (point 8)
• ••Virtue inclines to God••
	• Virtue is distinguished from vices and imperfections (point 8)
	• Virtue brings light to the will, but also inclines it to do right (point 8)
• ••True virtues make a person good••
	• Virtues are reciprocated by a good person and bring goodness to actions (point 9)
	• Virtues are not just habits, but principles of action (point 10)
• ••Virtue is not just about inclination, but also action••
	• Virtue inclines not only to good, but also to doing good (point 12)
	• The manner of action flows from virtue (point 12)

	• The rule of virtue is found in the Scriptures (point 13)
	• Virtue is not just a matter of human judgment, but of divine revelation (point 13)
