Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The struggle between virtue and sin••
	• The author experiences a conflict between their virtuous intentions and sinful actions (Rom 7:23)
	• This conflict is a result of the law of sin that is present in their members

â€¢ ••The distinction between intellect and virtue••
	• Intellect (knowledge, logic, etc.) is not the same as virtue
	• Virtue is demonstrated through virtuous actions, not just knowledge of what is right and good (Rom 7:4-5)

â€¢ ••The concept of the "Lesbian Rule"••
	• The Law (what is right and just) is not a fixed rule in every situation
	• It must be bent or shaped to fit the variables of each circumstance (Aristotle's concept)

â€¢ ••The qualifications for adapting the Law••
	• Not all people are qualified to adapt the Law to obtain a just outcome
	• Only moral men, with experience and the ability to recognize relevant distinctions, can shape the Law to fit the circumstances

â€¢ ••The relationship between Divinity and Ethics••
	• Divinity delivers the whole revealed will of God for directing reason, will, and life
	• Ethics is a discipline that is subordinate to Divinity

â€¢ ••The limitations of Ethics••
	• Those who think Ethics is a separate discipline from Divinity bring no valid reasons
	• Ethics is not a separate discipline, but rather a part of Divinity that teaches outward as well as inward obedience

â€¢ ••The scope of Divinity••
	• Divinity extends to a future life, not just the present life
	• A blessed life is one that is of the same life, not separate from it

â€¢ ••The subject of Divinity••
	• The subject of Divinity is not just a godly and religious man, but also one who lives temperately and justly (Tit 2:12)

â€¢ ••The relationship between moral and spiritual virtues••
	• Moral virtues are a degree of Theological virtue
	• Moral virtues are like warmth to heat, and morning light to noon light, in relation to spiritual virtues

â€¢ ••The importance of learning philosophy from the Gospel••
	• Peter Ramus believed that philosophy should be learned from the Gospel, not from Aristotle
	• Learning philosophy from Aristotle can lead to impieties that are forgotten too late.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Nature of Human Works••
	• Human works are contained within human power and nature
	• They are obtained through human art and industry
	• God is not involved in human works as an aider or workman
	• Divine providence is removed from human life

â€¢ ••The Concept of Virtue••
	• Virtue is a habit that inclines a person towards God
	• It is also called a gift, as it is given by God and inspired by the Holy Spirit
	• It is called grace, as it is freely bestowed upon us by God's favor
	• It is called fruit, as it brings perfection, profit, and sweetness

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Virtue and God••
	• Virtue is a means of approaching God
	• It is a gift from God, but also a human effort
	• It is a fruit that brings eternal life and blessedness

â€¢ ••The Seven Gifts of the Spirit••
	• The seven gifts of the Spirit are mentioned in Isaiah 11:2
	• They are: wisdom, understanding, counsel, might, knowledge, and the fear of the Lord
	• These gifts are not distinct from virtues, but rather set forth all virtues by their causes

â€¢ ••The Charismata (Graces)••
	• Charismata are gifts that are distinct from virtues
	• They are mentioned in 1 Corinthians 12:4
	• They set forth particular virtues or all virtues jointly

â€¢ ••The Fruits of the Spirit••
	• The fruits of the Spirit are mentioned in Galatians 5:22
	• They are: love, joy, peace, long-suffering, kindness, goodness, faith, meekness, and temperance
	• They are compared to virtues, as they are fruits that bring profit and sweetness

â€¢ ••The Nature of Virtues••
	• Virtues are fruits that bring profit and sweetness
	• They are required and expected by God
	• They bring profit and sweetness to the person who possesses them

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Holiness and Fruits••
	• Holiness is called a fruit of the Holy Spirit
	• It is also called our fruit, as it brings profit and sweetness to us

â€¢ ••The Concept of Blessedness••
	• Blessedness is the hope of eternal life
	• It is the result of possessing virtues and living a virtuous life

â€¢ ••The Futility of Distinguishing between Gifts and Virtues••
	• Some people try to distinguish between gifts and virtues
	• However, this distinction is not necessary, as virtues and gifts are closely related

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Beatitudes••
	• There are eight beatitudes in the Sermon of Christ (Matthew 5)
	• They describe the characteristics of blessed individuals
	• The beatitudes are not separate virtues, but rather different aspects of a single virtue

â€¢ ••The Cardinal Virtues••
	• There are four cardinal virtues: Justice, Prudence, Fortitude, and Temperance
	• These virtues are not separate kinds of virtues, but rather conditions that are necessary for virtue

â€¢ ••Justice••
	• The first condition of virtue, which involves doing what is right and giving each person their due
	• It is the rectitude of virtue and involves truth, honesty, and purity

â€¢ ••Prudence••
	• The second condition of virtue, which involves using reason to discover what is right and directing one's actions accordingly
	• It involves understanding, knowledge, and wisdom
	• It is opposed to foolishness, ignorance, and vanity

â€¢ ••Prudence in Action••
	• Prudence should be exercised with circumspection and diligence
	• It involves taking heed and being watchful, as commended in Scripture
	• It also involves making wise choices and prioritizing greater duties over lesser ones

â€¢ ••Opposites of Prudence••
	• Foolishness: a lack of understanding and wisdom
	• Ignorance: a lack of knowledge and understanding
	• Vanity: a lack of judgment and discernment

â€¢ ••Scriptural References••
	• Philippians 4:8: a description of virtue as involving truth, justice, and purity
	• Colossians 1:9: Prudence is described as spiritual understanding and wisdom
	• 1 Corinthians 6:12 and 10:23: Prudence involves understanding what is lawful and expedient

â€¢ ••The Importance of Prudence••
	• Prudence is essential for virtue and involves using reason to discover what is right
	• It is opposed to foolishness, ignorance, and vanity, and involves making wise choices

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Prudence and Virtue••
	• Prudence is a condition of virtue that involves using reason to discover what is right
	• It is not a separate virtue, but rather a necessary condition for virtue

â€¢ ••The Role of Prudence in the Christian Life••
	• Prudence is essential for living a virtuous life and involves using reason to discover what is right
	• It involves taking heed and being watchful, making wise choices, and prioritizing greater duties over lesser ones.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Virtue and Conditions••
	• Virtue is a firm persisting in doing rightly
	• There are four conditions of virtue: Justice, Prudence, Fortitude, and Temperance

â€¢ ••Justice (Condition 1)••
	• A firm persisting in doing rightly
	• Enduring and overcoming difficulties
	• Opposed to injustice and wickedness

â€¢ ••Prudence (Condition 2)••
	• Directs and frees virtue from error
	• Requires discernment and wisdom
	• Opposed to foolishness and ignorance

â€¢ ••Fortitude (Condition 3)••
	• Strengthens virtue against inconveniences
	• Requires confidence, perseverance, and endurance
	• Opposed to fear, fainting, and weariness

â€¢ ••Temperance (Condition 4)••
	• Makes virtue pure and undefiled
	• Assuages and restrains desires that divert from good
	• Requires sobriety, purity, and sincerity

â€¢ ••Relationship between Conditions••
	• Justice orders and constitutes virtue
	• Prudence directs and frees virtue from error
	• Fortitude strengthens virtue against inconveniences
	• Temperance makes virtue pure and defends it against allurements

â€¢ ••Scriptural References••
	• 1 Corinthians 6:12, 10:23, 2:15
	• Philippians 1:9, 4:17
	• Ephesians 4:17, 1:28
	• Matthew 25:5, 13
	• Hebrews 3:16, 12:3, 12

â€¢ ••General Affection••
	• A necessary attribute or disposition of the mind and heart
	• Affects our will and our affection for doing good
	• Equated with a condition of virtue

â€¢ ••Cardinal Virtues••
	• Justice, Prudence, Fortitude, and Temperance
	• Each virtue has a necessary attribute or disposition (condition)

â€¢ ••Importance of Virtue••
	• Virtue is essential for a good and holy life
	• Requires a combination of the four conditions: Justice, Prudence, Fortitude, and Temperance

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Virtue and Courage••
	• Virtue is associated with power, strength, wealth, and bravery (Proverbs 31:10)
	• The Church is described as a woman of strength, valor, and courage (John Gill's commentary on Proverbs 31:10)

â€¢ ••Strengthening with Might••
	• The Spirit of Christ strengthens believers with might in the inner man (Ephesians 3:16)
	• This strengthening enables believers to fight the Lord's battles and withstand every enemy

â€¢ ••Boldness and Endurance••
	• Believers are to speak the word of God with all boldness (Acts 4:29)
	• Endurance (hupomone) is a key virtue for believers to persevere in their faith

â€¢ ••Vigilance and Adversity••
	• Believers must be sober and vigilant because the devil is a roaring lion seeking to devour them (1 Peter 5:8)
	• The devil is a powerful adversary, but believers can overcome him through faith and virtue

â€¢ ••Love and Faith••
	• The purpose of the commandment is love from a pure heart, good conscience, and sincere faith (1 Timothy 1:5)
	• Jesus is the perfect example of love and faith, having committed no sin and being deceitful in His mouth (1 Peter 2:22)

â€¢ ••Virtue and Knowledge••
	• Believers are to add virtue, knowledge, self-control, perseverance, godliness, brotherly kindness, and love to their faith (2 Peter 1:5-7)
	• These virtues are essential for believers to grow in their faith and become more like Christ

â€¢ ••Virtue and Distribution••
	• Virtue can be distributed into various categories, such as prudence, temperance, fortitude, and justice
	• Each of these virtues has its own unique characteristics and applications

â€¢ ••Justice and Fortitude••
	• Justice is often associated with fairness and righteousness in dealings with others
	• Fortitude is often associated with bravery and courage in the face of danger or adversity

â€¢ ••Temperance and Pleasure••
	• Temperance is often associated with self-control and moderation in the face of sensual pleasures
	• Believers must be careful to avoid the allurements of pleasure and stay focused on their faith

â€¢ ••Virtue and Conformity••
	• The form of virtue is not found in mediocrity, but rather in conformity to God's rule
	• Virtue is a habit that is formed through practice and conformity to God's will

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Virtue and Excess••
	• Virtue cannot be over-intended or excessive
	• Excess can occur in material things or circumstances of operation
	• Excess is not the same as virtue, but rather a deviation from it

â€¢ ••The Mean••
	• The mean is a conformity to the rule or measure of virtues
	• Virtues have certain measures and bounds that define their nature
	• Mediocrity is not the form of virtues, but rather a subject matter

â€¢ ••Virtues and the Mean••
	• Virtues that consist in the middle between two vicious habits are not virtues because of their location, but because they follow a rule
	• Mediocrity is a subject matter, not a perfecting form

â€¢ ••Virtues and Excess••
	• Virtues cannot be excessive, as this would be a deviation from their nature
	• Excess can occur in virtues, but this is not the same as virtue

â€¢ ••Love of God••
	• The love of God is not praised for being mediocre, but for being most ardent
	• The measure of virtues can be without measure in certain cases

â€¢ ••Virtues and Their Nature••
	• Virtues have a specific nature that defines their measure
	• Someone who gives too much is not liberal, but rather defective in their virtue

â€¢ ••Justice and Self-Opinion••
	• Justice is not about being overly just, but rather about true virtue
	• Self-opinion can lead to attributing too much to oneself and not enough to true virtue

â€¢ ••Degrees of Virtue••
	• There are no degrees in any particular kind of virtue, if considered in itself and in its extent
	• Virtue extends itself to all things contained within its object

â€¢ ••Increasing Virtue••
	• Virtues are increased by daily use and exercise, but only in solid virtues that proceed from sanctifying grace
	• Daily use and exercise can lead to a greater habit of virtue and a greater gift of God

â€¢ ••Virtue and Disposition••
	• Virtue can be stronger in one person than another due to a more apt disposition by nature
	• Virtue can also be stronger due to a greater habit, better judgment, or a greater gift of God

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••1. The Role of Exercise in Virtue••
• Exercise is a disposing cause for virtue
• It is also a procuring cause by virtue of God's promise
• Exercise does not primarily produce virtue, but rather facilitates it

••2. The Effect of Vicious Acts on Virtue••
• Vicious acts lessen virtues
• This lessening is due to the disposition and merit of the virtues
• The more frequent and intense the vicious acts, the more virtues are diminished

••3. Types of Virtues••
• Virtues are distinguished into human and heroic
• They are also categorized as purging, purged, and exemplary
• Virtues are distinguished between infants and men of ripe age

••4. The Communion of Virtues••
• Virtues are connected and subordinate to one another
• This connection is due to:
	• Shared source (God)
	• Shared end and intention (God's glory)
	• Mutual helping and endeavour

••5. The Distinction between Virtues••
• Virtues are not essentially and intrinsically connected
• Each virtue has its own essence and does not necessarily depend on others

••6. Subordination of Virtues••
• Virtues are subordinate to one another
• This subordination is due to:
	• One virtue being a means to an end for another (e.g. Religion commands Justice)
	• One virtue being a cause to the effect of another (e.g. Religion brings forth Justice)

••7. The Role of Prudence in Virtue••
• Prudence directs the act of virtue
• Prudence depends on a superior virtue for its effectual force and authority

••8. The Nature of Good Works••
• A good work is an operation flowing from a disposition of virtue
• A good work is pleasing to God and has a good efficient (will) and good matter (subject)

••9. The Requirements of Good Works••
• A good work requires a good efficient (will) and a good matter (subject)
• The efficient is the will that is well-disposed and working from true virtue

••10. The Importance of Virtue••
• Virtue is essential for good works
• Virtue is the disposition that brings forth good things (Matthew 12:35)

Here are the 10 bullets and sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Nature of Good Actions••
	• A good action is one that is commended by God (Mat 15:9)
	• A good end is the glory of God and things that tend to his glory (1Cor 10:31)
• ••The End and Object of Actions••
	• The end and object of actions are often the same, especially in the intention and election of the will
	• Acts can be concerned with the end itself (e.g. desiring, willing, loving) or with things that tend to the end
• ••The Importance of Intention••
	• A good intention is necessary for a good action, but not sufficient on its own
	• An evil intention always makes an action evil
• ••The Role of God's Will••
	• A good action must agree with the revealed Will of God
	• The Will of God informs an action of man as far as it is apprehended by reason
• ••The Conscience as a Moral Guide••
	• The conscience of man is a subordinate rule of moral actions
	• An action must agree with a right conscience, but an erring or doubtful conscience must be laid down before acting against it
• ••The Importance of Circumstances••
	• All circumstances are good or evil, and affect the goodness or evilness of an action
	• Circumstances pass into the nature of an object when referred to the act of the will
• ••The Nature of Circumstances••
	• Circumstances can be referred to the act of the will or to other faculties and acts
	• The end itself is a circumstance, even if not in respect to the will
• ••The Impact of Circumstances on Actions••
	• Changing circumstances can change the act of the will
	• Circumstances can make an action good or evil, depending on their nature
• ••The Relationship between Actions and Faculties••
	• The will is the primary faculty concerned with good actions
	• Other faculties and acts are also involved in good actions, but in a secondary way
• ••The Importance of Considering All Faculties and Acts••
	• All faculties and acts must be considered when evaluating the goodness or evilness of an action

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Acts are classified as good, evil, or indifferent••
	• An act is indifferent if it doesn't pertain to God's will
	• An indifferent act can be good or evil depending on its end and circumstances

â€¢ ••Indifferent acts can be good or evil••
	• An indifferent act can be good if it's directed to a due end and conforms to God's will
	• An indifferent act can be evil if it's not rightly directed and dissents from God's will

â€¢ ••Acts that seem evil can be good••
	• Acts that seem evil, such as killing a man, can be good if they're done for a good reason (e.g. killing a murderer)
	• These acts should be referred to as indifferent, as they only seem to have evil in themselves

â€¢ ••The goodness or evilness of an act depends on its object and circumstances••
	• Slaying the innocent is evil, while slaying the guilty justly is good
	• Delivering the guilty is evil, while delivering the innocent is good

â€¢ ••Good works are imperfect and impure••
	• Our good works are imperfect and impure in themselves
	• They're not accepted by God except through Christ

â€¢ ••Merit is not obtained through good works••
	• Our good works don't merit a reward through justice
	• The reward we receive is through God's grace

â€¢ ••The fruits of grace are put on our account••
	• Although our good works are imperfect, the fruits of grace are put on our account
	• This gives us the certainty of God's gift

â€¢ ••The action of virtue is either inward or outward••
	• The internal action is properly of the will itself
	• The external action is of another faculty, such as the understanding or executive power

â€¢ ••Internal and external acts have different characteristics••
	• An internal act can't remain the same in its nature unless it remains the same in its manners
	• An external act can remain the same in its nature but become another nature in its manners

â€¢ ••Goodness or evilness is intrinsic to internal acts••
	• The goodness or evilness of an internal act is intrinsic to it
	• An external act can have the same goodness or evilness as its internal act, but it can also change in its nature and manners.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Nature of Good and Evil••
	• Goodness and evilness depend on the will and intention behind an action.
	• The internal act of virtue tends to an external act and produces it.
• ••The Relationship between Will and Action••
	• The will is the primary factor in determining the goodness or evilness of an action.
	• The external act is a consequence of the internal act, but does not increase the goodness or evilness of the act.
• ••The Order of Existence••
	• The goodness or evilness of an act exists first in the internal act, and then in the external act.
	• The external act is the cause of the inward act in the order of intention, and the inward act is the cause of the outward act in the order of execution.
• ••The Importance of Intention••
	• The intention behind an action determines its goodness or evilness.
	• The end or purpose of an action is also a factor in determining its goodness or evilness.
• ••Hypocrisy and Obedience••
	• Obedience that appears in outward actions without the inward act is hypocrisy.
	• Inward obedience without outward action is still true obedience.
• ••Judging Actions••
	• We should not judge an action good or evil by the event.
	• The inward sin is as great as the outward sin before the tribunal of God.
• ••The Role of Faith••
	• Faith is the helper of works, and brings them to their end.
	• Faith is necessary for the completion of good works.
• ••The Relationship between Will and Reward••
	• The wages of sin is death, but the gift of God is eternal life in Christ Jesus our Lord.
	• We should rejoice and be glad when we suffer persecution for righteousness' sake.
• ••The Importance of Performance••
	• We should perform our good intentions in deed, not just in will.
	• The readiness to will should be accompanied by a performance of the action.
• ••The Relationship between Will and Goodness••
	• The goodness of an action depends on the will and intention behind it.
	• The external act is a consequence of the internal act, but does not increase the goodness of the act.

â€¢ ••Inward vs. outward obedience••

	• The Papists' concept of "works of supererogation" is considered idle and foolish (1Cor 6:20)

â€¢ ••The importance of external obedience••
	• Inward obedience is not sufficient by itself (Mat 5:28, Rom 12:1)

	• These works are considered the "dotings" of idle men who do not understand the Law or the Gospel

â€¢ ••The imperfection of even the best works••
	• Even the best works of the faithful contain imperfection that needs remission (Rom 3:23)
	• However, the works themselves are not considered sins

â€¢ ••The distinction between Religion and Justice••
	• Observance of God's commands is either Religion or Justice (Mat 22:37, Rom 1:18)
	• This distinction is made by God in the Decalogue and is expressed in different words in various scriptures

â€¢ ••The distribution of obedience••
	• Obedience can be distributed into Piety and Justice (Rom 1:18, Tit 2:12)
	• Alternatively, it can be distributed into holiness and righteousness (Luk 1:75, Eph 4:24)

â€¢ ••The meaning of Religion and Justice••
	• Religion is a general term that contains all duties owed to God (Act 26:5, Jas 1:26-27)
	• Justice is a distinct way of fulfilling these duties, as emphasized in the Epistle to the Hebrews

â€¢ ••The concept of "holiness and righteousness"••
	• This distribution is used to describe the Christian life (Luk 1:75, Eph 4:24)
	• It has the same meaning as the distribution into love towards God and love towards our neighbor

â€¢ ••The importance of denying worldly lusts••
	• Temperance denotes the manner or means of performing righteous and pious works (Tit 2:12)
	• Denying worldly lusts is essential to living a Christian life (Tit 2:12)

• ••The First Commandment••
	• The first and great commandment is to love God (Matthew 22:37)

	• We should live in holiness and righteousness before God (Luke 1:75)
• ••True Religion••
	• True religion is not just about outward actions, but about a genuine relationship with God (James 1:26-27)
	• True religion involves visiting orphans and widows in their trouble and keeping oneself unspotted from the world (James 1:27)
• ••The Definition of Religion••
	• Religion is the observance of things that directly pertain to bringing honor to God (Romans 1:21)
	• Religion involves binding ourselves to God through obedience and devotion
• ••The Importance of Religion••
	• Religion has the first place in observance because it is the foundation of all other virtues
	• Righteousness towards men must be performed through the force and virtue of religion
• ••The Role of Religion in Justice••
	• Religion has command over the acts of justice and is the cause of them
	• Justice is sometimes called religion in the Scriptures because it is a sign of true religion
• ••The Offices of Religion••
	• The offices of religion are the first and highest offices
	• They are the first in order and should be taken care of in the first place
• ••Seeking God First••
	• We should seek God first in all things (Matthew 6:33)
	• This involves caring for the offices of religion above all else

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Duties of Religion vs. Duties of Justice••
	• Duties of Religion should be performed with more intent and fervor than duties of Justice.
	• This is because the rule to love God with all one's heart, soul, and thought (Matthew 22:37) pertains to Religion.

â€¢ ••Importance of Intention in Religion••
	• Intention is crucial in performing duties of Religion.
	• One can love their neighbor with too much intention, but not God, as God can be loved with all one's intention.

â€¢ ••Prioritizing Duties of Piety••
	• If duties of piety and justice cannot be performed together, duties of piety should be preferred.
	• This is because God's business (Matthew 12:46-48, Luke 2:49) takes precedence over human relationships.

â€¢ ••Equal Comparison between Duties••
	• An equal comparison between duties of piety and justice is when a just proportion is observed.
	• This means considering the greater to the greatest and the lesser to the least.

â€¢ ••Inward Affection vs. Outward Work••
	• God is more worshipped with inward affection than with outward work.
	• However, men need the outward work of Religion, and it may be omitted to fulfill a necessary work of Justice and mercy.

â€¢ ••Omitting External Work for a Necessary Work••
	• Religion commands us to omit an external work to perform a necessary work of Justice and mercy.
	• This does not violate Religion, as it is a means to fulfill a greater good.

â€¢ ••Immediate Object of Religion••
	• The immediate object of Religion is God.
	• No duty of Religion can be referred to any other object without injuring God.

â€¢ ••Respect for God's Excellency••
	• The respect under which Religion considers God is God's Divine excellency, which shines forth in His sufficiency and efficiency.
	• This is not a single attribute, but a perfection arising from all God's attributes.

â€¢ ••Attributes of God and Religion••
	• All God's attributes have some power to beget Religion in us.
	• The Scriptures sometimes refer to Mercy, Justice, and other attributes as the special respect of Religion.

â€¢ ••Faith and Religion••
	• Religion immediately flows from Faith, which believes in God as the sufficient and efficient cause of life.
	• This is what is meant by the usual statement that Religion respects God as the first beginning and supreme Lord of life.

	• The will is necessary for faith to occur (20)

••1. The Helmet of Salvation••
• The helmet of salvation is a spiritual armor that protects our minds and hearts.
• It is the word of God, which is the sword of the Spirit (Eph 6:17).

••2. The Breastplate of Faith and Love••
• The breastplate of faith and love is a spiritual armor that protects our hearts and emotions.
• It is the foundation of hope, and without it, we would have no hope (1Thes 5:8).

••3. The Hope of Salvation••
• The hope of salvation is a spiritual expectation of good things to come.
• It is grounded on faith, which beholds God in the promises (1Cor 13:12).

••4. The Difference between Faith and Hope••
• Faith apprehends what is promised, while hope expects what is promised.
• The difference between faith and hope is in respect to what is present and what is to come.

••5. The Certainty of Faith and Hope••
• The Papists make a distinction between faith and hope, but it is empty and vain.
• Faith and hope are one and the same certainty, and there is no difference between them.

••6. Hope in Scripture••
• In Scripture, especially in the Old Testament, hope is often put for faith.
• This is because faith and hope are closely related and often used interchangeably.

••7. The Expectation of Angels and Just Men••
• The expectation of angels and just men in heaven differs from our hope, not because one is certain and the other is uncertain.
• Their expectation is grounded on open sight, while ours is grounded on faith, which beholds God in the promises.

••8. The Imperfection of Our Hope••
• Our hope is an imperfect expectation, while their expectation is perfect.
• Our hope is with labor and contention, while theirs is without difficulty.

••9. The Perfection of Faith and Hope••
• Although faith and hope are said to be abolished in the life to come, this should not be understood as though they ceased in respect to their essence.
• Faith and hope are to be perfected in respect to their essence.

••10. Christian Confidence••
• Christian confidence is nothing but hope confirmed.
• It refers to the theological virtue of hope, which is one of the three virtues listed in 1Cor 13:13.

••11. The Fruit of Hope••
• The natural fruit of hope is joy and delight in God.
• This is because hope respects the greatest good, which is certainly to come.

••12. The Manner of Hope••
• The manner of hope depends on the respect of the object by which it is said to be and to come.
• Hope is not about those things which are seen, but about those things which are promised.

••13. The Companion of Hope••
• The fruit and companion of hope is patience towards God.
• Patience is a virtue that enables us to wait for the good things that are to come.

••14. The Abolition of Faith and Hope••
• Although faith and hope are said to be abolished in the life to come, this should not be understood as though they ceased in respect to their essence.
• Faith and hope are to be perfected in respect to their essence.

••15. The Relationship between Hope and Joy••
• Hope is the foundation of joy, and joy is the fruit of hope.
• When we have hope, we rejoice in the good things that are to come.

••16. The Relationship between Hope and Patience••
• Patience is the companion of hope, and hope is the foundation of patience.
• When we have hope, we are patient towards God, waiting for the good things that are to come.

••17. The Relationship between Hope and Salvation••
• We are saved by hope, which is the expectation of good things to come.
• Hope is the means by which we are saved, and it is a gift from God.

• ••Prayer Arises from Faith••

	• Prayer is concerned with our will, while hearing the word is concerned with God's will
	• Prayer involves offering our will to God, while hearing the word involves receiving God's will
• ••The Will in Prayer••
	• The will in prayer is not just a simple desire, but a representation of our desire to God
	• This representation involves a desire to obtain something from God and a will to seek it from him
• ••The Representation of the Will••
	• The representation of the will is done first and essentially in the will itself
	• This involves converting to God and stretching out our inclination and desire to him
• ••The Representation of the Will in Scripture••
	• In Scripture, the prayers of the godly are called desires and unspeakable groans
	• This highlights the importance of the will in prayer
• ••The Acts of the Mind in Prayer••
	• Prayer involves an act of the will, as well as an antecedent act of the mind and a consequent act of the mind
	• The antecedent act of the mind involves understanding what, whom, for what, and how to pray
	• The consequent act of the mind involves conceiving and expressing the prayer itself
• ••Attention in Prayer••
	• Prayer requires attention to God, the thing for which we pray, and the prayer itself
	• We must pray not only with the Spirit, but also with understanding

	• We must pray instantly, because our prayer is a necessary means for God's glory and our good

â€¢ ••Prayer and its components••
	• Prayer involves confession and a promise made to God
	• These two elements are essential in every acceptable prayer to God

â€¢ ••The purpose of confession••
	• Confession is a humble and penitential acknowledgement of our offense and guilt
	• It is necessary to obtain the glory of God and to be disposed to receive God's grace

â€¢ ••The manner of confession••
	• Confession can be general or specific, depending on the nature of the sin
	• Known sins should be confessed according to their nature and gravity

â€¢ ••The purpose of a promise••
	• A promise in prayer testifies to a purpose that is agreeable to God
	• It is a determination of the will to prosecute a particular goal or desire

â€¢ ••The nature of a promise••
	• A promise should be a determination to prosecute a particular goal or desire
	• It should be made with an earnest endeavour and a willingness to take necessary steps

â€¢ ••The distinction between a promise and a vow••
	• A promise is a general determination to prosecute a particular goal or desire
	• A vow is a specific promise made to God, distinct from other circumstances

â€¢ ••The characteristics of a vow••
	• A vow must be about a thing that is neither impossible nor necessary
	• It must be about a thing that is lawful and good in all circumstances
	• It must be referred only to God as the object of the vow

â€¢ ••The types of prayer••
	• Prayer can be ejaculatory (short and brief) or continued (long and sustained)
	• Ejectulatory prayer should be more frequent and cannot be hindered by other activities

â€¢ ••The importance of prayer••
	• Prayer is a means of flying to the mercy of God and receiving His grace
	• It is a way of acknowledging our misery and destitution, and seeking God's help

â€¢ ••The relationship between prayer and God's glory••
	• Prayer is a means of obtaining the glory of God and being disposed to receive His grace
	• It is a way of acknowledging God's sovereignty and seeking His honour.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Prayer and Petition••
	• Prayer is a formal act of Faith and Hope by which we receive all good things from God.
	• Petition is a specific type of prayer that asks for something from God.
• ••Nature of Petition••
	• Petition respects God's mercy and kindness, not his justice.
	• It is a good work, but not in the sense that it earns God's favor.
• ••Types of Petition••
	• Appreciation: petitioning for good things to be communicated.
	• Deprecation: petitioning for evil things to be removed.
• ••Purpose of Petition••
	• To receive good things from God.
	• To avert or remove evil or disaster.
• ••Manner of Petition••
	• Should be humble and submissive.
	• Should not prescribe the manner or time of God's response.
• ••Object of Petition••
	• Should be things necessary for God's glory and our salvation.
	• May include things that make for the celebration of God's glory and goodness.
• ••Scope of Petition••
	• Should include not only ourselves, but also others who may benefit from God's goodness.
• ••Scriptural Examples••
	• The Patriarchs and Prophets prayed and promised in the name of the Lord.
	• The Psalms and other scriptures provide examples of prayer and petition.
• ••Limitations of Petition••
	• Should not be used to pray for the dead, as there is no scriptural precedent.
	• Should not be used to pray for all people collectively, as God has determined their fate.
• ••Intercession and Prayer••
	• Intercession is a type of petition that involves asking for something on behalf of others.
	• Prayer should not be used to reject or exclude others from communion.

• ••What is an Oath?••
	• A requesting of God's testimony to confirm the truth of our testimony
	• A way to confirm the truth of a statement or promise
• ••Why was an Oath necessary?••
	• After the fall of man, people lost credit for their simple testimony
	• People also lost credit for the testimony of others
	• God himself had to demean himself to confirm his testimonies by oath
• ••God's role in an Oath••
	• God is the highest truth and cannot deceive or be deceived
	• God is the rewarder of truth and falsehood
	• God is the living God who provides for all things by an admirable providence
• ••Who can be sworn by?••
	• Only God can be sworn by
	• No creature can be sworn by
	• Swearing by a creature is not proper worship
• ••Why can't God swear by anyone greater?••
	• God has no greater or superior Judge
	• God's perfection of confirmation is found in his testimonies
• ••What is the purpose of an Oath?••
	• To confirm the truth of a statement or promise
	• To give credit to the testimony of others
• ••How should we use Oaths?••
	• Only use Oaths where human necessity requires
	• Only use Oaths where a weighty and just cause is in hand
• ••What is the relationship between Oaths and worship?••
	• An Oath is not directly worship, but it is a way to give honor to God
	• The request made in an Oath is worship
• ••What does Scripture say about Oaths?••
	• Scripture says that God confirmed his promise to Abraham by an oath (Heb 6:13-17)
	• Scripture says that we should not swear by heaven, earth, or Jerusalem (Mat 5:34-35)
	• Scripture says that we should not swear by the temple or heaven (Mat 23:21-22)
	• Scripture says that we should not swear by anything, but rather let our "Yes" be "Yes" and our "No" be "No" (Jas 5:12)
• ••What is the significance of taking Oaths in God's name?••
	• Taking Oaths in God's name is a way to worship and serve God
	• It is a way to acknowledge God as the Author of truth and the rewarder of truth and falsehood.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Nature of Oaths••
	• Oaths are a request for God's testimony and involve swearing by the name of the Lord.
	• Those who swear make themselves subject to God's vengeance and curse if they give false testimony.
• ••The Form of Swearing••
	• A common form of swearing in the Old Testament is "God do so to me, and more also."
	• This form contains a general or indefinite curse, allowing God to inflict evil as He sees fit.
• ••The Importance of Oaths••
	• Oaths involve great religion and cannot be used with equivocation or mental reservation.
	• Using oaths in a way that mocks God's judgment is considered impious.
• ••The Binding Nature of Oaths••
	• An oath is a testimony about a thing already done or to be done.
	• There can be no release or commuting from an oath, except in cases where the oath is unlawful.
• ••Types of Oaths••
	• Assertory oaths are about a thing past or present and confirm the truth of the thing done.
	• Promissory oaths are about a thing to come and involve a comminatory (a warning of punishment).
• ••Assertory Oaths••
	• Assertory oaths do not bind someone to do anything, but only confirm the truth of the thing done.
	• They respect the judgment of the one who swears and are considered true even if they differ from the thing itself.
• ••Promissory Oaths••
	• Promissory oaths have the force of an assertory oath and testify to a present firm intention of the mind.
	• They bind us to do what we declared was intended, but only so far as we can bind ourselves.
• ••The Limitations of Oaths••
	• Oaths can only bind us to do what is lawful and possible.
	• We can only bind ourselves to do what we can actually perform.
• ••The Significance of Oaths••
	• Oaths are a serious matter and involve great religion.
	• Using oaths in a way that mocks God's judgment is considered impious.
• ••The Roman Approach to Oaths••
	• The Romans used a considered wording when being sworn in, saying "I think" when being sworn in.
	• This approach acknowledges the importance of oaths and the need for careful consideration.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Oaths and their binding nature••
	• An oath binds us to fulfill it, even if it's unfavorable or damaging (Jos 9:19, Psa 15:4)
	• However, an oath against God's commands does not bind (Psa 15:4)

â€¢ ••Exceptions to the rule••
	• An oath made against God's commands may still bind in certain circumstances (e.g., when Jews swore to be subject to strangers)
	• A promissory oath ceases to bind if the foundation on which it was grounded is removed or taken away

â€¢ ••The nature of oaths for Christians••
	• Oaths are lawful and honest for Christians because they are based on natural law and moral law (which is not abrogated)
	• Oaths pertain to God's honor, and charity pertains to our neighbor

â€¢ ••Examples of oaths in the Bible••
	• There are commendable examples of oaths used in the New Testament (2Cor 1:23, Rev 10:6)
	• Christ does not condemn every oath, but only those that are rash, indirect, or made by creatures (Mat 5:33-37)

â€¢ ••The meaning of "swearing"••
	• Swearing is not condemned in the Bible, but rather the abuse of oaths (Jas 5:12)
	• The phrase "swear not at all" (Mat 5:34) is meant to be understood as a whole sentence, not separated from the following words

â€¢ ••The meaning of "amen"••
	• Amen is not a form of swearing, but rather a grave asseveration (Heb 6:14)
	• The word "amen" does not appear in the original Greek or Hebrew texts of Gen 22:16-17

â€¢ ••The nature of oaths in the Old Testament••
	• Oaths were used in the Old Testament to confirm promises and agreements (e.g., Jos 9:19)
	• Oaths were often made to God, and were considered binding (e.g., Psa 15:4)

â€¢ ••The relationship between oaths and God's commands••
	• An oath against God's commands does not bind, because it would be a bond of iniquity (Psa 15:4)
	• However, an oath made in some manner against God's commands may still bind in certain circumstances

â€¢ ••The importance of honesty in oaths••
	• Oaths should be made with honesty and integrity, and should not be used to deceive or manipulate others
	• Christians should strive to be truthful and transparent in their oaths and promises

â€¢ ••The role of oaths in Christian life••
	• Oaths can be a powerful tool for confirming promises and agreements in Christian life
	• However, oaths should be used wisely and with caution, and should not be used to bind oneself to something that is contrary to God's will.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Interpreting Oaths••
	• Oaths should be interpreted according to the meaning of the person who swore it.
	• If the person's intention is unclear, the oath should be interpreted according to their conscience.

â€¢ ••Perjury••
	• A perjured person is one who swears against their conscience or knowingly breaks a lawful oath.
	• Perjury is a serious offense, but it's not the only way to break an oath.

â€¢ ••Keeping Oaths••
	• A lawful oath should be kept, even to enemies, thieves, and pirates.
	• Respect for persons does not make an oath unlawful, but it can affect its validity.

â€¢ ••Extorted Oaths••
	• An oath extorted by fear is still binding, as long as it's not entirely involuntary.
	• Acts done under duress can still be considered voluntary if they're done with counsel.

â€¢ ••Reason and Oaths••
	• People who lack reason cannot understand the nature of an oath and are not capable of making one.
	• This principle is similar to the concept of mental capacity in contracts.

â€¢ ••Oaths to False Gods••
	• Requiring an oath from someone who swears by false gods is not inherently sinful.
	• This is illustrated in Genesis 31:53, where Jacob swears by the gods of his father Isaac.

â€¢ ••Christian Oaths••
	• A Christian's oath of innocence, which cannot be disputed, should end controversies related to it.
	• This principle is based on Exodus 22:11 and Hebrews 6:16.

â€¢ ••Simple and Solemn Oaths••
	• A simple oath made in words is just as binding as a solemn oath.
	• The formality of an oath is not as important as its content and intention.

â€¢ ••Signs of Oaths••
	• Touching or kissing a book, or lifting up one's hand, can be considered signs of consent to swear an oath.
	• These signs are equivalent to a solemn oath and should be taken seriously.

â€¢ ••Adjuring and Oaths••
	• Adjuring someone to swear an oath or to adopt a particular religion is a way of drawing them into a commitment.
	• This principle is illustrated in Genesis 24:8 and Numbers 5:21.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Adjuration and Oaths••
	• Adjuration refers to the act of requiring someone to take an oath
	• It is typically used by those with power to require an oath from others
	• However, it can also be used by inferiors to superiors or among equals

â€¢ ••Exercising Command over Devils••
	• Exercising adjuration over devils is not lawful unless one has received special power from God
	• This is because devils are not to be commanded or controlled by humans

â€¢ ••Superstitious Practices••
	• Exorcisms used before baptism are considered superstitious
	• Consecrating lifeless things for supernatural operations is also considered a superstitious enchantment

â€¢ ••Oath of Inquisition••
	• Requiring someone to accuse themselves of a crime is not grounded in Scripture and is against the law of nature
	• This type of oath is used in the Oath of Inquisition or Ex officio

â€¢ ••Indefinite Adjuration••
	• Requiring someone to answer to anything that will be demanded is not allowed
	• This is because it is an open-ended or unlimited request

â€¢ ••The Nature of a Lot••
	• A Lot is a request for Divine testimony to decide a controversy
	• It is a request that expects the use it serves is from God alone

â€¢ ••Defining a Lot••
	• A Lot is defined by contingency to avoid considering it as an efficient cause
	• Contingency refers to the idea that an event may or may not happen

â€¢ ••Types of Contingency••
	• There are three degrees of contingent things: some happen often, some seldom, and some equally on either part
	• A Lot is considered a mere contingency, not an efficient cause

â€¢ ••Examples of Lots••
	• A Lot can depend on whether birds will fly or some other such effect
	• A Lot can also depend on a cause that works of its own power

â€¢ ••Scriptural References••
	• Jesus was put under oath by the high priest to tell if He was the Christ, the Son of God (Matthew 26:63)
	• The apostle Paul charges the Thessalonians to read his epistle to all the holy brethren (1 Thessalonians 5:27)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Nature of a Lot••
	• A Lot is a means of determining a question or controversy
	• It is sought through mere contingency, which is undetermined
• ••The Role of God in a Lot••
	• The determination of a Lot is from the nature of the thing, which is always sought from a higher power
	• This higher power is either the true God or a feigned power (fortune)
• ••The Teaching of Divines••
	• Divines teach that there is a certain extraordinary providence of God set over all Lots
	• This providence is not always directly respected by those who use Lots
• ••The Judgment of a Lot••
	• In a Lot, some judgment is expected, but there is no power to give judgment in contingent events
	• The judgment is expected from God's providence
• ••The Limitations of Contingency••
	• Mere contingency cannot be a principal cause in deciding any question
	• Man cannot direct a contingent event to attain a certain end
• ••The Order of Proceeding••
	• When men desire a question to be determined, they seek it from a superior power
	• The consideration of a Lot agrees with this manner of proceeding
• ••The Nature of Counsel••
	• Counsel that intends a certain end and scope by certain reason cannot subject its action to fortune or mere contingency
	• Such counsel would be without knowledge and undetermined indifference would be a means of determining a cause
• ••Scriptural Teaching••
	• The action of every man surrounding a Lot is bounded in mere contingency (Pro 16:33)
	• The whole judgment is referred to God, and its entire disposition is from the Lord
• ••The Hebrew Word Mischphath••
	• The Hebrew word Mischphath is sometimes used to signify something beside judgment
	• However, it must always be taken according to the subject matter
• ••The Use of a Lot••
	• A Lot should not be used rashly, for sporadic use can lead to misinterpretation and misuse

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Purpose of Lots••
	• Not to be used for trivial or vain matters
	• Not to be used without special revelation
	• Not to be used for divining or consulting about past deeds

â€¢ ••Limitations of Lots••
	• Not to be used for ordinary or everyday decisions
	• Not to be used for matters that can be decided by ordinary means
	• Not to be used for matters that are not weighty or important

â€¢ ••Refutation of playing Lots••
	• The opinion of those who defend playing Lots is refuted by the fact that a Lot is naturally fit for seeking God's providence
	• It cannot be that a Lot is suitable for sacred use and yet also used for jests and games

â€¢ ••Civil Controversies••
	• The use of a Lot in civil controversies is not a valid reason to use it in light and playful matters
	• Civil controversies may be of lesser importance, but they can have significant consequences

â€¢ ••Examples of Lots in Scripture••
	• The tithes of living creatures (Lev 27:32)
	• The orders of priestly and Levitical administrations (1Chr 26:13-14; Luk 1:9)
	• These examples demonstrate that Lots can be used for important and weighty matters

â€¢ ••Nature of Lots••
	• Lots are not naturally suited for light and playful matters
	• Even if a matter is not weighty, it may still be suitable for a Lot if it is a matter of great importance

â€¢ ••Sanctity of Lots••
	• The nature of a Lot is holy, just like an Oath
	• A Lot does not need special sanctification from any institution

â€¢ ••Use of created things••
	• Christians are free to use created things for their natural purposes
	• However, mere contingency (chance) has no aptitude to determine any question

â€¢ ••Aptitude of contingency••
	• Contingency has no aptitude to determine any question on its own
	• It does not gain aptitude by the consent of those who use it for that end

â€¢ ••Sanctity in application••
	• When a Lot is applied to its use, it puts on a certain sanctity
	• This is similar to the words of an Oath and the elements used in the Sacraments

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••1. The Nature of a Lot••
• A Lot is considered extraordinary and dependent on God, not men.
• The same consent is required for a Lot as for other actions.
• A Lot adds nothing to an action.

••2. Indifference and God's Providence••
• A Lot is considered indifferent only if there is no special appeal to God's providence.
• The matter of sporting things has bounds that prevent it from being tied to communion with God.

••3. Repeating a Lot••
• Repeating a Lot will have different outcomes, but this is not a valid objection.
• God's providence does not necessarily bring with it a special operation.

••4. Objecting to God's Providence••
• It is futile to object that God cannot be drawn to exercise an extraordinary providence.
• We can still appeal to God's extraordinary providence when we want.

••5. Playing at Dice••
• Playing at Dice is repugnant to religion due to its inward nature and circumstances.
• Dice games are grounded on mere contingency.

••6. Other Games••
• Games like Table and Cards are also grounded on contingency, but differ from Dice.
• These games are subject to skill and casualty, but not to the same extent as Dice.

••7. Consequences of Playing at Dice••
• Playing at Dice tends to stir up swearing, cursing, and blasphemy.
• Players persist in a pertinacious expectation of success.

••8. Losses and Inconveniences••
• Losses and inconveniences in other games tend to become extrinsically vicious.
• In Dice, they depend partly on the nature of the game.

••9. Tempting God••
• Tempting God is opposed to Hearing the Word and Prayer.
• To tempt God is to test some Divine perfection in an unworthy manner.

••10. Consequences of Tempting God••
• Tempting God can lead to hardening one's heart and provoking God.
• It is a form of testing God's power in an unworthy manner.

Here are 15 bullets and sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••What is Tempting God?••
	• Refers to testing or challenging God's power, presence, or providence
	• Can be done intentionally or unintentionally
• ••Types of Tempting God••
	• Test of knowledge (e.g. doubting God's awareness of something)
		- Psa 94.7: "saying, the Lord does not see, nor does the God of Jacob regard"
	• Test of presence (e.g. doubting God's presence among us)
		- Exo 17.7: "They tempted God, saying, Is the Lord among us or not?"
	• Test of providence (e.g. expecting God to provide despite abandoning His means)
		- Mat 4.7.3: "Do we provoke the Lord to anger?"
• ••Forms of Tempting God••
	• Express intention to try God (e.g. casting lots)
	• Secret and implied consent (e.g. doing something that tends to test God's power)
	• Refusing to subject our wills to God's will
	• Presuming God will do something He has not promised
• ••Causes of Tempting God••
	• Doubting or unbelief (e.g. not trusting God's revealed word)
	• Despair (e.g. not expecting God's promises)
	• Contempt or base esteem of God
	• Arrogance and pride (e.g. refusing to subject our wills to God's will)
	• Presumption (e.g. being confident God will do something He has not promised)
• ••Opposition to Hearing the Word••
	• Tempting God is opposed to hearing the word, as it:
		- Cherishes divine hope
		- Fosters a love and fit esteem of God
		- Refuses to subject our wills to God's will
• ••Consequences of Tempting God••
	• Can lead to provoking God to anger
	• Can lead to murmuring and strife against God or those sent by God
	• Can lead to a lack of trust in God's revealed word
• ••Relationship to Prayer••
	• Tempting God is opposed to prayer, as prayer involves humbly presenting our will to God

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Tempting God••
	• Is opposed to acts of religion that depend on God's will
	• Involves trying to get God to depend on human will
	• Can be done through:
		- Asking for special signs or reasons
		- Refusing signs offered by God
		- Seeking signs for trivial or unnecessary things
• ••Types of Tempting God••
	• Proving or purging suspected offenses through trials (e.g. hot iron, scalding water)
	• Joining with sinners or standing in their path
	• Dangling from a string to test one's limits
• ••Consequences of Tempting God••
	• Can lead to:
		- Wearying God
		- Hurting the honor of God
		- Making oneself vulnerable to the devil's temptations
• ••Tempting God through Inducement••
	• Involves seeking help from God to commit wickedness
	• Can be referred to as tempting by trial, but with a different object (unlawful action)
	• Involves a mocking of God
• ••Tempting God as an Act of Faith••
	• Can be seen as good and commanded (Mal 3:10)
	• Involves obeying and practicing God's commands with expectation of fruit and blessing

	• Can push back the devil's temptations
	• Can make one vulnerable to the devil's temptations
• ••Tempting God and the Devil's Temptations••
	• Unlawful tempting of God can lead to being overcome by the devil's temptations
	• Lawful tempting of God can push back the devil's temptations
• ••Instituted Worship••
	• Is the means ordained by God for worship
	• Includes the second commandment: "You shall make no graven image"

Here are 11 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Will of God••
	• The will of God is to exercise and further natural worship.
	• This worship is ordained by God and declared in the Second Commandment.
• ••Meaning of Worship••
	• Worship does not depend on the nature of God or our creation, but on God's free institution.
	• Worship is a means related to natural worship, and is not an end in itself.
• ••Diversity of Worship••
	• Worship was diverse according to the diverse constitution of the Church, one before Christ and another after.
	• This diversity is due to the free institution of God.
• ••Characteristics of Worship••
	• Worship is a means and helping cause of primary worship.
	• It is an effect, instrument, and adjuvant cause of natural worship.
• ••Command of God••
	• The command of God is given and depends on primary worship.
	• It is urged by arguments from the inward and essential manner of worshipping God.
• ••Interpreting the Scriptures••
	• The rule of interpreting the Scriptures that all moral and immutable duties have moral and immutable reasons joined to them is not universally true.
	• This rule is only true if there is no special command coming between the duty and the reason.
• ••Laws of Worship••
	• No worship of this kind is lawful unless it has God as its Author and ordainer.
	• This is declared in the Second Commandment and other scriptures.
• ••Additions and Subtractions••
	• We are not to add to or take from the word of God.
	• This is declared in Deuteronomy 4:2, 12:32, and 1 Chronicles 15:13.
• ••Authorship of Worship••
	• Worship is not lawful unless it has God as its Author and ordainer.
	• This is declared in the Second Commandment and other scriptures.
• ••Judgment and Worship••
	• We are not to make for ourselves images or idols, as declared in the Second Commandment.
	• This is a warning against seeking God in our own way, rather than seeking him aright.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Idolatry and Worship••
	• Idolatry is the worship of false gods or objects
	• It can be against the First Commandment (worshipping a false god) or the Second Commandment (making or worshipping images of God)
	• Idolatry can also involve worshipping God in a way that is not pleasing to Him

â€¢ ••Images and Idols••
	• Images and idols can be used to worship God, but they can also lead to idolatry
	• The intention of the worshipper is not always the issue, as the nature of the thing itself can lead to idolatry
	• Even if the intention is to worship God, the act of worshipping an image can lead to idolatry

â€¢ ••Sacrifices and Sacraments••
	• Sacrifices, including bloody and unbloody ones, are opposed to the Sacraments
	• The Sacraments are sufficient to seal and exhibit Christ's gift, and there is no need for other types of sacrifices
	• The ordination and use of new seals or ceremonies can also be opposed to the Sacraments

â€¢ ••Prayer and Idolatry••
	• Prayer can be opposed to the use of images, which can lead to idolatry
	• Superstition of this kind is called idolatry and is against the Second Commandment
	• Idolatry can involve worshipping God in a way that is not pleasing to Him

â€¢ ••Idolatry and the Church••
	• Human forms of the Church, such as ecumenical, provincial, and diocesan churches, can be a form of idolatry
	• The hierarchy and orders of the Church can also be a form of idolatry
	• These forms of idolatry can lead to a false sense of security and a lack of dependence on God

â€¢ ••Idols and Idolatry••
	• Idols and idolatry can be found in various forms, including images, statues, and other objects
	• The worship of idols can lead to a false sense of security and a lack of dependence on God
	• Idols and idolatry can also lead to a lack of faith and trust in God

â€¢ ••Consequences of Idolatry••
	• Idolatry can lead to a lack of faith and trust in God
	• It can also lead to a false sense of security and a lack of dependence on God
	• Idolatry can also lead to a lack of spiritual growth and maturity

â€¢ ••Shunning Idolatry••
	• We must shun not only idolatry, but also the idols and idolatrous practices themselves
	• We must also shun the monuments and institutions of idolatry
	• Shunning idolatry requires a deep dependence on God and a commitment to faith and trust in Him

â€¢ ••Scriptural References••
	• The Bible warns against idolatry and the worship of false gods (Exodus 32:4-5, Psalm 106:20, Acts 7:41-1)
	• The Bible also warns against the use of images and idols in worship (Isaiah 40:18-19, Jeremiah 10:8, Habakkuk 2:18)

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• Idolatry is a serious issue that can lead to a lack of faith and trust in God
	• We must shun idolatry and the idols and idolatrous practices themselves
	• We must also commit to faith and trust in God, and depend on Him for our spiritual growth and maturity.

• ••The Sabbath and its significance••
	• The Sabbath is a day of rest, observed on the seventh day of the week
	• It is a day of respect for its first institution, which was equal in time with the creation of man
• ••Hebrews and the Sabbath••
	• The Hebrews were commanded to observe the Sabbath in the Decalogue (Exodus 20:8-11)
	• The Sabbath was a day of rest, a holy day to the Lord (Exodus 16:23, 29)
• ••The Sabbath in other cultures••
	• The observation of the seventh day was also practiced by other cultures, including the Greeks and Barbarians
	• Josephus and other ancient writers attest to the widespread practice of observing the seventh day as a day of rest
• ••Philosophers and poets••
	• Many philosophers and poets recognized the seventh day as a holy day
	• Examples include Hesiod, Linus, and others who revered the seventh day as sacred
• ••The importance of remembering the Sabbath••
	• The command to "remember" the Sabbath is a reminder of its importance and significance
	• The Sabbath is a moral and perpetual institution, commanded by God in the Decalogue
• ••The Sabbath and the Hebrews' disobedience••
	• The Hebrews were disobedient to God's command to observe the Sabbath, which led to their not entering God's rest
	• God designated a new day, saying "Today, if you will hear His voice, do not harden your hearts" (Hebrews 4:7)
• ••The Sabbath as a day of rest••
	• The Sabbath is a day of rest, a time to cease from labor and observe God's holy day
	• God gave the Hebrews bread for two days on the sixth day, so they could rest on the seventh day (Exodus 16:29)
• ••The Sabbath in Jewish tradition••
	• The Sabbath was observed in Jewish tradition, with holy days granted to children in schools on the seventh day
	• Some Jewish scholars and philosophers, such as Diogenes, were known to dispute only on the Sabbaths
• ••The Sabbath in Christian tradition••
	• The Sabbath is still observed in Christian tradition, with many churches and denominations continuing to observe the seventh day as a day of rest
	• The Sabbath is seen as a moral and perpetual institution, commanded by God in the Decalogue
• ••The significance of the Sabbath today••
	• The Sabbath remains a significant day in many cultures and traditions, a day of rest and reverence for God's holy day

â€¢ ••The Decalogue and Feast-days••

	• The Commandment of God would not command specific individuals to observe certain days

	• This would allow for flexibility and discretion in choosing specific days

	• Any ceremonial aspects of the Sabbath are added to its original institution

â€¢ ••First Commandment and Christ••
	• The authority of fathers and the firstborn of families pertain to the First Commandment

â€¢ ••Scriptural Evidence••

	• The Sabbath is a moral institution, not a ceremonial one
	• Its original institution is based on the creation story and the rest of God
	• The Commandment of God is a general principle that allows for flexibility and discretion in choosing specific days for worship.

• ••Understanding the Sabbath••

• ••The Jewish Day••
	• Begins at sunset (5 pm)
	• The Sabbath begins at sunset on Friday
• ••The Creation Account••
	• God created the world in six days, with the seventh day being a day of rest
	• The evening and morning were the first day
• ••The Lord's Day••
	• Begins from the morning of the day
	• The resurrection of Christ was early in the morning
• ••Observing the Sabbath••
	• Requires rest and the sanctification of that rest
	• Two things are necessary for the right observation of the Sabbath
• ••What Constitutes Rest••
	• A cessation from every work that might hinder divine worship
	• Includes:
		- Servile works (mechanical work, great bodily labor)
		- All works that are ours (not strictly speaking servile or mechanical)
• ••Servile Works••
	• Examples include:
		- Mechanical work
		- Plowing, digging, etc.
		- Works that require great bodily labor
• ••Forbidden Works on the Sabbath••
	• All works that are ours, even if not strictly speaking servile or mechanical
	• Includes all work that might hinder divine worship
• ••The Fourth Commandment••
	• Deals with the Sabbath day
	• Includes the prohibition of servile works and all works that are ours

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Servile Work••
	• Refers to work that is laborious or burdensome
	• Includes work that is done out of fear of punishment or hope of reward
	• Can be contrasted with liberal arts, which are done for their own sake

â€¢ ••Sabbath Rest••
	• A day of rest and solemn assembly (Lev 23:3, 32)
	• No customary work is to be done on this day (Lev 23:3, 32)
	• Includes a holy convocation and offerings made by fire to the Lord (Lev 23:25, Num 28:25)

â€¢ ••Types of Work••
	• Handiworks: work produced by hand labor (Lev 23:36)
	• Natural and civil things: work that pertains to the uses of this life (Lev 23:36)
	• Includes studying, exercising liberal arts, and handling civil causes

â€¢ ••Isaiah 58:3-6••
	• Critiques fasting that is done for selfish reasons (Isa 58:4)
	• Distinguishes between servile and liberal work (Isa 58:3)
	• Includes examples of servile work, such as striking with the fist of wickedness

â€¢ ••Interpretation of Isaiah 58••
	• Some godly divines err in interpreting Isaiah 58 as prohibiting all human words and deeds on the Sabbath
	• The chapter actually critiques work that is done for gain or is repugnant to holy exercises

â€¢ ••Exceptions to the Sabbath Rest••
	• All work that belongs to common honesty is permitted (e.g. behaving decently)
	• Work that is imposed by singular necessity is permitted (e.g. healing on the Sabbath)
	• Only obvious and unavoidable necessities are included

â€¢ ••Examples of Servile Work••
	• Going on ordinary journeys (Exo 16:4)
	• Frequenting markets or fairs (Neh 13:5)
	• Building the Tabernacle (Exo 31:13)

â€¢ ••Examples of Permitted Work••
	• Healing on the Sabbath (Mat 12:11)
	• Doing things that are imposed by singular necessity (Mat 12:11)

â€¢ ••Necessity and the Sabbath••
	• Only obvious and unavoidable necessities are permitted on the Sabbath
	• Scripture itself must allow for the necessity

â€¢ ••Common Honesty and the Sabbath••
	• All work that belongs to common honesty is permitted on the Sabbath
	• Includes behaving decently and doing things that are plainly permitted

••Religion and the Sabbath••

• ••The Sabbath is a divine institution••

• ••Pious use of any day for worship••

• ••Ceremonial vs. Correlative Precepts••
	• Dr. Ames distinguishes between ceremonial and correlative precepts (Num 28:8-10)
	• The Sabbath is a correlative precept tied to the Fourth Commandment (Exo 20:8-11)

••Memorial Days and Human Institutions••

• ••Memorial days instituted by men••
	• Not expressly forbidden, but not sanctified by divine institution (Neh 12:27, Est 9:21)
	• Examples include Christmas and Easter, which some Puritans did not celebrate
• ••Exclusion of memorial days••
	• Dr. Ames excludes memorial days instituted by men from the Sabbath (thesis 6)

••Justice and Charity••

• ••Justice as a virtue••
	• Defined as a virtue that inclines us to perform our duty to our neighbor (Eph 6:1, Col 4:1)
	• Includes all duties owed to our neighbor, including those of the Second Table
• ••Justice in a specific sense••
	• Not taken in the most general sense, which includes every duty owed to another
	• Not taken in the most particular sense, which would only include duties of like for like
• ••Object of justice••
	• Our neighbor, including everyone who is or may be a partaker with us of the same end and blessedness (Luk 10:36-37)
• ••Exclusion of holy men and angels••
	• Holy men and angels cannot be a fit object of religion or worship (FIRST TABLET)
	• Arguments from the nature of the thing exclude all adoration of creatures

	• No enmities should prevail with us to the point of not loving our enemies

	• They are therefore more to be loved
• ••Immediate Affection••
	• Our love should be immediate and present
	• We may will good to others in the future, but our love should be present
• ••Equality of Love••
	• If there is no apparent disparity, we should love equally
	• If there is a disparity, we should love those who are nearer to God and to us
• ••Special Nearness••
	• We should love those who are joined to us in some special respect
	• This is why Paul more fervently desired the conversion of the Israelites than of other nations
• ••Exercise of Love••
	• The exercise and care of our love is chiefly due to those who are near to us
	• We should take most care of those who are closest to us in rank or respect
