	• A special friend is more to be loved than an ordinary kinsman in blood

â€¢ ••Love for Parents••
	• Parents are to be loved more than any friend
	• The nearness of parents is greater than that of friends in communicating intimate things

â€¢ ••Love for Children••
	• Parents are more to be beloved than children in good things that redound from the effect to the cause
	• Children are more to be loved than parents in good things that are derived from the cause to the effect

â€¢ ••Love for Husbands and Wives••
	• Husbands and wives are to be loved more than parents or children in things that pertain to society and union
	• This is because they are one flesh (Gen 2:24, Mat 19:5)

â€¢ ••Love for Those Who Have Deserved Good••
	• Those who have deserved good from us are more to be beloved than others
	• Those who have communicated spiritual good things to us are most to be beloved

â€¢ ••Love for Community••
	• A community or whole society is more to be beloved than any member of it
	• This is because the conjunction of a part with the whole is greater than with another part

â€¢ ••Order of Priority••
	• A prince's life and safety is necessary or most profitable for the community
	• This is because the conjunction of the prince with the community is greater than with another member

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Common Good••
	• Is more important than individual interests
	• Includes praying for and working towards the good of others

â€¢ ••Prayer for the Common Good••
	• Pertains to religion and justice
	• Includes petition and thanksgiving
	• Is opposed to imprecation (cursing)

â€¢ ••Working of Good••
	• An endeavour to promote the good of others
	• Includes love in deed and good deeds
	• Is distinct from prayer, which is directed to God

â€¢ ••Types of Working of Good••
	• By moral persuasion (admonition and good example)
	• By real effecting (direct action)

â€¢ ••Moral Persuasion••
	• Includes propounding the good to be performed
	• Uses arguments to stir up others to do good

â€¢ ••Admonition••
	• Includes teaching, exhorting, comforting, and correcting others
	• Is a duty to promote the good of others

â€¢ ••Brotherly Correction••
	• Is used when evil has been committed and there is hope of good to follow
	• Must be done in a timely and fitting manner

â€¢ ••Opposition to Admonition••
	• Includes consent or communion with others in their sins

â€¢ ••Prayer and Working of Good••
	• Both are essential for promoting the common good
	• Must be done with the intention of promoting the good of others

â€¢ ••Importance of the Common Good••
	• Is more important than individual interests
	• Includes praying for and working towards the good of others

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Partaking in sin••
	• There are 9 ways to partake in another's sin: iussio, consilium, consensus, palpo, recursus, participans, nutans, non obstans, non manifestans
	• These include giving consent, counselling, defending, helping, permitting, and holding one's peace when one could speak

â€¢ ••Good example••
	• A good example is representing a good work that stirs others to perform a similar work
	• Examples include 1Tim 4:12, Tit 2:4, 7, Mat 5:16, and 1Pet 2:12

â€¢ ••Scandal••
	• A scandal is a representation of an evil work that stirs others to sin or hinders them from doing good
	• Examples include 1Cor 10:32-33, Rom 14:21, and Mat 18:6-8

â€¢ ••Types of scandal••
	• There is a scandal in every evil work that is made known to others
	• There can also be a scandal in a work that is lawful in itself, but not expedient for others

â€¢ ••Expediency••
	• An indifferent thing is said to be expedient or not expedient when all circumstances are considered
	• This means it brings glory to God and edification to one's neighbor

â€¢ ••Authority and scandal••
	• No human authority can make an action lawful if it scandalizes one's neighbor
	• A scandal can be given when a manifest sin is committed or when an action brings spiritual hurt to others

â€¢ ••Offense taken••
	• An offense taken is when an offense follows that is not from the condition of one's work, but from the pure malice of others
	• Examples include the Pharisees' offense taken against Jesus

â€¢ ••Causing offense••
	• Whoever causes one of these little ones to sin is guilty of a great offense
	• This includes causing offense through one's actions or words

â€¢ ••Woe to the offender••
	• Woe to the man by whom the offense comes
	• This includes those who cause offense through their actions or words

â€¢ ••Glory to God••
	• The ultimate goal is to bring glory to God and edification to one's neighbor
	• This means considering all circumstances and acting in a way that brings honor to God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Offending others••
	• Jesus teaches to cut off a limb that causes sin (Matthew 18:15)
	• It's better to enter life maimed than to be cast into eternal fire
	• Offending others is a serious matter

â€¢ ••Addressing offenses••
	• Go and tell your brother his fault alone (Matthew 18:15)
	• If he hears you, you've gained your brother
	• But if he doesn't, the sin of the offended remains

â€¢ ••Avoiding offense••
	• God never lays a necessity to offend on His people
	• We should avoid giving offense whenever possible

â€¢ ••Types of offense••
	• Offense given to others (e.g., sinning against them)
	• Offense taken by others (e.g., being offended by someone's words)

â€¢ ••Charity and Justice••
	• Charity and Justice are distinct, but related virtues
	• Charity rules more in some acts, while Justice shines more in others

â€¢ ••Distinguishing Justice and Charity••
	• Acts of Justice consider debt and equality
	• Acts of Charity prioritize the good of others over debt

â€¢ ••Prior obligations••
	• We are more bound to pay debts than to give charity
	• Those who offend others are more bound to seek reconciliation

â€¢ ••Types of Justice••
	• Distributive Justice: giving to each their due
	• Emendative Justice: restoring what is owed to others

â€¢ ••Distributive Justice••
	• Requires a right judgment of things and persons
	• Involves a fit comparison of things to things and persons to persons

â€¢ ••Emendative Justice••
	• Includes Commutative Justice (equality of exchange)
	• Includes Corrective Justice (restoring what is owed to others)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Corrective Justice••
	• Presupposes some injustice
	• Can be civil or criminal
• ••Civil Justice••
	• Chiefly corrects the injustice of the cause
	• Focuses on the issue at hand
• ••Criminal Justice••
	• Chiefly corrects the injustice of the person
	• Focuses on punishing the offender
• ••Revenge••
	• An act of corrective justice
	• Aims to punish the offender and deter others
	• Should aim to amend or restrain the offender
• ••Restitution••
	• An act of corrective justice
	• Aims to restore what was taken unjustly
	• Must be against justice, not charity
• ••Justice vs. Charity••
	• Justice is opposed to injury
	• Charity is opposed to ill-will or evil-will
• ••Honouring Our Neighbour••
	• Justice towards our neighbour can affect them directly or indirectly
	• Can respect the degree of their condition or the condition itself
• ••Honour••
	• A form of justice that respects the degree of our neighbour's condition
	• Commanded in the First Commandment of the second Tablet
• ••Society and Justice••
	• Human society is a foundation for all other offices of justice and charity
	• Transgressions that contribute to the disturbance of society are grievous sins
• ••Economic and Ecclesiastical Society••
	• Both are established by God
	• There is a prescribed form of economic society for all people

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Role of Politics in Society••
	• Politics is left to human liberty to preserve power
	• Humans ordain society to establish religion and justice among themselves

â€¢ ••The Importance of Economic Society••
	• Economic society is the first degree of all society
	• It is the fountain and seminary of all society
	• It is the same throughout all ages and nations

â€¢ ••The Concept of Honour••
	• Honour is an acknowledgement of another's dignity or excellence
	• It is a combination of outward observance and inward reverence

â€¢ ••The Nature of Honour••
	• Honour respects excellence or dignity
	• We are affected with reverence upon the apprehension of some excellence

â€¢ ••The Duty of Reverence••
	• Reverence is the duty due to those placed above us in eminence
	• It is a synecdoche for every duty that respects another's dignity or excellence

â€¢ ••Honour in the Bible••
	• Honour is mentioned in various biblical passages (Rom 12:10, 1Pet 3:7, 1Pet 2:17)

â€¢ ••The Importance of Honour••
	• Honour is a duty due to our neighbours
	• It is the bond and foundation of all other justice

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Honour and Justice••
	• Honour leads to a quiet and peaceable life with all piety and honesty
	• It is the reason for the promise to prolong one's days on earth

â€¢ ••Honour and the Knowledge of Others••
	• Honour respects the knowledge and opinion of others
	• It is a duty that is due to our neighbours, regardless of their position

â€¢ ••The Significance of Honour••
	• Honour is a fundamental aspect of human society
	• It is essential for the preservation of life and the establishment of justice

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Nature of Honour••
	• Honour is the external good of a man
	• It is closely related to fame, but not the same
	• Honour is about preserving a person's dignity

â€¢ ••The Office of Honouring••
	• We owe it to all people to preserve their dignity
	• This office is opposed by vices that hurt others' dignity

â€¢ ••Harming a Person's Good Name••
	• Can be done by conceiving ill of them without just cause (rash judgment)
	• Can be done by others through words, deeds, gestures, or signs
	• Can be done directly or indirectly

â€¢ ••Types of Detraction••
	• Directly exercised about the evil of a neighbour in four ways:
		- Falsely laying a fault on them
		- Discovering a secret fault without just cause
		- Harping on a true crime too much
		- Blaming their intention, not the deed
	• Indirectly exercised about the good of a neighbour in four ways:
		- Denying them good
		- Hiding their good
		- Lessening their good
		- Praising it coldly

â€¢ ••Restoring a Person's Good Name••
	• Can be done by retracting a statement
	• Can be done by desiring pardon
	• Can be done by recompensing the harm

â€¢ ••Duties of Honour••
	• Some duties belong to unequals (superiors and inferiors)
	• Some duties belong to equals

â€¢ ••Duties of Honour among Unequals••
	• Superiors should excel in well-deserving
	• Inferiors should reverence and give thanks
	• Inequality can be in age, gifts, or authority and power

â€¢ ••Duties of Honour among Superiors••
	• Those who are above others in age should set a grave example
	• Those who excel in gifts should readily impart them to others
	• Those who have power should administer justice and charity

â€¢ ••Duties of Honour among Inferiors••
	• Should reverence and give thanks to those above them
	• Should be protected and ruled by those above them

â€¢ ••Justice and Charity••
	• Justice is administered in charity by protecting and ruling
	• Protection is an application of power to defend others from evil

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Ruling and Power••
	• Ruling is an application of power to further others in good (Rom 13:4)
	• It is a ministry of God for the good of others (1 Tim 2:2)
• ••Types of Ruling••
	• Direction: propounding what is right and good (Eph 6:4)
	• Rewarding: recompensing obedience (1 Pet 2:14)
• ••Justice and Power••
	• Distributive and emendative justice shine forth when administered with fit power
	• The right of revenge belongs only to those with super-eminent power (Rom 13:4)
• ••Responsibilities of Those in Higher Power••
	• Provide for the commodities of those under their care (Eph 6:4)
	• Provide for their souls and bodies (food, raiment, and a fit dwelling)
• ••Types of Authority••
	• Private persons: husband, parents, and masters
		- Husband: moderated power with equality
		- Master: commanding power
		- Parent: mixed power
	• Public authority: ministers and magistrates
• ••Difference between Magistrates and Ministers••
	• Magistracy is an ordinance from man, while the ordinance of ministers is from God
	• Magistracy belongs to all kinds of men, while the ecclesiastical ministry is a gift of Christ
	• Magistrates have jurisdiction and power to make laws, while ministers are merely mandatory
• ••Responsibilities of Magistrates••
	• Procure the common good of all those committed to their jurisdiction
	• Use political means and coercive power to achieve this goal
• ••Responsibilities of Ministers••
	• Mandatory: they have no authority of their own, but act in Christ's stead
	• Cannot make laws or commit power to others
• ••Importance of Power and Authority••
	• Power and authority are necessary for the good of others
	• They must be exercised with justice and righteousness
• ••Proper Exercise of Power and Authority••
	• Must be exercised with a fit power and for the good of others
	• Must be exercised with justice and righteousness

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Relationship between Church and State••
	• The Church and State have different jurisdictions, but there is overlap (bullet 49)
	• The State has authority to punish evil and praise good (Rom 13:4, 1Pet 2:13-14)
• ••Minister's duties••
	• Ministers are responsible for procuring spiritual good for those committed to them (Ecclesiastical means, Act 20:28, Heb 13:17)
	• Ministers are God's ministers to the people (Rom 13:4, 1Pet 2:13)
• ••Obedience to authority••
	• Inferiors owe subjection and obedience to superiors (Heb 13:17, 1Pet 2:18, Eph 5:22)
	• Obedience should be limited to the limits of power (bullet 54)
• ••Limits of obedience••
	• Inferiors should not obey commands that go against God's command (Eph 6:1, Col 3:22)
	• Inferiors should not obey commands that go against the authority of those with greater authority (bullet 55)
• ••Blind obedience••
	• Obedience should not be blind, but rather examined to ensure it is lawful, convenient, and binding (Act 4:19)
• ••Punishment for disobedience••
	• Enduring punishment wrongfully inflicted can be considered a form of obedience (1Pet 2:19-20)
• ••Submission and thankfulness••
	• Inferiors owe submissive thankfulness to superiors for the good they communicate (bullet 58)
	• Thankfulness is a desire to recompense benefits received (bullet 59)
• ••Exemption from civil magistrate••
	• Exempting Ecclesiastical men from the jurisdiction of the civil magistrate is contrary to the perfect Law of God (bullet 50)
• ••Submission to leaders••
	• Inferiors should submit to their leaders and subordinates (Heb 13:17)
• ••Authority and power••
	• Authority and power should be exercised with joy and not with grief (Heb 13:17)
• ••Accountability••
	• Leaders are accountable for the souls of those under their care (Heb 13:17)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Thankfulness and Submission••
	• Thankfulness is a common duty of all men who have received benefits from others
	• There is a special way of thankfulness, called submission, which is shown by inferiors towards superiors
	• Examples of submission: servants to masters, wives to husbands, children to parents, and bondservants to masters
• ••Submission in Daily Life••
	• Submission is not just limited to relationships, but also in daily life
	• Examples: obeying parents, obeying masters, and being obedient to those in authority
• ••Piety and Humility••
	• Piety is a form of thankfulness that respects those who have benefited us under God
	• Humility is a virtue that involves moderating one's esteem of oneself
• ••Honour and Respect••
	• Honour and respect are due to superiors and equals
	• The beginning of honour is humility
• ••Pride and Envy••
	• Pride is an inordinate affection for one's own excellence
	• Envy is sorrow over another's good, which seems to diminish one's own excellence
• ••Types of Pride••
	• Boasting: pride in one's own good things
	• Arrogance: pride in things one would seem to have
	• Vainglory: pride in seeking fame and esteem from others
	• Ambition: pride in seeking dignities
	• Presumption: pride in undertaking matters beyond one's strength
• ••Types of Envy••
	• Envy: sorrow over another's good, which seems to diminish one's own excellence
	• Honest fear: fear of another's good because of potential evils
	• Emulation: sadness because one does not have something good
	• Indignation: sadness because of the unworthiness of someone who enjoys good
• ••Proper Measure••
	• All these affections, if they exceed proper measure, are usually noted in the Scriptures under the name of envy
• ••Relieving Needs••
	• Relieving the needs of others, whether it be substance, help, or counsel, is a form of thankfulness
• ••Scriptural References••
	• Various scriptural references are cited to support the concepts of thankfulness, submission, piety, humility, honour, and respect.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Sixth Commandment: You Shall Not Murder••
	• Respects the condition of our neighbor, absolutely considered
	• Concerns the person of our neighbor, or his outward commodities
• ••Justice and Humanity••
	• Justice respects the person of our neighbor, or his life
	• Humanity is commanded in the Sixth Commandment
	• Concerns the life of man, not the life of brute creatures
• ••Humanity as a Virtue••
	• Inclines us to preserve the life of our neighbor and its quietness
	• Performed by supplying helpful things and hindering hurtful things
• ••Types of Humanity••
	• Spiritual duty: furthering the edification of our neighbor
		- Includes prayer, good example, and admonition
	• Corporal duty: preserving the life of our neighbor
		- Includes ceasing from due offices, consenting with others in their sins, and giving offense
• ••Importance of Humanity••
	• Concerns the life of man, which is twofold (spiritual and corporal)
	• Includes duties that are spiritual and corporal
• ••Duties of Humanity••
	• Spiritual duties: include prayer, good example, and admonition
	• Corporal duties: include ceasing from due offices, consenting with others in their sins, and giving offense
• ••The Importance of Not Envy••
	• Do not envy the oppressor or choose his ways
	• Do not fret because of evildoers or be envious of the workers of iniquity
• ••The Commandment Against Murder••
	• "You shall not murder" (Exo 20:13)
	• "Surely for your lifeblood I will demand a reckoning" (Gen 9:5)
• ••The Consequences of Murder••
	• The soul of man and the blood of man are precious
	• All that duty is handled here is rightly set forth under the name of humanity
• ••The Relationship Between Humanity and Other Virtues••
	• Humanity is a virtue that implies some respect for other living creatures
	• Cruelty against brute creatures tends to declare a certain inhuman disposition

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Value of Human Life••
	• Human life is precious and sacred (Gen 9:2)
	• Shedding human blood is punishable by death (Gen 9:6)

â€¢ ••The Fear of God••
	• All creatures should fear and respect God (Gen 9:2)
	• Humans have dominion over all creatures (Gen 9:2)

â€¢ ••Spiritual Life vs. Physical Life••
	• Spiritual life is more valuable than physical life (Jas 5:20)
	• Hurting someone's spiritual life is a greater sin than hurting their physical body

â€¢ ••Degrees of Duty to Neighbors••
	• There are various degrees of duty to protect our neighbors' physical lives
	• This includes virtues like meekness, patience, and longsuffering

â€¢ ••Meekness••
	• Meekness is a virtue that moderates anger (Pro 17:17, 1Cor 13:4)
	• Meekness is opposed to slowness and wrath

â€¢ ••Slowness and Wrath••

	• Wrath is an inordinate stirring up of anger (Gen 49:7, Ecc 7:9)

â€¢ ••Patience••

	• Patience is necessary to deal with grievous wrongs

â€¢ ••Responsibility of Superiors••

â€¢ ••Consequences of Neglecting Duty••
	• Neglecting to warn the wicked can lead to their death (Eze 3:18, 13:19, 33:6, 8)
	• Neglecting to care for our neighbors' spiritual lives can lead to their spiritual death

â€¢ ••Importance of Love and Compassion••
	• We should not destroy our neighbors with our actions (Rom 14:15, 1Cor 8:11)
	• We should show love and compassion to our neighbors, especially those who are weak (Rom 14:15, 1Cor 8:11)

â€¢ ••Love and Virtue••

• ••The Nature of War••
	• A war can never be just on both sides, because there cannot be a just cause for death on both sides.
	• It is not lawful in any war to intend the occasion of the death of those who are not partakers of the cause.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hatred and Murder••
	• The Sixth Commandment prohibits murder (Exo 20:13)
	• Intending the deaths of innocent bystanders is also prohibited (Deu 19:5)
	• However, killing in self-defense or with just authority is permitted (Deu 19:5, Num 31:3)

â€¢ ••Warfare and Killing••
	• Warfare is not inherently against religion, justice, or charity (Num 31:3, 1Sam 18:16)
	• Those skilled in warfare may offer their help to lawful captains (1Chr 5:22, Luk 3:14)
	• However, no law permits killing oneself (Deu 19:5)

â€¢ ••Exposing Oneself to Danger••
	• It is lawful and just to expose oneself to certain danger of death (Jon 1:12)
	• In some cases, one may and ought to offer themselves to death (Jon 1:12)

â€¢ ••Chastity••
	• Chastity is a virtue that preserves the purity of one's person (1Thes 4:3-5)
	• Chastity has two parts: shamefastness and honesty (1Thes 4:3-5)
	• Shamefastness draws back from impurity, while honesty leads to purity (1Thes 4:3-5)

â€¢ ••Shamefastness and Honesty••
	• Shamefastness and honesty are inward choices that are revealed in outward conduct (1Thes 4:3-5)
	• Chastity is chiefly called shamefastness, as it takes away outward signs of impurity (1Thes 4:3-5)

â€¢ ••Justice and Chastity••
	• Justice respects the purity of one's neighbor, which is the essence of chastity (1Thes 4:3-5)

â€¢ ••Law Enforcement and Warfare••
	• Law enforcement and warfare are similar, as both involve taking a life (Rom 13:4, 1Pet 2:14)

â€¢ ••Police Power and Warfare••
	• The police power is a form of warfare, as it involves taking a life (Rom 13:4, 1Pet 2:14)

â€¢ ••Governors and Law Enforcement••
	• Governors are sent by God to punish evildoers and praise those who do good (1Pet 2:14)

â€¢ ••Biblical Examples••
	• The Bible provides examples of lawful killing, such as in warfare and law enforcement (Num 31:3, 1Sam 18:16)

• ••War and Military Service••

	• No one goes to war at their own expense (1Cor 9:7)

	• Modesty refers to shamefastness and gravity
	• Gravity refers to the decorum of purity
	• Modesty is a virtue that contains fleshly desire
• ••Chastity••
	• Chastity is a virtue that is observed in different states (virgin, married, widow)
	• Virgin chastity refers to keeping oneself until marriage (1Cor 7:34)
	• Conjugal chastity refers to keeping oneself in marriage (Tit 2:5)
	• Widow chastity refers to keeping oneself as a widow (1Tim 5:7)
• ••Marriage••
	• Marriage is the individual conjunction of one man and one woman
	• Marriage is a mutual communion of bodies and a society of life
	• Marriage is a means to preserve purity and chastity

	• Wives should be submissive to their husbands (1Pet 3:1-4)

	• Adultery is a sin that God will judge (Heb 13:4)

	• Man should not separate what God has joined together (Mat 19:6)

	• Marriage should not be blasphemed (Tit 2:5)
	• Wives should be discreet and chaste (Tit 2:5)

• ••Adultery and Sin••
	• The Bible warns against adultery and other forms of sin (2 Pet 2:14, Mat 5:28)

	• The Bible warns against pride, idleness, and lack of concern for the poor (Eze 16:49)
	• These behaviors are considered sinful and can lead to other forms of sin

	• The Bible discusses the concept of justice and dominion (Lev 18:23, Mat 20:15)
	• Dominion refers to the right to completely dispose of something
• ••Types of Dominion••
	• There are two parts of complete dominion: property and the use of it (Luk 20:9-10, 1Cor 9:7)
	• These can be separated, with one person owning the property and another using it
• ••Justice of Getting••
	• The justice of getting depends on the cause of the dominion (Lev 18:23)
	• A just title is a just occupying, inheritance, gift, reward, or contract
• ••Occupation and Possession••
	• A just occupation is a lawful taking of things that belonged to nobody (Lev 18:23)
	• Things that are not possessed or in anyone's dominion are said to belong to nobody
• ••Common Property••
	• In the beginning of the world, and after the flood, all things were common (Gen 1:28, Gen 9:1-3)
	• This means that all things were available for anyone to take or occupy
• ••Vacant or Forsaken Things••
	• Things that once belonged to someone but are now vacant or forsaken are still available for occupation (Lev 18:23)
	• These things are considered common and available for anyone to take or occupy

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Occupation of Things••
	• Refers to the possession of material goods
	• Includes things that are not physically present, but are still possessed by another (e.g. due to right of war or inheritance)

â€¢ ••Vacant or Forsaken Things••
	• Are not considered vacant or forsaken unless due diligence has been used to find the true owner
	• Includes things that are thrown into the sea or brought to shore by a shipwreck

â€¢ ••Captivity••
	• Refers to occupying caused by right of war, justly undertaken

â€¢ ••Inheritance••
	• Refers to succeeding to the goods of another by virtue of their just will
	• Includes buying the children of strangers who dwell among you (Lev 25:45-46)

â€¢ ••Gift••
	• Refers to a free bestowing of a good thing
	• Includes examples such as a king giving a gift to another (1Kng 10:10, 13:1)

â€¢ ••Reward••
	• Refers to the recompensing of a work done
	• Includes examples such as a reward for a job well done

â€¢ ••Contract••
	• Refers to the communicating of a good thing upon an agreement binding to it
	• Includes forms such as "I give, that you may give" or "I do, that you may do"

â€¢ ••Possession by Contract••
	• Includes buying, letting, and borrowing
	• Includes examples such as buying, letting, and borrowing goods

â€¢ ••Lawful Occupation••
	• Refers to a course of living that is common to all men
	• Includes occupations such as farming, craftsmanship, and trade

â€¢ ••Importance of Labour••
	• All are bound to exercise some occupation, unless they are in greater offices or preparing for them
	• Labour should be done for what is good, and in a way that agrees with the will of God and the profit of men.

	• A mutuum was a loan for consumption, which had to be returned in kind
	• A commodatum was a loan for use, which did not transfer ownership or possession
	• If interest was charged, the agreement was considered a hire

â€¢ ••Ministry and Vocation••

• Truth should be told in all areas of life, including public, political, and sacred testimonies
• Truth is essential for justice, fairness, and righteousness in society

Here are 10 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Testimony••
	• A testimony is a statement or evidence that serves as a witness to a fact or event
	• It can be a strong and violent presumption, equal to testimonies

â€¢ ••Importance of Truthfulness••
	• We are members of one another and should speak truth with our neighbors (Eph 4:25)
	• Honoring our parents and not stealing are also important (Exo 20:12, 15)

â€¢ ••Witnesses and Testimony••
	• A true witness delivers souls, but a deceitful witness speaks lies (Pro 14:25)
	• Two or three witnesses are required for the death penalty (Deu 17:6, 19:15)

â€¢ ••False Witnesses••
	• We can be false witnesses of God if we testify falsely about Him (1Cor 15:15)
	• John the Baptist bore witness to Jesus as the Son of God (Joh 1:32-34)

â€¢ ••Circumstantial Evidence••
	• Sufficient circumstantial evidence can be equal to testimony (res ipsa loquitur)
	• The injury speaks for itself and requires no further explanation

â€¢ ••Truth in Testimony••
	• Truth in a testimony is threefold:
		- Conforming to the thing in hand
		- Conforming to the mind of the speaker
		- Conforming to both the thing and the mind

â€¢ ••Veracity••
	• Veracity is required in those things of which we are bound or profess to have certain knowledge
	• It is either in a simple assertion or in a promise

â€¢ ••Assertion and Promise••
	• The truth of an assertion is always necessary
	• An assertion is necessary when justice or charity requires it

â€¢ ••Justice and Charity••
	• Justice requires truth in public judgments and when we are bound to bear witness
	• Charity requires truth when good comes to our neighbor without equivalent hurt

â€¢ ••Fidelity••
	• Fidelity is a virtue that requires truth in promises
	• It is a necessary aspect of keeping promises and maintaining trust

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Fidelity and Justice••
	• Fidelity is the foundation of civil justice
	• A reciprocal promise is a contract
	• Truth is essential in all agreements and contracts

â€¢ ••Definition of a Lie••
	• A lie is a testimony that declares something other than what is in one's heart
	• A lie is properly a testimony that affirms something false
	• Examples of lies include false testimony and deceitful speech

â€¢ ••Types of Lies••
	• Ironies, fables, and jests are not lies if they are not testimonies
	• Repeating false things without intending to deceive is not a lie
	• Intent to deceive is not the essence of a lie, but it can increase the mischief of a lie

â€¢ ••Intent to Speak Falsehood••
	• An intention to speak what is false is what makes a lie
	• Even if what is spoken is true, an intention to deceive can make it a lie
	• An intention to hurt can increase the mischief of a lie, but it does not make it a lie

â€¢ ••Lies and Uncertainty••
	• Assuring something uncertain as being certain is considered a lie
	• Concealing the truth when justice or charity requires it is a lie
	• Concealing the truth when neither justice nor charity requires it may not be a sin

â€¢ ••Severity of Lies••
	• Lies are more heinous when the testimony is more solemn
	• Lies in public judgments, sacred matters, and other serious contexts are particularly egregious
	• Subscriptions, testimonies, and commendatory letters given against the known truth are foul lies

â€¢ ••Dissembling••
	• Dissembling through deeds or signs is not properly a lie unless they have the force and use of speech
	• Examples of non-verbal dissembling include gestures and actions that have a certain meaning
	• Dissembling through non-verbal means is not always a lie, but it can be if it has the force of a testimony

â€¢ ••Consequences of Lies••
	• Lies can be pernicious to the author himself, even if they do not harm others
	• Lies can be a result of flattery, boasting, or a desire to please
	• Lies can be a result of delighting in confirming monstrous fables or fictions

â€¢ ••Justice and Charity••
	• Justice and charity require us to speak the truth
	• Concealing the truth when justice or charity requires it is a lie
	• Concealing the truth when neither justice nor charity requires it may not be a sin

â€¢ ••Scriptural References••
	• The Bible teaches that we should speak truth with our neighbors (Eph 4:25)
	• The Bible warns against lying and deceit (Act 5:3, Psa 12:2, 1Joh 2:4)
	• The Bible emphasizes the importance of truth and honesty in all aspects of life

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Lying and Unfaithfulness••
	• A lie is committed when there is no intention to do what is promised (Jos 8.6)
	• Unfaithfulness is committed when there is no corresponding effort to perform a promise
• ••Perjury and Sin••
	• Perjury is a sin against the reverence due to God (35)
	• It also violates justice due to our neighbor (35)
• ••Oaths and Testimony••
	• An oath is an adjunct to testimony, confirming its truth (34)
	• Perjury in an oath is a sin against God and justice (35)
• ••Asseveration and Protestation••
	• Asseveration is a manner of testimony that declares sincerity and certitude (36)
	• It is not unfitly called a protestation, as it produces witnessing by explication (36)
• ••Oaths and Asseverations••
	• An oath involves a second contesting, whereas asseveration does not (37)
	• An asseveration is a middle degree between a simple testimony and an oath (39)
• ••Contentment••
	• Contentment is a virtue that rests in the portion God has given (1Tim 6.6; Heb 13.5; Phi 4.11)
	• It is commanded in the Tenth Commandment (2)
• ••Internal Virtue••
	• Contentment is an internal virtue, intimate to primitive righteousness (3)
	• It is a condition of the second Tablet, but also pertains to the first (2)
• ••Purity and Contentment••
	• Purity is not unfitly handled in relation to contentment (3)
	• Contentment is a virtue that leads us to contemplate and seek purity (3)
• ••Common Speech••
	• We must abstain from asseverations in common speech that have some show of an oath (40)
	• Asseverations in common speech can be convenient for grave testimonies (39)
• ••Tenth Commandment••
	• The Tenth Commandment commands contentment (1)
	• Contentment is a virtue that rests in the portion God has given (1)

Here are 12 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Contentment is a virtue••
	• It is a key aspect of godliness (1 Tim 6:6)
	• It is a foundation of all charity towards our neighbor (Rom 12:15)
• ••Contentment is a duty towards our neighbor••
	• It is a duty that is most perfect and most unknown to us by nature (Rom 12:15)
	• It is the foundation of all our duties towards our neighbor
• ••Contentment is opposed to concupiscence••
	• Concupiscence is a desire for the good things of our neighbor (Heb 13:5)
	• It is a desire that is not yet accompanied by a purpose to get them by unlawful means
• ••Concupiscence is not the same as original sin••
	• Original sin is an inbred habit that perpetually dwells in us (Rom 7:5)
	• Concupiscence is a transient action that proceeds from that habit
• ••Concupiscence is a specific type of sin••
	• It is a sin that is manifestly circumscribed as respecting only our neighbor (Rom 7:7)
	• It is a sin that is condemned in the precepts of the Law
• ••Contentment is a key aspect of charity••
	• It is a peak and perfection of all charity towards our neighbor (Rom 12:15)
	• It is a foundation of all our duties towards our neighbor
• ••Contentment is commanded in the last precept••
	• It is a duty that is most perfect and most unknown to us by nature (Rom 12:15)
	• It is a duty that is commanded in the last place because it is last in coming into being in corrupted man
• ••Concupiscence is opposed to contentment••
	• Concupiscence is a desire for the good things of our neighbor (Heb 13:5)
	• It is a desire that is opposed to contentment and charity towards our neighbor
• ••Contentment is a key aspect of godliness••
	• It is a key aspect of godliness (1 Tim 6:6)
	• It is a foundation of all our duties towards our neighbor
• ••Concupiscence is a specific type of sin••
	• It is a sin that is manifestly circumscribed as respecting only our neighbor (Rom 7:7)
	• It is a sin that is condemned in the precepts of the Law
• ••Contentment is a duty that is most perfect••
	• It is a duty that is most perfect and most unknown to us by nature (Rom 12:15)
	• It is a duty that is commanded in the last place because it is last in coming into being in corrupted man
• ••Concupiscence is a desire that is not yet accompanied by a purpose to get things by unlawful means••
	• It is a desire that is not yet accompanied by a purpose to get things by unlawful means (Heb 13:5)
	• It is a desire that is opposed to contentment and charity towards our neighbor

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Concupiscence••
	• Concupiscence refers to inordinate affection, evil desire, or covetousness
	• It is a form of idolatry and a corruption of character

â€¢ ••Examples of Concupiscence••
	• Ahab's request for Naboth's vineyard (1 Kings 21:2)
	• Looking at a woman with lustful intent (Matthew 5:28)

â€¢ ••Effects of Concupiscence••
	• Renders good acts tainted and ineffective
	• Leads to moral corruption and ruin

â€¢ ••The Tenth Commandment••
	• Prohibits coveting (Exodus 20:17)
	• Includes coveting a neighbor's house, wife, or other possessions

â€¢ ••Concupiscence and the Law••
	• The law arouses sinful passions and leads to death (Romans 7:5)
	• The law is not the cause of sin, but rather reveals sin (Romans 7:7)

â€¢ ••Types of Concupiscence••
	• Coveting a house
	• Coveting a wife or other possessions

â€¢ ••The Relationship between the Ninth and Tenth Commandments••
	• Some divide the tenth commandment into two separate precepts
	• Others join or confound the ninth and tenth precepts

â€¢ ••The Foundation of Concupiscence••
	• Self-love is the root of all sins, including concupiscence (2 Timothy 3:2-4)
	• Concupiscence is a form of idolatry and a corruption of character

â€¢ ••Classification of Concupiscence••
	• John classifies concupiscence into three types:
		- Of the flesh (lust and food)
		- Of the eyes (outward delight and profit)
		- Of the pride of life (glory and pomp)

â€¢ ••The Purpose of the Law••
	• The law is not the cause of sin, but rather reveals sin and leads to a deeper understanding of God's will.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Second Commandment••
	• Prohibits making carved images or likenesses of anything in heaven, earth, or water (Exo 20:4)
	• Prohibits coveting one's neighbor's wife, house, field, servants, or animals (Deu 5:21)
• ••The Tenth Commandment••
	• Prohibits bearing false witness against one's neighbor (Exo 20:16)
• ••Characteristics of the Wicked••
	• Lovers of themselves, money, and pleasure (2Tim 3:2-4)
	• Unloving, unforgiving, and slanderers (2Tim 3:2-4)
• ••Opposition to Joy and Neighborly Love••
	• Envy and rejoicing in one's neighbor's hurt (Mat 20:15, Psa 70:2-3, Oba 1:12)
• ••The Perfection of Justice••
	• Commanded in the last precept of the second tablet (Exo 20:17)
	• Explained throughout the second tablet
• ••The Impossibility of Perfect Obedience••
	• The rule and measure of obedience is impossible in this life (Rom 7:14, Gal 5:17)
	• We act only in part and know only in part (1Cor 13:9, Rom 8:23)
• ••The Nature of Human Obedience••
	• We carry about us flesh that lusts against the Spirit (Gal 5:17)
	• We cannot obey without concupiscence (Rom 7:14)
• ••The Petition for Forgiveness••
	• We always need to have the petition in our heart and mouth, "Forgive us our debts" (Mat 6:12)
• ••The Yoke of Christ••
	• His yoke is easy and his burden is light (Mat 11:30)
	• His commandments are not grievous (1Joh 5:3)
• ••The Law Considered••
	• Observed by the faithful who delight in it (Rom 7:22, Psa 119:14, 16)
	• Considered in respect to the spirit, not the flesh (Mat 26:41)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Imperfection of Human Understanding••
	• We know in part and prophesy in part (1 Corinthians 13:9)
	• We groan within ourselves, eagerly waiting for the adoption and redemption of our body (Romans 8:23)

â€¢ ••The Conflict between Flesh and Spirit••
	• The flesh lusts against the Spirit, and the Spirit against the flesh (Galatians 5:17)
	• We do not do the things we wish because of this conflict

â€¢ ••The Imperfection of Human Endeavors••
	• We have not yet attained perfection, but press on to lay hold of what Christ has laid hold of us (Philippians 3:12)
	• We are imperfect and in need of remission of sin and imperfection

â€¢ ••The Law of God vs. the Letter of the Law••
	• The Law of God is spiritual, but the letter of the law kills (2 Corinthians 3:6)
	• The Spirit gives life, but the letter of the law brings death

â€¢ ••The Easiness of the Law of God••
	• The lightness of the Law of God is not in proportion to our strength, but in the grace of Jesus Christ and the love of God (2 Corinthians 4:17)
	• All afflictions are counted light in comparison to the reward appointed by God

â€¢ ••The Communication of the Holy Spirit••
	• The Holy Spirit is with all those who love the Law of God
	• The Holy Spirit gives life and enables us to obey the Law of God

â€¢ ••The Attributes of God••
	• God is a Spirit, having life in himself (Book 1, Chapter 4)
	• God's attributes include infinity, immensity, incomprehensibility, omnipotence, and eternity

â€¢ ••The Essence of God••
	• God's essence is absolute, whereby he can do more than he wills
	• God's essence is omnipotency, whereby he does what he wills

â€¢ ••The Efficiency of God••
	• God's efficiency is that whereby he works all in all
	• The manner of God's co-working of the persons in all external actions is distinct and appears in the order of their working

â€¢ ••The Persons of God••
	• The Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are the persons of God
	• Each person has distinct properties, such as the Father's ability to beget, the Son's ability to be begotten, and the Holy Spirit's ability to proceed from both

Here are 10 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••God's Nature and Attributes••
	• God's decree is a demonstration of his constancy, truth, and faithfulness
	• God's knowledge is omniscient, prescient, and includes science, sapience, and prudence
• ••God's Will and Intention••
	• God's will is free and effectual
	• God's intention is to bring about his glory
• ••Creation and Providence••
	• God's creation is a demonstration of his power, wisdom, and goodness
	• Providence is God's governance of the world, including the natural and moral order
• ••Man's Creation and Nature••
	• Man is created in God's image, with a soul that is immortal and wise
	• Man's excellency is in his understanding, will, and dominion over creatures
• ••Conservation and Governance••
	• God's conservation of the world is immediate and common
	• Governance is a natural inclination, with some good drawn out of it
• ••Law and Miracles••
	• God's law is a special law, consisting in teaching, fulfilling, and establishing it
	• Miracles are extraordinary, special, and a demonstration of God's power
• ••Sin and its Consequences••
	• Sin is a turning from good to evil, resulting in spiritual death and security
	• Sin's consequences include guiltiness, filthiness, and punishment
• ••Punishment and Mercy••
	• Punishment is a demonstration of God's holiness, justice, and mercy
	• Death has degrees, members, and kinds, including spiritual and corporal
• ••Restoration and Imputation••
	• Restoration is a real communication of God's righteousness
	• Imputation is a propagation of sin, with consequences including spiritual death and security
• ••The Nature of Sin••
	• Sin is the nature of the thing, including its beginning, confirmation, and propagation

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••1. Types of Death••

• ••Inward••: spiritual death, resulting from sin and separation from God
	• Loss of health and long life
	• Increase of death in the dominion over the creature
• ••Outward••: bodily death, resulting from circumstances of time and place

••2. Manner of Committing Sin••

• ••Commission••: outward, visible sin
• ••Omission••: inward, hidden sin

••3. Parts of Death••

• ••The Heart••: internal, spiritual death
	• Remainders of God's image in the understanding and conscience
• ••The Mouth••: external, visible sin
• ••The Work••: external, visible sin

••4. Object of Death••

• ••Against God••: spiritual death, resulting from sin against God
• ••Against Man••: spiritual death, resulting from sin against man
• ••Will••: inclination to good or evil

••5. Effect of Death••

• ••Reigning••: spiritual death, resulting from hardening in evil and despair of good
• ••Mortification••: bodily death, resulting from separation of soul from body

••6. Consummation of Death••

• ••Spiritual••: separation from God, resulting in hardening in evil and despair of good
• ••Bodily••: separation of soul from body, resulting in death and judgment

••7. Person of Christ••

• ••Divine••: divine nature, fitness for redemption
• ••Human••: human nature, union with God

••8. Office of Christ••

• ••Prophetical••: preaching and teaching
• ••Priestly••: sacrifice and atonement
• ••Kingly••: rule and dominion

••9. Parts of Redemption••

• ••Satisfaction••: payment for sin
• ••Merit••: reward for obedience
• ••Resurrection••: witness to Christ's power and glory

••10. Application of Redemption••

• ••New Covenant••: different from the old covenant
• ••Exaltation••: three degrees of exaltation in Christ's kingdom
• ••Sitting at the right Hand of God••: judgment and rule over men and angels

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Predestination and Reprobation••
	• Predestination: 3 acts
		- To will the glory of God
		- To appoint some to partake of it
		- To prepare and direct the means
	• Reprobation: 3 acts
		- To will the setting forth of Justice
		- To appoint some in whom it shall be manifested
		- To direct the means
• ••The Offer of Christ••
	• Outward: general offer to all
	• Inward: personal call to salvation
• ••Receiving Christ••
	• Passive: principle of grace generated
	• Active: elicited act of faith leading to repentance
• ••Application of Salvation••
	• Relative: justification
	• Proper adjunct: witness of the Spirit
	• Communion: adoption and change of state
	• Effects: Christian liberty, dignity, and use of creatures
• ••Sanctification••
	• 2 parts: mortification and vivification
	• 4 degrees: apprehension of God's love, hope, abundance of gifts, and experience of God's kindness
• ••Glorification••
	• Perfected at death or resurrection
	• Purer and more perfect in the afterlife
• ••The Church••
	• Instituted by prophets, apostles, and evangelists
	• Extraordinary ministers: prophets, apostles, and evangelists
	• Ordinary ministers: logical, grammatical, and rhetorical analysis
• ••Ministry of the Word••
	• By analysis: logical, grammatical, and rhetorical
	• By interpreting: to clarify doubtful points
	• By explaining: doctrine and clear testimonies
• ••Means of Application••
	• Sacraments: baptism and Lord's Supper
	• Discipline: less principal means
• ••Ordinary Ministry••
	• Preserving, propagating, and restoring the Church
	• 2 parts: deduction of uses and practice
	• Deduction of uses: judgement, information, and reformation
	• Practice: instruction, correction, and consolation

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••Chapter 13: Worship••

• ••Instituted Worship••
	• Worship is the means to exercise natural worship commanded by God
	• Contrary to hearing the word, observing traditions, and using images devised by men
• ••Ecclesiastical Discipline••
	• Respects the proper and immediate means of worship
	• Contrary to the sacraments, sacrifices, and ordaining new ceremonies
• ••Contrary to Worship••
	• Will-worship or superstition
	• Idolatry (using images in prayer)
	• Human forms of the Church (e.g. churches, dioceses, hierarchy)

••Chapter 14: Antecedent or Preparation in Desire••

• ••Desire••
	• Stirring up the mind
	• Meditation
	• Inward reverence (due esteem of things)
• ••Consequent••
	• Retaining the virtue of worship in the mind
	• Endeavouring to attain the end and use of worship

••Chapter 15: Rest from All Servile Labour••

• ••Rest••
	• Before Christ's resurrection: the seventh day
	• Sanctifying rest in worship (public morning and evening)
• ••Contrary to Rest••
	• Feast days ordained by men as holy
	• Transaction of business, feasts, sports, etc.

••Chapter 16: Charity (Love) Toward Our Neighbour••

• ••Love••
	• Union, complacency, goodwill
	• Praying for neighbour's good in petition and thanksgiving
• ••Contrary to Love••
	• Cursing
	• Consent in evil (counselling, defending, helping, permitting, silence)
• ••Justice Toward Our Neighbour••

• ••Distributive Justice••
	• Gives to everyone their own
	• Contrary: unjustified preference of persons
• ••Commutative Justice••
	• Equality of the thing given and received
• ••Emendative Justice••
	• Restores to every one their own
	• Corrective, supposing

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Justice••
	• Corrects injustice of the thing (Civil)
	• Corrects injustice of the person (Criminal)
	• Includes punishment (Revenge) and restitution

â€¢ ••Ill-will••
	• Formal intention (direct)
	• Virtual intention (interpretation)
	• Leads to unjust discord (Schism)

â€¢ ••Justice towards our neighbor••
	• Respects their person (Life, Chap. 18)
	• Includes humanity, purity, chastity, and goods

â€¢ ••Mediate justice••
	• Veracity (Chap. 21, Precept 9)
	• Contentment (Chap. 22, Precept 10)

â€¢ ••Weakening a good opinion••
	• Direct (formal intention)
	• Indirect (virtual from nature or circumstances)
	• Can be done through words, actions, or gestures

â€¢ ••Slander and detraction••
	• Directly about evil: falsely charging a fault, revealing a secret crime, exaggerating a true crime, or blaming intention rather than fact
	• Indirectly about good: denying, hiding, lessening, or praising coldly

â€¢ ••Honour of our neighbor••
	• Unequals: in a simple quality, in respect of age or gifts
	• Includes protection, direction, law-making, and publishing

â€¢ ••Duties of honour••
	• In authority: jurisdiction, remuneration, and emendative justice
	• Private: husbands, parents, masters, and ministers
	• Public: magistrates and those in government

â€¢ ••Neglect of duty••
	• Rending to our neighbors good
	• Consent with others' sins
	• Scandal given

â€¢ ••Humanity and meekness••
	• Humanity: preserving life, affording helpful things, and avoiding hurtful things
	• Meekness: not hurting life, being patient, long-suffering, gentle, and pardoning wrongs
	• Contrary to meekness: anger, which has three degrees (stirring up, waxing hot, and hatred)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Good of Kindred••
	• Affinity
	• Conjugal in Marriage
		- With lawful consent of ripe years
		- Of parents
		- Of contracting parties arising from deliberate counsel
	• Without compulsion
	• Deceit
	• Chastity

â€¢ ••Marriage••
	• An undivided conjunction for communion of bodies
	• Society of life
	• Whose contrary is Luxury

â€¢ ••Adjuvant causes, signs, and effects of Luxury••
	• Unchaste looks
	• Nods
	• Kisses
	• Embracings
	• Touches
	• Dances
	• Stage-plays
	• Songs
	• Gestures
	• Gluttony
	• Drunkenness
	• Lascivious attire
	• Obscene speech

â€¢ ••Kinds of Luxury••
	• Scortation
	• Adultery
	• Incest
	• Rape
	• Whoredom
	• Fornication

â€¢ ••Ownership and Good Title••
	• A power to receive the profit of that which belonged to none before
	• Of things forsaken
	• Of things lost whose owners cannot be found
	• By captivity in a just war
	• Justly obtained by a just occupation
	• Inheritance
	• Donation
	• Reward

â€¢ ••Commutative Justice••
	• Exercised about goods to which we have a good title
	• Contract which is by buying, hiring, or borrowing
	• A good title is Mutuum, when a thing is to be freely restored in the same kind
	• Accommodation, when a thing as good is to be restored, requiring a pledge

â€¢ ••Just Use of Goods••
	• Justly used in a lawful way of employment or calling
	• Whose contrary is Sloth
	• Voluntary beggary
	• Arts that are vain, curious, dishonest
	• Needless meddling with others' matters

â€¢ ••Conservation of Goods••
	• Justly conserved by Parsimony and Frugality
	• Whose contrary is Extravagance

â€¢ ••Perfection of Commutative Justice••
	• In liberality, to which pertains free giving and remission of a debt
	• Free lending
	• Hospitality
	• Whose contrary is Covetousness

â€¢ ••Theft••
	• An unjust taking away, which comprehends receiving, detaining, and damnifying
	• Of that which is another's, by ownership, power, or possession
	• Against the owner's will

â€¢ ••Testimony••
	• A true testimony is one that is conformed to the thing, mind, or both
	• In a simple assertion, which is necessary
	• When Justice requires
	• In public judgments, as by the judge, plaintiff, defendant, witness, advocate, or notary
	• Out of judgment, when we are bound to testify by some special right
	• When charity requires it, when good comes to our neighbor without our own or others' equal hurt

Here are 10 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••1. Faithfulness••
• Definition: Faithfulness is the intent to perform a task or duty
• Contrary: Unfaithfulness

••2. Oath and Perjury••
• An oath is an adjunct of a testimony
• Contrary: Perjury
• Difference from asseveration: An oath is a declaration of sincerity and knowledge, while asseveration is a protestation

••3. Envy and Joy••
• Envy is an evil eye, contrary to rejoicing in the good of a neighbor
• Virtue commanded: Contentment
• Cause: Inordinate self-love

••4. Concupiscence and Lust••
• Concupiscence is the contrary of contentment
• Types of concupiscence:
	• Lust of the flesh (about food and luxury)
	• Lust of the eye (about external pleasure and profit)
	• Pride of life (about glory and worldly pomp)

••5. Divinity••
• Divinity has two parts: Essence and Subsistence
• Essence includes Sufficiency, Faith, and Efficiency
• Subsistence includes Virtue, Observance, and Good works

••6. Faith••
• Explicit faith is resting on God's faithfulness
• External acts: Confession, Martyrdom
• Contrary: Unbelief, Doubting, Error, Heresy, Apostasy

••7. Hope••
• Hope is a principle that respects God as to come
• Foundation: Faith
• Effect: Confirmation of the soul, Joy, Patience

••8. Religion••
• Act: Worship of God in himself, love
• Degree: The highest in respect to the object, price
• Contrary: Fear, Hatred of God, Love of the World

••9. Observance••
• Types: Hearing and receiving God's word, Prayer
• Acts:
	• Prayer (Chap. 9)
	• Justice (Chap. 16)
• Adjuncts: Confession, Promise (vow)

••10. Prayer••
• Types:
	• Ejaculatory (mental, vocal, in prose or metre)
	• Solitary or with others
• Kinds: Appreciation, Deprecation, Complaint
• Extraordinary adjunct: Fasting

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Lotteries and Presumption••
	• Lotteries are a form of temptation of God
	• They involve a secret and implicit intent to test God's assistance
	• They often involve running a danger without necessity

â€¢ ••Tempting of God••
	• Tempting of God involves expecting God's assistance for wicked actions
	• It is a form of presumption and contempt of God

â€¢ ••The Church as an Heir••
	• The Church is considered an heir of God's promises
	• It is free and spiritual, having the spirit of adoption

â€¢ ••The Administration of the Covenant of Grace••
	• The administration of the covenant of grace has varied throughout history
	• It was administered in an extraordinary way from Adam to Abraham
	• It was administered in an ordinary way from Moses to Christ

â€¢ ••The Old and New Testaments••
	• The Old Testament differs from the New Testament in quality, clarity, and freedom
	• The New Testament is more spiritual and extensive in its scope

â€¢ ••Christ's Exhibitions••
	• Christ has been exhibited in different ways throughout history
	• He was exhibited in an ordinary way from Moses to Christ
	• He will be exhibited in an extraordinary way at the end of the world

â€¢ ••Calling and Election••
	• Calling is the process of election by the Church
	• Acceptance is the process of acceptance by the one elected
	• Ministers play a role in the process of calling and election

â€¢ ••Sacraments••
	• Baptism and the Lord's Supper are the two sacraments of the Church
	• They are essential for the life of the Church

â€¢ ••The End of the World••
	• The end of the world will be marked by the Resurrection of the dead and the Last Judgment
	• These events will be done in two acts

â€¢ ••Efficient and Principal Causes••
	• Efficient causes are the immediate and mediate causes of action
	• Principal causes are the final and less principal causes of action
	• The final principal cause is God's glory, and the less principal cause is our salvation
